\section{Configurations, output maps, and the base probability space}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:model}

Fix an integer $q\ge2$ and put $Q=\{0,\ldots,q-1\}$. Let $I$ be a nonempty standard Borel space. Its elements are labels for boxes. The configuration space is
\[
X=Q^I,
\]
with the product topology of the discrete spaces $Q$. There are two different structures in this definition: the standard Borel structure on the set of labels $I$, and the product topology on $Q^I$. No topology on $I$ is used to construct that product topology.

Let
\[
\cyl=\sigma\{x:x_i=v\mid i\in I,\ v\in Q\}
\]
be the cylinder $\sigma$-algebra, and let $\mu$ be its product probability, with all coordinates independent and uniform. Write $\A$ for its completion, and use $\mu$ also for the completed measure. Let $\B(X)$ denote the full product-topology Borel $\sigma$-algebra. We will prove
\begin{equation}
\cyl\subseteq\B(X)\subseteq\A.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:inclusions}
\end{equation}
For uncountable $I$, the first inclusion can be strict. The second inclusion is a completion-regularity theorem, not a definition.

For a set $E\subseteq X$, its inner and outer measures throughout this article are
\[
\mu_*(E)=\sup\{\mu(A):A\in\A,\ A\subseteq E\},\qquad
\mu^*(E)=\inf\{\mu(A):A\in\A,\ E\subseteq A\}.
\]
Using $\cyl$ instead of its completion gives the same numbers.

\begin{definition}[Blind output]
A player is represented by a total map
\[
\tau=(b,a):X\longrightarrow I\times Q.
\]
Here $b(x)$ is the chosen box and $a(x)$ the predicted color. For $i\in I$ and $v\in Q$, let $x^{i\leftarrow v}$ replace only coordinate $i$. The output is \emph{blind} if
\begin{equation}
\tau(x)=(i,c)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\tau(x^{i\leftarrow v})=(i,c)\quad(v\in Q).
\label{mbg:ext:eq:blind}
\end{equation}
It is \emph{regular} in the main theorems if it is measurable from $(X,\A)$ to the standard Borel space $I\times Q$.
\end{definition}

Every total deterministic probing strategy that ultimately predicts an unread box induces a blind map: changing that box changes none of the observations on the original run, so the run and its output remain the same. The converse is not assumed. The repository report gives finite blind maps that are not executable by legal probing trees \cite{ext:RepoReport}.

For player $p$, set
\[
E_p=\{x:x_{b_p(x)}=a_p(x)\}.
\]
These success sets are subsets of $X$; they are not initially assumed measurable. For a finite team, let $\Omega_m=\{0,1\}^m$ and define the actual success-pattern cells
\[
E_s=\{x:(\ind_{E_1}(x),\ldots,\ind_{E_m}(x))=s\}
\quad(s\in\Omega_m).
\]
They form a finite partition of $X$. The enlarged $\sigma$-algebra is
\[
\Fext=\sigma(\A,E_1,\ldots,E_m).
\]
An \emph{extension} always means a countably additive probability $\nu$ on $\Fext$ satisfying $\nu\restr\A=\mu$.

\begin{remark}[Why a random target is delicate]
For each fixed $i$, the function $x\mapsto x_i$ is measurable. This does not imply that $x\mapsto x_{b(x)}$ is measurable when $b$ has an uncountable range. An uncountable union of measurable target fibers need not belong to the cylinder $\sigma$-algebra, and even full Borel regularity of the output does not fix the issue. Section~\ref{mbg:ext:sec:examples} gives a continuous example with inner success measure zero and outer success measure one.
\end{remark}

\section{Classical support reduction, with the required exceptional-set control}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:support}

\subsection{Countable support and the Borel sandwich}

\begin{lemma}[Literal factorization]
\label{mbg:ext:lem:literal}
Every $A\in\cyl$ has the form $\pi_J^{-1}(A_J)$ for a countable $J\subseteq I$ and a Borel set $A_J\subseteq Q^J$. Every cylinder-measurable map to a standard Borel space factors measurably through a countable subproduct.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Sets of the displayed form constitute a $\sigma$-algebra: take the union of the countably many supports when forming a countable union. They contain every finite-coordinate cylinder. For maps, take preimages of a countable family of Borel sets separating points in the target. A common countable support of those preimages determines the value of the map. To see that the factor is measurable, compose with the section that places color zero outside $J$.
\end{proof}

The following is a finite-alphabet instance of classical completion regularity \cite{ext:Kakutani,ext:GK}. We include its proof because it identifies exactly which null sets later may be changed.

\begin{lemma}[Open-set sandwich]
\label{mbg:ext:lem:open}
For every open $U\subseteq Q^I$ there are a countable $J\subseteq I$ and open sets $V_J,W_J\subseteq Q^J$ such that
\[
\pi_J^{-1}(V_J)\subseteq U\subseteq\pi_J^{-1}(W_J),
\qquad \mu_J(W_J\setminus V_J)=0.
\]
The gap $W_J\setminus V_J$ is also nowhere dense.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $t$ be the supremum of the measures of countable unions of basic finite-coordinate cylinders contained in $U$. Choose such unions approaching $t$ and take their union $V$. Then $V\subseteq U$, $\mu(V)=t$, and $V=\pi_J^{-1}(V_J)$ for a countable $J$ and open $V_J$.

If $O\subseteq U$ is a basic cylinder, maximality gives $\mu(O\setminus V)=0$. Write $O$ as the conjunction of its restrictions in $J$ and in a finite set outside $J$. The latter finite pattern has strictly positive probability. Independence therefore implies
\[
\mu_J\bigl(\pi_J(O)\setminus V_J\bigr)=0.
\]
The projection $W_J=\pi_J(U)$ is open. Since $Q^J$ is second countable, it is a countable union of sets of the form $\pi_J(O)$ with $O\subseteq U$ a basic cylinder. Thus $\mu_J(W_J\setminus V_J)=0$, and the claimed sandwich follows.

Full support of $\mu_J$ also implies $W_J\subseteq\overline{V_J}$: an open portion of $W_J$ disjoint from $\overline{V_J}$ would have positive measure. Hence the gap lies in the boundary of the open set $V_J$, a closed nowhere dense set.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Completion regularity in the needed form]
\label{mbg:ext:cor:borel}
Every $B\in\B(X)$ differs from a cylinder-measurable set by a subset of a cylinder-measurable null set. The null set can also be chosen meager. In particular, \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:inclusions} holds.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Sets with this approximation property form a $\sigma$-algebra. Complements retain the same exceptional set; countable unions use the union of the exceptional sets and their supports. Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:open} supplies the property for open sets. The lift of a nowhere dense subset of $Q^J$ is nowhere dense in $Q^I$, since projection is an open continuous surjection.
\end{proof}

Consequently there is exactly one probability on $\B(X)$ with the prescribed cylinder law: every Borel sandwich forces its value. Its completion is precisely $\A$. The freedom in the subsequent classification concerns adjoining success events which can be non-Borel, not choosing different Borel product measures.

\subsection{A common invariant good set}

\begin{lemma}[Normal form for a countable family]
\label{mbg:ext:lem:normal}
Let $(f_p)$ be a finite or countable family of $\A$-measurable maps into standard Borel spaces. There exist a countable $J\subseteq I$, a Borel null set $N_J\subseteq Q^J$, and Borel maps $f_p^J$ such that, with
\[
G=X\setminus\pi_J^{-1}(N_J),
\]
the following hold:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $f_p(x)=f_p^J(x\restr J)$ for all $p$ and all $x\in G$;
\item $G$ is invariant under changing any finite set of coordinates in $I$;
\item if all $f_p$ are full product-topology Borel, $N_J$ can also be meager, so $G$ is both conull and comeager.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Embed each target Borel-isomorphically into a Borel subset of $2^\N$. Approximate the preimages of the bit-coordinate sets by cylinder-measurable sets, with cylinder-null error envelopes. A countable union produces one support $J$ and one null envelope on which all coded approximations might fail. Away from it the approximating bits are the code of the actual value, so the Borel inverse coding map gives a Borel factor. Give it an arbitrary fixed value on the exceptional set. For full Borel maps use the null-and-meager approximations of Corollary~\ref{mbg:ext:cor:borel}.

Finally saturate the exceptional set in $Q^J$ under the group of coordinate permutations with finite support. This group is countable: $J$ is countable and each coordinate has only finitely many permutations. Its transformations preserve the uniform product measure and are homeomorphisms, so the saturation is still Borel null, and still meager in the Borel case. Every change of finitely many coordinates of $Q^J$ can be effected by such a permutation at that configuration. Changes outside $J$ do not affect membership in $G$ at all. This proves (ii) without taking an uncountable union of null sets.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The factors need not be blind everywhere]
The original outputs are blind everywhere. Their arbitrary Borel definitions on $N_J$ need not be blind. All uses of blindness below take place on $G$, which is invariant under the relevant changes. Ignoring this qualification would leave a gap in the averaging argument.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Universally measurable outputs]
\label{mbg:ext:cor:universal}
If the preimage of every target Borel set under an output map is universally measurable relative to $\B(X)$, then that output is $\A$-measurable and satisfies Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:normal}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The canonical Borel product probability is one of the Borel probabilities used in the universal completion. Its completion is $\A$.
\end{proof}

Here universal measurability refers explicitly to the full Borel structure of the product topology on $X$, and to the standard Borel structure on the target. The Baire \emph{property}, which concerns category rather than measure, will be treated separately in Section~\ref{mbg:ext:sec:category}.

\section{Target atoms and measurable internal success}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:atoms}

For a regular target map $b_p$, its probability law is the ordinary pushforward $\lambda_p=(b_p)_*\mu$ on $I$. Define
\[
A_p=\{i\in I:\lambda_p(\{i\})>0\},\qquad
\alpha_p=\lambda_p(A_p).
\]
Every $A_p$ is countable: for each positive integer $n$ only finitely many points have mass at least $1/n$. On a standard Borel space these point atoms account for the atomic part of the measure; the remaining part is diffuse.

Choose $J$ from Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:normal} for the outputs and enlarge it to contain all $A_p$. Extend the old exceptional set cylindrically to the enlarged subproduct. The good set remains conull and invariant, and every output on it depends only on $x\restr J$.

We call $b_p(x)\in J$ an \emph{internal target}, and $b_p(x)\notin J$ an \emph{external target}. These terms refer only to this proof. They do not say which boxes a legal strategy has physically inspected. Because $J$ is countable and includes all point atoms,
\begin{equation}
\mu(b_p\in J)=\alpha_p.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:internal-mass}
\end{equation}
In particular, adding further countably many labels changes the internal-target event only on a null set.

\begin{lemma}[Diffuse targets escape a prescribed countable set]
\label{mbg:ext:lem:escape}
For every countable $L\subseteq I$ and a finite or countable team,
\[
\mu\{x:\exists p\ (b_p(x)\notin J\text{ and }b_p(x)\in L)\}=0.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For each $p$ and each $i\in L\setminus J$, $\mu(b_p=i)=0$. There are only countably many pairs $(p,i)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Internal fairness]
\label{mbg:ext:lem:fair}
For every regular blind player,
\[
\mu\bigl(G\cap\{b_p\in J\}\cap E_p\bigr)=\frac{\alpha_p}{q}.
\]
The set on the left belongs to $\A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $i\in J$ and $c\in Q$, consider the measurable set
\[
H_{p,i,c}=G\cap\{b_p=i,\ a_p=c\}.
\]
Blindness and invariance of $G$ make this set invariant under changing coordinate $i$. Therefore its intersections with the $q$ possible values of $x_i$ have equal measure. Equivalently, integrate the indicator over that finite coordinate in the countable subproduct. Thus
\[
\mu(H_{p,i,c}\cap\{x_i=c\})=\frac1q\mu(H_{p,i,c}).
\]
Summing over the countably many pairs $(i,c)$ and using \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:internal-mass} gives the result. All the displayed sets are countable unions of measurable sets. No integration of an external random evaluation has been used.
\end{proof}

The countable set $J$ is not canonical. The eventual extension laws are canonical: enlarging $J$ changes the feasible-pattern description only on a null set, by Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:escape}.

\section{The finite feasible-pattern object}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:feasible}

Fix a team of $m$ regular blind players, and keep the normal form above. At a configuration $x\in G$, let
\[
P_{\rm in}(x)=\{p:b_p(x)\in J\},\qquad
P_{\rm out}(x)=\{1,\ldots,m\}\setminus P_{\rm in}(x).
\]
Partition $P_{\rm out}(x)$ into groups $C$ whose members choose the same box. For each such group and each color $v$, set
\[
n_{C,v}(x)=|\{p\in C:a_p(x)=v\}|.
\]

\begin{definition}[Feasible patterns]
\label{mbg:ext:def:gamma}
For $x\in G$, a vector $s\in\Omega_m$ belongs to $\Gamma(x)$ if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item for every $p\in P_{\rm in}(x)$,
\[
s_p=\ind_{\{x_{b_p(x)}=a_p(x)\}};
\]
\item for every external target group $C$, there is a color $v_C\in Q$ such that
\[
s_p=\ind_{\{a_p(x)=v_C\}}\qquad(p\in C).
\]
\end{enumerate}
On $X\setminus G$, define $\Gamma(x)=\Omega_m$. For $s\in\Omega_m$, write
\[
D_s=\{x:s\in\Gamma(x)\}.
\]
\end{definition}

The exceptional-set convention ensures $E_s\subseteq D_s$ pointwise, and only alters null sets. The set $\Gamma(x)$ is always nonempty. It is determined by $x\restr J$ on $G$. Each $D_s$ is measurable, indeed cylinder-measurable after using the Borel normal form: only finitely many target-equality tests and color tests are needed, together with countably many possible internal coordinates. Equality of two targets is Borel because the diagonal of a standard Borel space is Borel.

The shared-target constraint is indispensable. For example, three players choosing the same external binary box and guessing $(0,0,1)$ can produce only
\begin{equation}
\Gamma(x)=\{(1,1,0),(0,0,1)\}.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:shared}
\end{equation}
Assigning each player a separately sampled fair answer would incorrectly admit impossible patterns such as $(1,0,1)$.

For $H\subseteq\Omega_m$, use the abbreviations
\[
E_H=\bigcup_{s\in H}E_s,\qquad
D_H=\{x:\Gamma(x)\cap H\ne\varnothing\}.
\]
Thus $D_H$ is the measurable event that some pattern in $H$ is feasible. The measurable event that every feasible pattern lies in $H$ is
\[
L_H=\{x:\Gamma(x)\subseteq H\}=X\setminus D_{\Omega_m\setminus H}.
\]

\section{The envelope lemma}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:envelope}

The next statement is the main bridge between the pointwise box game and ordinary measure-extension theory. It identifies all measurable obstructions to realizing a pattern.

\begin{lemma}[Exact measurable obstruction]
\label{mbg:ext:lem:envelope}
For $A\in\A$ and $s\in\Omega_m$,
\begin{equation}
A\cap E_s=\varnothing\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\mu(A\cap D_s)=0.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:envelope}
\end{equation}
More generally, $A\cap E_H=\varnothing$ implies $\mu(A\cap D_H)=0$ for every $H\subseteq\Omega_m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose $B,N\in\cyl$ with $\mu(N)=0$ and $A\mathbin\triangle B\subseteq N$. Take a common countable support $L$ for $B$ and $N$. Suppose, towards a contradiction, that $\mu(A\cap D_s)>0$.

By removing $N$, $X\setminus G$, and the null set from Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:escape}, choose
\[
x\in A\cap D_s\cap G\setminus N
\]
such that every selected external target lies outside $L$. For each external target group, choose a color witnessing feasibility of $s$ and change that box to this color. Only finitely many coordinates are changed. None belongs to $J\cup L$. Call the result $y$.

The point $y$ remains in $G$, and all output maps are unchanged, since they depend only on $J$ there. All internal success bits remain unchanged. The chosen external colors give exactly pattern $s$, so $y\in E_s$. On the other hand, $x$ and $y$ agree on $L$, so $y\notin N$ and $y\in B$. Hence $y\in A$, a contradiction.

For a set $H$ of patterns, apply the preceding conclusion to each $s\in H$ and take the finite union.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Inner and outer probabilities of every pattern event]
\label{mbg:ext:thm:envelopes}
For every $H\subseteq\Omega_m$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\quad
\mu_*(E_H)=\mu\{\Gamma\subseteq H\},\qquad
\mu^*(E_H)=\mu\{\Gamma\cap H\ne\varnothing\}.
\quad}
\label{mbg:ext:eq:innerouter}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Since the actual success pattern always belongs to $\Gamma(x)$, we have
\[
L_H\subseteq E_H\subseteq D_H.
\]
If a measurable set $B$ contains $E_H$, its complement is disjoint from $E_H$. Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:envelope} implies $\mu(D_H\setminus B)=0$, whence $\mu(B)\ge\mu(D_H)$. This proves the outer-measure formula. Apply it to the complementary event to obtain the inner-measure formula.
\end{proof}

In particular, the formula describes the size of all threshold-success events before any probability extension has been chosen. It also shows precisely when such an event is already measurable: its lower and upper expressions must be equal.

\section{Classification of all finite-team extensions}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:classification}

\begin{theorem}[Full density classification]
\label{mbg:ext:thm:density}
Every probability extension $\nu$ of $\mu$ to $\Fext$ corresponds to a family of $\A$-measurable functions $(h_s)_{s\in\Omega_m}$ satisfying, almost everywhere,
\begin{equation}
h_s\ge0,\qquad \sum_{s\in\Omega_m}h_s=1,\qquad
h_s=0\ \text{on }X\setminus D_s.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:densityconditions}
\end{equation}
Conversely, every such family defines an extension, by
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\qquad
\nu\left(\bigcup_{s\in\Omega_m}(A_s\cap E_s)\right)
=\sum_{s\in\Omega_m}\int_{A_s}h_s\,d\mu,
\qquad A_s\in\A.
\qquad}
\label{mbg:ext:eq:extension}
\end{equation}
The family is unique up to equality almost everywhere. No countable-generation hypothesis on $\A$ is needed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Necessity.} Given an extension, define finite measures on the base by
\[
\lambda_s(A)=\nu(A\cap E_s)\qquad(A\in\A).
\]
They satisfy $0\le\lambda_s\le\mu$. The Radon--Nikodym theorem gives $\lambda_s=h_s\mu$. Summing the measures gives $\sum_s\lambda_s=\mu$, so the densities sum to one almost everywhere. Since $E_s\subseteq D_s$, $\lambda_s(X\setminus D_s)=0$, yielding the support condition. Radon--Nikodym uniqueness gives uniqueness of the densities. This theorem applies to any finite measure space, including the completed uncountable product.

\emph{Description of the enlarged sets.} The sets of the form
\[
\bigcup_s(A_s\cap E_s),\quad A_s\in\A,
\]
form a $\sigma$-algebra. Complements and countable unions can be taken separately within each cell of the finite partition $(E_s)$. They contain $\A$ and the success events, so they are exactly $\Fext$.

\emph{Well-definedness.} Suppose $(A_s)$ and $(B_s)$ describe the same set. Then
\[
(A_s\mathbin\triangle B_s)\cap E_s=\varnothing
\]
for every $s$. Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:envelope} gives
\[
\mu((A_s\mathbin\triangle B_s)\cap D_s)=0.
\]
As $h_s$ vanishes outside $D_s$, the two integrals in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:extension} agree.

\emph{Countable additivity.} Let $F_n\in\Fext$ be pairwise disjoint, with
$F_n=\bigcup_s(A_{n,s}\cap E_s)$. For fixed $s$ and $n\ne k$, the base set $A_{n,s}\cap A_{k,s}$ is disjoint from $E_s$. Its $h_s\mu$ measure is therefore zero by the same envelope argument. Removing these countably many null overlaps makes the sets $A_{n,s}$ pairwise disjoint for the measure $h_s\mu$. Ordinary countable additivity on the base then gives
\[
\nu\Bigl(\bigcup_n F_n\Bigr)
=\sum_s\int_{\cup_n A_{n,s}}h_s\,d\mu
=\sum_n\sum_s\int_{A_{n,s}}h_s\,d\mu
=\sum_n\nu(F_n).
\]
Finally, the density sum gives total mass one and $\nu(A)=\mu(A)$ for $A\in\A$. This proves sufficiency.
\end{proof}

The theorem directly answers Question~5 of the inspected report. It provides the measure on every event of the enlarged $\sigma$-algebra, not only the vector $(\nu(E_p))$ or the distribution of the number of correct guesses.

\begin{remark}[Why densities live on the full base]
It would be incorrect to require every $h_s$ to depend only on the original strategy support $J$. In a one-player diffuse example, both outcomes are feasible almost everywhere. For a fixed coordinate $k$ outside that support, the choice
\[
h_1(x)=\ind_{\{x_k=0\}},\qquad h_0=1-h_1
\]
defines a valid extension, correlating success with that additional base event. Each particular finite family of densities has a countable support modulo null sets, but that support can be larger than the one used by the outputs.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[All bounded payoff ranges]
\label{mbg:ext:cor:payoff}
For any function $\psi:\Omega_m\to\R$, the set of extension expectations of $\psi$ of the actual success pattern is exactly
\begin{equation}
\left[
\int\min_{s\in\Gamma(x)}\psi(s)\,d\mu(x),\quad
\int\max_{s\in\Gamma(x)}\psi(s)\,d\mu(x)
\right].
\label{mbg:ext:eq:payoff}
\end{equation}
Both endpoints occur, and every intermediate value occurs.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
At almost every $x$, the densities in Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:density} define a probability supported on $\Gamma(x)$, so its conditional payoff is between the indicated extrema. A measurable choice of the first minimizing, or first maximizing, pattern attains the endpoint: there are finitely many patterns and their feasibility sets are measurable. Constant convex mixtures of the two density families attain the whole interval.
\end{proof}

For $\psi=\ind_H$, this also proves directly that every probability between the inner and outer values in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:innerouter} is obtained by an extension.

\begin{corollary}[Uniqueness]
\label{mbg:ext:cor:unique}
The extension is unique if and only if $\Gamma(x)$ is a singleton almost everywhere. For blind regular players with $q\ge2$, this is equivalent to every target law being purely atomic.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A singleton feasible set forces the density vector; conversely, two feasible patterns on a positive-measure set allow distinct measurable density choices. If every target is internal, the success vector is fixed. If some target is external, its nonempty group contains a player guessing a color $c$; choosing $c$ or a different color changes that player's success bit. Thus an external group creates at least two patterns. Use \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:internal-mass} and the finite union over players.
\end{proof}

\section{The exact atomic-mass law for one player}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:single}

\begin{theorem}[Atomic--diffuse formula]
\label{mbg:ext:thm:alpha}
Let $\tau=(b,a)$ be one regular blind player and let
\[
\alpha=\sum_{i:\mu(b=i)>0}\mu(b=i)
\]
be the mass of the atomic part of its target distribution. Then
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\quad
\mu_*(E)=\frac{\alpha}{q},\qquad
\mu^*(E)=\frac{\alpha}{q}+1-\alpha
=1-\frac{q-1}{q}\alpha.
\quad}
\label{mbg:ext:eq:alpha}
\end{equation}
Every value in this closed interval is the success probability in some countably additive extension. Moreover,
\begin{equation}
E\in\A\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\alpha=1.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:meascriterion}
\end{equation}
When these equivalent conditions hold, $\mu(E)=1/q$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
On the internal-target event the success bit is fixed and its success portion has measure $\alpha/q$, by Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:fair}. On the external-target event, of measure $1-\alpha$, both success and failure are feasible: set the selected color equal to or different from the guess. Consequently the lower feasibility event for success is exactly the internal success event, up to a null set; the upper feasibility event also includes every external-target point. Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:envelopes} gives \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:alpha}, and Corollary~\ref{mbg:ext:cor:payoff} gives attainability.

The interval has width $1-\alpha$. In a complete finite measure space a subset has equal inner and outer measures exactly when it is measurable: choose measurable inner and outer approximations with equal limiting measure and use completeness for the intervening null difference. This proves \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:meascriterion}.
\end{proof}

The formula is stronger than a countable-target obstruction: it measures exactly how much probability remains undetermined. A purely atomic target law can have an uncountable pointwise range on a null set, so the criterion concerns its law rather than literal global countability of the range.

\subsection{Nonuniform independent colors}

The geometric extension theorem does not require uniform probabilities. Suppose coordinate $i$ has probabilities $p_i(v)>0$ for every $v\in Q$, independently across coordinates. The same open-set proof works because every finite pattern has positive probability. Finite coordinate permutations need not preserve the measure, but they preserve null sets: on a finite set of coordinates all likelihood ratios are bounded above and below by positive finite constants. The invariant normal form and the envelope argument therefore survive unchanged.

For one blind player define $A_b=\{i:\mu(b=i)>0\}$ and
\[
\beta=\sum_{i\in A_b}\sum_{v\in Q}
p_i(v)\,\mu(b=i,a=v).
\]
The fiber $\{b=i,a=v\}$ is insensitive to coordinate $i$, so this is exactly the measure of internal success. The analogue of \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:alpha} is
\begin{equation}
\mu_*(E)=\beta,\qquad \mu^*(E)=\beta+1-\alpha.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:nonuniform}
\end{equation}
The same uniqueness criterion holds. The uniform minimax value in Section~\ref{mbg:ext:sec:minimax}, however, is a worst-case combinatorial statement obtained using an auxiliary uniform product law; it is not a claim that every nonuniform marginal success probability is $1/q$.

\section{The joint-law polytope and exact inequalities}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:polytope}

The full extension space contains arbitrary measurable density functions. Its projection to unconditional success-pattern laws is finite-dimensional and has a particularly explicit description.

For every nonempty $T\subseteq\Omega_m$, put
\[
w_T=\mu\{x:\Gamma(x)=T\},
\qquad
\Delta(T)=\{p\in\R^{\Omega_m}:p_s\ge0,\ \sum_s p_s=1,
\ p_s=0\text{ for }s\notin T\}.
\]
Only finitely many $T$ occur, and $\sum_Tw_T=1$. The set $\Delta(T)$ is the probability simplex on $T$, embedded in the common pattern-law space $\R^{\Omega_m}$.

\begin{theorem}[Complete finite law description]
\label{mbg:ext:thm:polytope}
The set $\mathcal P$ of all joint pattern laws $(\nu(E_s))_{s\in\Omega_m}$ is
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\qquad
\mathcal P=\sum_{\varnothing\ne T\subseteq\Omega_m}w_T\Delta(T).
\qquad}
\label{mbg:ext:eq:polytope}
\end{equation}
A probability vector $p$ belongs to $\mathcal P$ if and only if, for every $H\subseteq\Omega_m$,
\begin{equation}
\sum_{s\in H}p_s\le
\sum_{T:T\cap H\ne\varnothing}w_T
=\mu\{\Gamma\cap H\ne\varnothing\}.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:hall}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, every such $H$ satisfies
\[
\sum_{s\in H}p_s\ge\mu\{\Gamma\subseteq H\}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $h$ is an extension density, average it over each measurable set $\{\Gamma=T\}$ of positive measure. Its average is a probability vector supported on $T$. Thus its unconditional law has the form in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:polytope}. Conversely, assign any chosen vector from $\Delta(T)$ as a constant density on $\{\Gamma=T\}$ and use Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:density}. This proves the Minkowski-sum formula.

Necessity of \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:hall} is immediate: mass assigned to $H$ can only come from feasible sets intersecting $H$. For sufficiency, use the finite transportation network whose left nodes are the nonempty sets $T$ with supply $w_T$, whose right nodes are patterns $s$ with demand $p_s$, and whose permitted edges are $T\to s$ for $s\in T$. A flow of total mass one is exactly a decomposition in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:polytope}.

For completeness, the cut condition can be checked directly. Give source-to-$T$ edges capacity $w_T$, allowed $T$-to-$s$ edges capacity $2$, and $s$-to-sink edges capacity $p_s$. A cut of capacity below one cannot cross an edge of capacity $2$. If $H$ is the collection of pattern nodes on the sink side, then all $T$ meeting $H$ must also be on that side. The cut capacity is therefore at least
\[
\sum_{T:T\cap H\ne\varnothing}w_T
+\sum_{s\notin H}p_s\ge1
\]
by \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:hall}. The finite max-flow/min-cut theorem gives a unit flow. The lower inequalities are the upper inequalities for complementary pattern sets.
\end{proof}

This is a finite instance of the marriage-type characterization of random-set selection distributions \cite{ext:Artstein}. Our application permits randomized conditional selections, as the extension densities show. Restricting to deterministic selections on a fixed atomic probability space would in general produce a smaller set, so that restriction is not being made here.

\begin{remark}[Pattern laws and player marginals are different spaces]
The vector of individual success probabilities is the image of $p$ under
\[
p\longmapsto\left(\sum_s s_1p_s,\ldots,\sum_s s_mp_s\right).
\]
Its attainable region is consequently
\[
\sum_Tw_T\conv(T)\subseteq[0,1]^m.
\]
The summands $\conv(T)$ need not be simplices. The simplex terminology in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:polytope} applies to distributions over full patterns, not to their projection onto individual marginals.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Explicit score formulas}

Let $S(x)=\sum_{p=1}^m\ind_{E_p}(x)$, and for $x\in G$ let $S_{\rm in}(x)$ be the measurable internal score. Define
\begin{align}
\ell(x)&=S_{\rm in}(x)+\sum_{C}\min_{v\in Q}n_{C,v}(x),\label{mbg:ext:eq:low-score}\\
u(x)&=S_{\rm in}(x)+\sum_{C}\max_{v\in Q}n_{C,v}(x),\label{mbg:ext:eq:high-score}
\end{align}
where the sums run over distinct external target groups. These are respectively the smallest and largest feasible scores. Values on the null exceptional set can be defined arbitrarily. Corollary~\ref{mbg:ext:cor:payoff} yields the exact expected-score interval
\begin{equation}
\left[\E\ell,\E u\right].
\label{mbg:ext:eq:score-interval}
\end{equation}
Internal fairness makes its endpoints
\[
\frac1q\sum_{p=1}^m\alpha_p
+\E\sum_C\min_v n_{C,v}
\quad\text{and}\quad
\frac1q\sum_{p=1}^m\alpha_p
+\E\sum_C\max_v n_{C,v}.
\]
For a threshold $t$, the exact possible range of $\nu(S\ge t)$ is
\begin{equation}
\left[\mu\{\ell\ge t\},\ \mu\{u\ge t\}\right].
\label{mbg:ext:eq:threshold}
\end{equation}
These formulas account for target collisions, so they can be much sharper than bounds using only separate player marginals.

\section{Full Borel and universal minimax}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:minimax}

\begin{theorem}[A fair extension]
\label{mbg:ext:thm:fair-extension}
Every finite team of regular blind outputs admits an extension with
\[
\nu(E_p)=1/q\qquad(1\le p\le m).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At each $x\in G$, retain the internal bits and assign an independent uniform color to each distinct external target group. This induces a measurable probability vector $h_s(x)$ supported on $\Gamma(x)$. It is a finite sum over finite color assignments; all group-equality tests are measurable. Any assignment on the exceptional null set is harmless. Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:density} gives an extension.

For player $p$, its internal success contribution is $\alpha_p/q$. Conditional on the base configuration, it succeeds with probability $1/q$ whenever its target is external. Thus
\[
\nu(E_p)=\frac{\alpha_p}{q}+\frac{1-\alpha_p}{q}=\frac1q.
\]
Independence is imposed between distinct external \emph{boxes}, not between players who may choose the same box.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp guaranteed score under broader regularity]
\label{mbg:ext:thm:minimax}
For every nonempty standard Borel label space $I$, every $q\ge2$, and every $m\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\qquad
\max_{\substack{\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_m\\
\text{regular and blind}}}\ \min_{x\in Q^I}S(x)
=\floor{m/q}.
\qquad}
\label{mbg:ext:eq:minimax}
\end{equation}
The same value holds if the class is restricted to full product-topology Borel outputs or to universally measurable outputs. The lower bound is attained by legal strategies that inspect no boxes.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Under the fair extension, $S$ is a genuine measurable random variable with $\E_\nu S=m/q$. If every configuration had score at least $\floor{m/q}+1$, this expectation would be at least that larger integer. Hence some configuration has score at most $\floor{m/q}$.

For attainment, fix one box $i_0$ and distribute the $m$ constant guesses as evenly as possible among the $q$ colors. Every color is named at least $\floor{m/q}$ times, so every configuration receives at least that many correct guesses. The outputs are constant, hence Borel, universally measurable, regular, and blind. They are implemented without any observation. Corollaries~\ref{mbg:ext:cor:borel} and~\ref{mbg:ext:cor:universal} give the upper bounds in the two narrower classes.
\end{proof}

For the three-player binary continuum game, regular blind players can guarantee one correct guess and cannot guarantee two. This is the precise negative conclusion for the specified regularity classes. It is not a resolution of Glazer's unrestricted ZF question.

\begin{corollary}[Witnesses outside arbitrary prescribed null exceptions]
\label{mbg:ext:cor:robust}
Put $k=\floor{m/q}$ and $L=\{x:S(x)\le k\}$. For the fair extension,
\begin{equation}
\nu(L)\ge1-\frac{m}{q(k+1)}>0.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:robust}
\end{equation}
Consequently $\mu^*(L)$ is at least the same number. For every $N\in\A$ with $\mu(N)=0$, there is a losing witness $x\in L\setminus N$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The inequality $S\ge(k+1)\ind_{X\setminus L}$ gives \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:robust}. An extension probability never exceeds the base outer measure. Since $\nu(N)=0$, positive extension mass of $L$ also gives a point outside $N$.
\end{proof}

The null set in this corollary may be chosen after the strategies are specified. The corollary gives an existence and outer-measure statement, not an algorithm for finding a configuration or a claim that the losing event is Borel.

\subsection{Fixed-size lists}

If each player gives a set of $r$ colors, with $1\le r<q$, replace the external compatibility condition by $s_p=\ind_{\{v_C\in A_p(x)\}}$. The envelope and density proofs are identical, and the fair marginal is $r/q$. The sharp guaranteed score is therefore
\[
\floor{mr/q}.
\]
For the lower bound, let player $p$, numbered from zero, use the $r$ consecutive residues $pr,pr+1,\ldots,pr+r-1$ modulo $q$, all at one fixed box. The $mr$ consecutive residues distribute as evenly as possible over the colors. The cylinder version of this statement was already in the prior report; the conclusion here is its completed, full-Borel, and universal extension.

\section{Examples: what the extension freedom means}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:examples}

\subsection{Continuous output, maximally nonmeasurable success}

Take a countable infinite set $J_0$, let $C=Q^{J_0}$ with its compact metric product topology, and let
\[
I=J_0\sqcup C
\]
be a tagged disjoint union. Give it the standard Borel structure of that topological disjoint union. For $x\in Q^I$, set
\[
b(x)=\text{the $C$-tagged label }x\restr J_0,
\qquad a(x)=0.
\]
The output is continuous. It is blind because its chosen target is in the $C$ component, whereas it depends only on the $J_0$ coordinates. The target law is diffuse: every single sequence in $Q^{J_0}$ has product probability zero. Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:alpha} gives
\[
\mu_*(E)=0,\qquad\mu^*(E)=1.
\]
Its success is the diagonal evaluation assertion that the box labeled by the observed sequence itself contains zero. The existing repository report already contains a binary diagonal example of this type \cite{ext:RepoReport}; the purpose here is to identify it as the endpoint $\alpha=0$ of the general formula. Nonmeasurability of evaluation on an uncountable Bernoulli cube has related classical examples, such as Janson's Appendix~G \cite{ext:Janson}.

The player can be understood operationally as first revealing the entire countable set $J_0$, then choosing the box carrying the resulting label. This uses the broad probing convention in which an entire set of boxes may be observed. A model requiring termination after finitely many individual queries is a stricter notion of executability.

\subsection{Every atomic mass is realized}

Add a separate label $\star$ to the preceding label space. Choose a Borel set $A\subseteq Q^{J_0}$ of product probability $\alpha\in[0,1]$. Such sets exist because this countable product is an atomless standard probability space; explicitly, use the base-$q$ expansion of a uniform point of $[0,1]$ and a threshold. Define
\[
b(x)=
\begin{cases}
\star,&x\restr J_0\in A,\\
\text{the $C$-tagged label }x\restr J_0,&x\restr J_0\notin A,
\end{cases}
\qquad a(x)=0.
\]
It is Borel and blind. Its only possible positive-mass target atom is $\star$, of mass $\alpha$. Thus every interval in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:alpha}, including every binary interval in Figure~\ref{mbg:ext:fig:band}, occurs in a concrete example.

\subsection{Shared targets force exact correlations}

In the binary case $q=2$, let three players use the same diffuse diagonal target and the guesses $(0,0,1)$. The feasible patterns are exactly those in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:shared}. Every extension has a parameter $t\in[0,1]$ such that
\[
\nu(110)=t,\qquad\nu(001)=1-t.
\]
Its individual success probabilities are
\[
(t,t,1-t),
\]
and its expected score is $1+t$. The fair extension has $t=1/2$, while other extensions give different marginal probabilities. Regardless of the extension, every actual configuration has either one or two successes. This shared-target constraint persists even though each separate success event has inner measure zero and outer measure one.

If instead all three guesses are zero, the only patterns are $000$ and $111$. In the fair extension, successes are perfectly correlated. A statement that the players' successes are independent would therefore be false even though every marginal is $1/2$.

\subsection{Distinct diffuse targets allow every joint law}

Use the label space
\[
I=J_0\sqcup\bigl(\{1,\ldots,m\}\times Q^{J_0}\bigr)
\]
with tags, and let player $p$ choose $(p,x\restr J_0)$ and guess zero. The target labels are diffuse and always distinct. Every success pattern can be obtained by setting these $m$ boxes independently to a matching or nonmatching color, so $\Gamma(x)=\Omega_m$ almost everywhere. Hence every probability distribution on $\Omega_m$ is the joint success law of an extension. The full theorem allows, more generally, every measurable conditional distribution given the base space.

In particular, one extension makes every player succeed almost surely and another makes every player fail almost surely, while both preserve the original measure of every base-measurable event. This does not change the pointwise game or create a strategy that succeeds everywhere. Both all-success and all-failure configurations still exist. The distinction concerns what the original measurable structure determines about a nonmeasurable event. Classical saturated-partition extensions provide related constructions \cite{ext:Vidmar}; here the feasible-pattern theorem determines when that freedom is available to adaptive targets.

\subsection{What remains fixed in every extension}

Every extension preserves the joint law of every fixed finite or countable collection of box coordinates, as well as every full product-topology Borel event. It also preserves every completed base event. A varying target can evade every fixed countable coordinate set almost surely without there being one common null set on which it evades \emph{all} coordinates simultaneously. Indeed, each actual target is some coordinate. This order of quantifiers explains how diffuse random evaluation can be unconstrained while all fixed-coordinate laws remain fixed.

\section{A complete classification for countably many players}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:countable}

The finite theorem answers the repository's Q5. The following extension treats countably many players and shows that the same geometry remains complete when probability vectors are replaced by kernels.

Let $(b_p,a_p)_{p\ge1}$ be a countable family of regular outputs. For the classification in this section, blindness is not required. Choose one countable support $J$ and good set $G$ as before, including every atom of every target law. Let $\Omega_\infty=\{0,1\}^{\N_{>0}}$. Define $\Gamma(x)$ by fixing all internal success bits and requiring one consistent color for every external target group. Put $\Gamma(x)=\Omega_\infty$ on the exceptional set.

For a binary word $w$ of length $n$, let $[w]\subseteq\Omega_\infty$ be its cylinder, and let $D_w$ be the event that $w$ is a feasible prefix. These are base-measurable. The finite alphabet gives
\begin{equation}
\Gamma(x)=\bigcap_{n\ge1}
\{s:s\restr\{1,\ldots,n\}\text{ is feasible at }x\}.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:compactgamma}
\end{equation}
Each set in the intersection is clopen in $\Omega_\infty$, and $\Gamma(x)$ is nonempty and compact. To check the equality, note that for any fixed external group the colors compatible with its first $n$ members form a decreasing sequence of nonempty subsets of finite $Q$. Some color is therefore compatible with the entire group. The graph of $\Gamma$ is measurable for $\A\otimes\B(\Omega_\infty)$ by \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:compactgamma}.

Write $S_\infty(x)=(\ind_{E_p}(x))_{p\ge1}$ for the actual success sequence. A base-measurable probability kernel $K$ means that $K(x,\cdot)$ is a probability on $\Omega_\infty$ for every $x$, and $x\mapsto K(x,B)$ is $\A$-measurable for every Borel $B\subseteq\Omega_\infty$.

\begin{theorem}[Countable kernel classification]
\label{mbg:ext:thm:kernel}
All extensions of $\mu$ to $\sigma(\A,E_p:p\ge1)$ correspond, up to almost-everywhere equality of kernels, to base-measurable probability kernels satisfying
\begin{equation}
K(x,\Gamma(x))=1\quad\text{for almost every }x.
\label{mbg:ext:eq:kernel-support}
\end{equation}
The associated extension satisfies
\begin{equation}
\nu\bigl(A\cap\{S_\infty\in B\}\bigr)
=\int_A K(x,B)\,d\mu(x)
\quad(A\in\A,\ B\in\B(\Omega_\infty)).
\label{mbg:ext:eq:kernel-law}
\end{equation}
The base probability space need not be standard or countably generated.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Necessity without a disintegration assumption on the base.}
Given an extension, let $k_w$ be the Radon--Nikodym density of
\[
A\longmapsto\nu(A\cap\{S_\infty\in[w]\}).
\]
There are countably many finite words. After removing one common base-null set, all identities
\[
k_{\varnothing}=1,\qquad k_w\ge0,\qquad
k_w=k_{w0}+k_{w1}
\]
hold pointwise. They determine a unique probability on the compact binary sequence space for each remaining $x$, by the usual countable product extension theorem. Set $K(x,[w])=k_w(x)$ and give the kernel any fixed value on the common null set. The monotone-class theorem extends measurability from cylinders to all Borel sets. It also proves \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:kernel-law} for all $B$.

Apply the finite feasible-pattern necessity argument to each prefix. It gives $k_w=0$ almost everywhere outside $D_w$. Removing a further countable union of null sets makes all these conditions simultaneous. For every $n$, the kernel then gives probability one to the permitted length-$n$ prefixes. Continuity of probability for the decreasing intersection in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:compactgamma} proves \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:kernel-support}. Agreement on the countably many cylinder values gives almost-everywhere uniqueness of the entire kernel.

\emph{Sampling a proposed kernel.}
Conversely, suppose $K$ satisfies \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:kernel-support}. On an auxiliary completed product probability space, take $Y$ with law $\mu$ and an independent sequence of uniform random numbers. Recursively choose the next bit, conditional on a prefix $w$, with probabilities
\[
\frac{K(Y,[w0])}{K(Y,[w])},\qquad
\frac{K(Y,[w1])}{K(Y,[w])}
\]
when the denominator is positive, and choose a default bit otherwise. This is an explicitly measurable sequential construction. It produces a random sequence $Z$ with conditional law $K(Y,\cdot)$. In particular, $Z\in\Gamma(Y)$ almost surely. The construction uses only the measurable functions $K(\cdot,[w])$, not a regular conditional probability theorem for the base space.

\emph{Realizing the sampled sequence.}
For $Y\in G$ and $Z\in\Gamma(Y)$, use the least player index choosing each external target as its representative. For each representative, choose the least color compatible with all members of its group and their bits in $Z$. Such a color exists by feasibility. The condition for a color to work is a countable conjunction of measurable equality and bit tests, so these color choices are measurable. Replace each selected external coordinate by its chosen color, leaving every other coordinate unchanged, and call the result $T(Y,Z)$. On the exceptional set where these conditions fail, define $T(Y,Z)=Y$.

For every fixed label $i$, the coordinate $T_i$ is measurable: partition into the countably many events on which a specified player is the least representative targeting $i$, and use the measurable selected colors. This gives coordinatewise, hence cylinder, measurability; full Borel measurability of $T$ is not being assumed.

Only countably many coordinates are replaced, and every one lies outside $J$. Thus $T$ fixes $J$ identically. On the good event all outputs are unchanged, because they factor through $J$, and the actual success sequence of $T$ is exactly $Z$.

\emph{Preservation of the completed base.}
For any fixed countable $L\subseteq I$, Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:escape} shows that almost surely no selected external target lies in $L$. Consequently
\[
T(Y,Z)\restr L=Y\restr L\quad\text{almost surely}.
\]
Every cylinder-measurable set has such a support. If $A\in\A$, choose cylinder sets $B,N$ with $A\mathbin\triangle B\subseteq N$ and $\mu(N)=0$, and take a common support for both. The displayed equality proves that $\ind_A(T)=\ind_A(Y)$ almost surely. It also proves measurability of the pullback of $A$: the possible difference lies in a measurable null set of the completed auxiliary space.

The pullback of every success-coordinate event agrees almost surely with the corresponding bit event of $Z$, and is therefore measurable. The collection of all subsets of $X$ with measurable pullback under $T$ is a $\sigma$-algebra containing the base and all success events. Its pushforward probability gives an extension. Finally, for $A\in\A$ and Borel $B\subseteq\Omega_\infty$,
\[
\Prb(T\in A,\ S_\infty(T)\in B)
=\Prb(Y\in A,\ Z\in B)
=\int_A K(x,B)\,d\mu(x).
\]
This proves \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:kernel-law}. The sets $A\cap\{S_\infty\in[w]\}$ form a generating $\pi$-system, so these values uniquely determine the extension.
\end{proof}

The countable proof requires finite $Q$ in the compact-feasibility step. For an infinite alphabet, an infinite group may have every finite prefix realizable without the whole success sequence being realizable. For example, if one group lists every color once, the all-zero sequence has every finite prefix realizable but is not itself realizable. That case is a separate problem.

\subsection{Asymptotic minimax}

For blind players, independently assigning uniform colors to distinct external groups produces a kernel with all marginals $1/q$. The internal contribution is again given by Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:fair}. Hence one common extension satisfies $\nu(E_p)=1/q$ for every player of a countable team.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp lower asymptotic density]
\label{mbg:ext:thm:asymptotic}
For every countable regular blind team there is a configuration $x$ with
\[
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\sum_{p=1}^n\ind_{E_p}(x)\le\frac1q.
\]
The optimum guaranteed lower asymptotic success density is exactly $1/q$. In particular, no such team is eventually correct on every configuration.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Under the common fair extension, every success average has expectation $1/q$. Fatou's lemma gives
\[
\int\liminf_n\frac1n\sum_{p=1}^n\ind_{E_p}\,d\nu\le\frac1q.
\]
If the lower limit were strictly larger than $1/q$ at every point, its integral would be strictly larger: the sets where it exceeds $1/q$ by at least $1/k$ form a countable cover, one of positive measure. This contradiction proves existence of $x$.

For sharpness, cycle through the $q$ constant guesses at one fixed box. The success frequency tends to $1/q$ on every configuration. Eventual correctness everywhere would make every lower limiting average equal to one, so it is impossible.
\end{proof}

This conclusion is a worst-case statement. It does not preclude a strategy that performs much better on a set assigned full measure by a particular extension, or on a selected family of configurations. Such qualifications matter when comparing it with almost-sure prediction results.

\section{The Baire-property clause: a reduction, not a solution}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:category}

The word ``Baire'' can denote two different objects. The \emph{Baire $\sigma$-algebra} is generated by continuous real-valued functions; for the compact finite-alphabet cube it agrees with the cylinder $\sigma$-algebra \cite[4A3N]{ext:Fremlin4A}. The \emph{Baire property} means differing from an open set by a meager set. It is the latter, larger regularity condition that remains in the repository's Q2. A Baire-property output map here means that the inverse image of every target Borel set has the Baire property; this definition does not require a chosen topology on a standard Borel target.

\begin{proposition}[Category normal form]
\label{mbg:ext:prop:category}
Every set with the Baire property in $Q^I$ differs from a countably supported Borel set by a subset of a countably supported Borel meager set. A countable family of Baire-property output maps to standard Borel spaces therefore factors through one countable subproduct on a common comeager set, which may be made invariant under finite coordinate changes.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For an open $U$, Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:open} already gives an appropriate countably supported approximation and a meager envelope for its difference. If $F$ is closed nowhere dense, apply that lemma to the dense open set $X\setminus F$. Its lower open approximation $V$ is dense: otherwise a nonempty open part disjoint from $\overline V$ would meet $X\setminus F$ in a nonempty open subset of the nowhere dense sandwich gap. Hence $F$ is contained in the countably supported closed nowhere dense set $X\setminus V$. A general meager set is contained in a countable union of such closed nowhere dense envelopes.

Combine an open approximation with the envelope for the meager error. Encode standard Borel output spaces into $2^\N$ and treat the countably many coordinate preimages, as in Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:normal}. Saturation under the countable finite-coordinate permutation group preserves meagerness. These are standard category-support facts; see also Fremlin, 4A2E and 4A3S \cite{ext:Fremlin4A}.
\end{proof}

This proposition is insufficient for the measure-based minimax proof. The following elementary example isolates the obstruction. In $2^\N$ let
\[
B_n=\{x:x_n=x_{n+1}=\cdots=x_{2n-1}=0\}\quad(n\ge1),
\qquad H=\bigcap_{k\ge1}\bigcup_{n\ge k}B_n.
\]
Each union is open dense: extend any finite cylinder by a sufficiently late block of zeros. The Baire category theorem makes $H$ comeager. But
\[
\mu\Bigl(\bigcup_{n\ge k}B_n\Bigr)
\le\sum_{n\ge k}2^{-n}\longrightarrow0,
\]
so $H$ is null. It is invariant under finite coordinate changes, since sufficiently late blocks avoid any fixed finite set of coordinates.

Thus even an invariant comeager good set can have product measure zero. A category factorization cannot be inserted into the earlier expectation argument as though it held almost everywhere. The Baire-property minimax clause remains unanswered here. We do not infer either a positive strategy or an impossibility theorem for that class.

\section{Foundations, verification, and a route into ProveIt}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:verification}

\subsection{The exact logical scope}

All arguments are in ordinary ZFC. The spaces are sets; no proper-class probability space or class-sized family of coordinates is used. The label set is standard Borel, so its cardinality is at most the continuum. The support lemmas themselves hold for arbitrary index sets, but the output factorization relies on a countable family separating target values and on Borel coding of that target space. One cannot replace the specified target measurable structure by an arbitrary uncountable discrete one without checking these hypotheses again.

Several steps use familiar classical principles: countably many choices of supports and null envelopes; Radon--Nikodym; product extension; and the Baire category theorem in the category comparison. We have not established their exact strength over a choiceless or reverse-mathematical base. Consequently the conclusions do not answer the original question of what ZF alone proves about unrestricted strategies.

The central geometric classification has a slightly broader scope than the game corollaries. Blindness is used in Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:fair}, hence in the value $\alpha/q$ and the fair/minimax consequences. It is \emph{not} needed for Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:envelope}, Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:density}, the joint-law polytope, or Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:kernel}. Those results also classify arbitrary regular adaptive evaluations. For one such nonblind output, replace $\alpha/q$ by its actual measurable atomic success mass
\[
\theta=\mu\{b\in J,\ x_b=a\}.
\]
The interval is then $[\theta,\theta+1-\alpha]$. For example, a map choosing a fixed coordinate and simply reporting its observed value is not blind and has success probability one; the blind fair-value conclusion would be inapplicable.

Full support of finite coordinate laws matters for the Borel-completion bridge and for null-preservation under finite changes. The biased finite-color extension in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:nonuniform} already handles unequal strictly positive probabilities. Zero probabilities and nonproduct laws need a separate analysis; they should not be included by an informal limiting argument.

\subsection{Executed finite checks}

The supplied program \texttt{verify\_finite.py} uses Python's standard library, integer arithmetic, and exact rational numbers. It performs exhaustive small checks and independent finite-polytope checks. The results are recorded in \texttt{finite\_results.json}; the companion text explains the enumeration and its scope.

A blind binary output on the finite $n$-cube pairs every vertex with the neighbor obtained by changing its chosen coordinate. Blindness makes these pairs a perfect matching, and the guessed bit is an edge label. Conversely, every edge-labeled perfect matching gives a blind output. This correspondence, already discussed in the prior repository report, justifies exhaustive enumeration rather than sampling a convenient subclass.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
Coordinates $n$ & Configurations & Perfect matchings & Blind labeled maps\\
\midrule
1 & 2 & 1 & 2\\
2 & 4 & 2 & 8\\
3 & 8 & 9 & 144\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Every enumerated map succeeds on exactly half the configurations. All $8^3=512$ ordered triples on two coordinates were checked. Their worst-case scores have the distribution
\[
\#\{\text{teams with worst score }0\}=236,
\qquad
\#\{\text{teams with worst score }1\}=276.
\]
No triple guarantees two successes, and the constant guesses $(0,0,1)$ at one fixed coordinate attain the optimal guarantee one.

The collision tests verify the shared-target patterns in \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:shared}, all eight patterns for three distinct binary targets, and a ternary example in which the unused color makes all three guesses wrong. Two mixed law polytopes are also checked exactly, with patterns ordered as $00,01,10,11$:
\begin{align*}
\mathcal P_A={}&\frac12\Delta(\{00,11\})
+\frac13\Delta(\{01,10\})
+\frac16\Delta(\{00,01,10,11\}),\\
\mathcal P_B={}&\frac14\Delta(\{00\})
+\frac14\Delta(\{01,11\})
+\frac12\Delta(\{10,11\}).
\end{align*}
Their Hall descriptions have respectively eight and four vertices. Exact Gaussian elimination enumerates those vertices and verifies equality with the convex hulls of weighted simplex-vertex sums. A separate integer maximum-flow implementation checks Hall membership on every probability-vector lattice point with denominators 12 and 8, respectively. The numbers of feasible points are 142 of 455 and 15 of 165. Finally, 15 rational specializations of the atomic-mass interval are checked for $q\in\{2,3,5\}$ and five values of $\alpha$.

These checks are finite consistency evidence and reproducible examples. They do not prove completion regularity, the envelope lemma, Radon--Nikodym classification, or the countable kernel theorem. On a finite target space every target law is atomic; the formal diffuse weights in the polytope tests are not being misrepresented as diffuse finite-space distributions.

In the collection the program is shipped as \texttt{code/\allowbreak 03-\allowbreak adaptive-\allowbreak extensions-\allowbreak verify\_\allowbreak finite.py}, the companion text as \texttt{code/\allowbreak 03-\allowbreak adaptive-\allowbreak extensions-\allowbreak finite\_\allowbreak checks\_\allowbreak README.txt} and the results as \texttt{data/\allowbreak 03-\allowbreak adaptive-\allowbreak extensions-\allowbreak finite\_\allowbreak results.json}, all byte-identical to the delivery; the companion text and the delivered command (Appendix~\ref{mbg:ext:app:sources}) use the delivery names. The program writes its JSON to standard output, so it is rerun with an explicit redirection to a scratch file; the report's \texttt{README.md} gives the commands. At the write (Python 3.14.4, Windows, on a copy) it passed and reproduced the shipped results, which on Windows differ only by CR line ends. The counts above (2, 8 and 144 blind maps; 236 and 276 triples; 142 of 455 and 15 of 165 lattice points; 15 interval cases) are those of the shipped JSON. The $236/276$ split agrees with the table of Section~\ref{mbg:sec:checks}, computed independently by Part~\ref{mbg:part:one}'s checker.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{A focused formalization plan}

The inspected report already connects finite probing semantics to \repo's certificate-oriented work \cite{ext:RepoReport}. This article suggests a separate sequence of proof obligations rather than a monolithic formalization:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.}]
\item \textbf{Finite output semantics.} Define a finite output table, the precise blindness condition, the success vector, and the grouped-target feasible-pattern predicate. Prove the edge-labeled matching characterization in the binary case and verify small tables with finite reflection.
\item \textbf{Abstract finite-partition extension lemma.} For a finite partition $(E_s)$ and measurable envelopes $(D_s)$ satisfying \eqref{mbg:ext:eq:envelope}, formalize Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:density} independently of any box game. This isolates the Radon--Nikodym and well-definedness arguments from product geometry.
\item \textbf{Completed product normal forms.} Prove literal countable support, completed support, and the finite-change-invariant good-set lemma. Record explicitly whether a function is pointwise factored or only factored off a named null set.
\item \textbf{Game-specific envelope computation.} Formalize diffuse target escape and the finite coordinate replacement that proves Lemma~\ref{mbg:ext:lem:envelope}. This is the central application-specific step.
\item \textbf{Finite law polytope.} Connect the feasible-pattern predicate to a finite rational transportation problem when the weights are supplied by exact certificates. This can be verified separately from computing those weights in an infinite game.
\item \textbf{Countable kernel construction.} Build kernels from compatible Radon--Nikodym cylinder densities, then verify the countable diffuse-coordinate replacement. The common-null-set construction and the individual almost-sure preservation of every base event must be explicit.
\end{enumerate}

These are proposed development stages, not names of existing implemented modules. No Lean or Rocq files are supplied, no repository files were changed, and no proof-assistant build was performed for this article. The mathematical proof status and the finite computational status are recorded separately in Appendix~\ref{mbg:ext:app:ledger}.

\section{Further research questions and topics}
\label{mbg:ext:sec:questions}

The following are proposed directions after the results above. Except where expressly tied to a cited question, they are not claimed to be established open problems in the literature. They are ordered roughly from direct extensions of the present argument toward broader foundational questions.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{R\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.9em,itemsep=0.7em]
\item \textbf{The remaining Baire-property minimax problem.}
Does the guarantee $\floor{m/q}$ remain optimal for blind outputs having the Baire property relative to the full product topology? Proposition~\ref{mbg:ext:prop:category} reduces the outputs to countably many coordinates on an invariant comeager set. A solution still needs a category argument that replaces averaging, or a counterexample exploiting a comeager null set. This is the unresolved clause of the inspected report's Q2.

\item \textbf{Simultaneous extensions for uncountable teams.}
Which uncountable families of outputs admit one countably additive extension making every player fair? A union of uncountably many supports or target atoms need not be countable, so the current escape argument may fail. Seek necessary and sufficient conditions on the family of target maps or on the collection of their supports. This refines the prior report's Q4.

\item \textbf{Countably infinite and general standard Borel color spaces.}
For countably infinite colors, characterize the realizable infinite success fibers without relying on compactness of a finite color set. For general standard Borel colors, separate exact guesses, finite lists, and measurable acceptance sets. The finite-prefix example following Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:kernel} shows that mere prefix compatibility is insufficient in the countably infinite case.

\item \textbf{Correlated configuration laws.}
Find a condition on a nonproduct probability law under which diffuse selected coordinates can be changed while preserving all prescribed base-measurable events almost surely. The support-and-escape portion of the envelope lemma is largely measure-theoretic, whereas completion regularity and fixed-target fairness depend on the law. The correct replacement may involve conditional support rather than unconditional independence.

\item \textbf{Additional invariance requirements on the enlarged measure.}
The classification preserves every original base event. Determine which density families also permit a specified group of configuration symmetries to act measurably and preserve the extended measure. Enlarging the $\sigma$-algebra by all symmetry translates of success events may add compatibility constraints not visible in a single finite team. Distinguish finite coordinate changes from arbitrary coordinate permutations.

\item \textbf{Measurable evaluation and target topology.}
Characterize pairs of configuration and label structures for which joint evaluation is measurable. Under such hypotheses every regular output automatically has measurable success, eliminating the diffuse ambiguity. Compare restricting the allowed targets, strengthening the observable $\sigma$-algebra, and requiring a jointly measurable family of coordinate maps.

\item \textbf{Weak-choice strength and coded versions.}
Determine which choice and comprehension principles suffice for the normal form, the diffuse escape lemma, and the finite-partition extension separately. A theorem about explicitly coded Borel outputs may have a different foundational strength from one quantifying over an abstract generated $\sigma$-algebra. This is related to the report's Q3 and to Glazer's foundational program, but the present ZFC proof supplies no exact equivalence.

\item \textbf{Executable strategies and effective witnesses.}
If outputs are given by terminating finite-query algorithms, can the losing witness and feasible-pattern certificate be extracted with useful complexity bounds? For transfinite or arbitrary Borel probing rules, what extra representation of the strategy supports such extraction? Blindness alone is insufficient to answer the operational question.

\item \textbf{The complexity of the law polytope.}
The generic Hall description contains exponentially many pattern subsets, and there are already $2^m$ patterns. Exploit the partition of players into common-target groups to produce smaller extended formulations, efficient separation oracles, or exact descriptions for bounded collision-group size. Distinguish computation with known weights $w_T$ from the potentially harder task of deriving them from strategies.

\item \textbf{Observables with extension-independent laws.}
Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:envelopes} decides when a finite success-pattern event is already measurable, and Corollary~\ref{mbg:ext:cor:payoff} decides when a scalar payoff has a forced expectation. For countable teams, characterize tail events and asymptotic statistics whose entire distributions are the same under every supported kernel. Examples should distinguish a fixed expectation from a fixed law.

\item \textbf{General adaptive evaluations beyond guessing games.}
Apply the envelope theorem without blindness to randomly indexed coordinate statistics, dynamically selected data records, or independently sampled fields. Determine when the atomic/diffuse decomposition controls more general loss functions, especially where several outputs share indices. The regularity assumptions must remain explicit; the theorem does not automatically apply to arbitrary statistical data models.

\item \textbf{Formal proof and literature comparison.}
Carry out the staged formalization above, and compare the resulting general finite-partition lemma and countable kernel theorem with the precise hypotheses of existing extension and random-set selection results. Historical priority of the application-specific formulas should be assessed independently of successful formal verification.
\end{enumerate}

The nearest concrete verification milestone is the abstract finite-partition lemma together with the game envelope lemma. Those two pieces would certify the exact atomic-mass formula and the whole finite joint law classification before committing to the more extensive countable-kernel development.

\begin{writenote}[Status of the directions, 4 October 2026, batch~92]
All twelve directions stay open as printed; the write searched no literature for them. R1 is the clause of Part~\ref{mbg:part:one}'s Q2 that this Part leaves open (see the note after the questions of Section~\ref{mbg:sec:questions}); R2 refines Q4, whose countable case Theorem~\ref{mbg:thm:fresh} and, for kernels, Theorem~\ref{mbg:ext:thm:kernel} treat; R7 is related to Q3. Part~\ref{mbg:qry:part} does not bear on them: its targets are fixed and its subject is the cost of inspection.
\end{writenote}

\section{Sources, attribution, and reproducibility}
\label{mbg:ext:app:sources}

The user-specified LinkedIn profile was used to identify the intended researcher; his authored papers and dissertation ground the mathematical connection. The box-game arXiv record lists version~1 submitted November~15, 2022. We inspected its stated question through the paper and the repository's explicit discussion, and consulted the dissertation's corresponding box-game chapter \cite{GlazerBox,ext:GlazerThesis}. The present paper does not rely on any unverified recent research announcement.

The repository audit used the pinned commit stated in Section~\ref{mbg:ext:sec:setting}. In its box-game report, the two questions answered here are explicitly research directions proposed by that report. The report's intake note still listed all twelve directions as open. We read its definitions, the cylinder minimax proof, its fair-extension and target-collapse arguments, its diagonal example, and its question list. The new article therefore builds on a precise existing result rather than presenting a renamed copy of the earlier minimax theorem.

Completion regularity is credited to Kakutani and its subsequent development. Gryllakis--Koumoullis's Lemma~2.6 contains a closely related maximal countable union argument, and its Theorem~2.9 gives a broader product result \cite{ext:GK}. Fremlin supplies standard factorization and category-support references \cite{ext:Fremlin25,ext:Fremlin4A}. The general one-set interval of extension probabilities and its countable additivity belong to \L o\'s--Marczewski's theory \cite[Theorems~1, 2, 4]{LosMarczewski}; finite saturated-partition extensions are also illustrated by Vidmar \cite[Lemma~7]{ext:Vidmar}. The finite selection polytope and its inequalities are presented with Artstein's prior work in view \cite{ext:Artstein}. The specific mathematical work here is the exact envelope computation for adaptive targets, its atomic-mass consequence, and the resulting resolution of the repository's questions under the stated regularity conventions.

The source is self-contained LaTeX and uses standard TeX Live packages. Compile it with
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
latexmk -pdf -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error\newline
\hspace*{1em}adaptive\_box\_games.tex
\end{quote}
The finite checks run with Python~3.9 or later:
\begin{quote}\ttfamily
python3 verify\_finite.py > finite\_results.json
\end{quote}
No external numerical libraries, network access, or random seed are needed to reproduce those checks. The figure is drawn from its exact formulas in the LaTeX source. The delivered PDF was compiled and visually inspected. Neither the infinite theorems nor the whole article have a proof-assistant certificate, and the literature review is not a proof of historical novelty.

% ============ Part IV (batch 92, manuscripts 06 (base) and 04) ============
\clearpage
\fancyhead[R]{\small Part~IV: the cost of inspection}
\part{Two queries in expectation: sharp information thresholds and the exact inspection cost of infinite hat guessing}
\label{mbg:qry:part}

% Part IV continues the section numbers after Part III's appendices. Source 06
% is Sections 45-58 (offset 44), source 04 Sections 59-69 (offset 58);
% equations are numbered within sections, as in both manuscripts.
\setcounter{section}{44}
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\arabic{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{qry.\arabic{section}}
\makeatletter\gdef\Hy@chapapp{\Hy@chapterstring}\makeatother
\counterwithin{equation}{section}
\renewcommand{\theHequation}{\theHsection.\arabic{equation}}

\noindent{\large Source~06 (the base): \emph{Two Queries in Expectation: Sharp Information Thresholds for Infinite Hat Guessing}. Research article and reproducibility package, October 4, 2026.\par}
\smallskip
\noindent{\large Source~04:\emph{The Exact Inspection Cost of Infinite Hat Guessing: Geometric query laws, bounded-depth obstructions, and affine rigidity of optimal finite teams}. Research report prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov, 4 October 2026.\par}

\paragraph{Abstract (source 06).}
In the compulsory binary hat game, let \(D_n\) be the number of correct
guesses among the first \(n\) players minus \(n/2\). We resolve the
bounded-inspection question posed in a recent \repo\ research report.
For every strategy with a common finite bound on adaptive queries,
both events \(D_n\to+\infty\) and \(D_n\to-\infty\) are null and meager.
The probability conclusion needs no restriction on query distance or on
the number of players who inspect one hat.

We obtain an exact threshold for deterministic expected query cost:
the least uniform expected budget permitting divergent positive excess
is \(2\), whether success is required with positive probability or
almost surely. Below \(2\), a decision-tree path estimate forces uniformly
bounded depth. At \(2\), an early-stopping implementation of Eldredge's
existing block strategy has \(\E Q_i<2\) for every player,
\(\sup_i\E Q_i=2\), and \(\Prb(Q_i>t)\le2^{-t}\), while
\(D_n\to+\infty\) almost surely. Explicit schedules give exact asymptotic
equivalents, including \(D_n\sim\log\log n\).

Further results include an exact classification of eventually positive
query-count budgets, allowing arbitrarily sparse large values; a separation between count and
distance budgets; a finite-chromatic visibility obstruction allowing
infinite neighborhoods; finite sign bounds from sparse Fourier supports;
and the sharp one-query success probability \(3/4\). Private free
randomization changes the expected-cost threshold: every budget
strictly greater than \(1\) becomes sufficient.

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Contribution and verification status (source 06).}
The article contains full proofs and reproducible finite checks.
The threshold, classification, and obstruction results are developed here
as answers to the repository's follow-up questions. The block mechanism
is explicitly credited to Eldredge, and the analytic sign inequality is
classical. Historical priority is not established. These are unrefereed
mathematical results, not a claimed proof-assistant formalization or an
endorsed contribution by Elliot Glazer.
\end{resultbox}

\paragraph{Abstract (source 04).}
Let \(D_n=S_n-n/2\) be the excess number of correct guesses in the
countably infinite binary hat game with independent fair hats.
Players use deterministic adaptive decision trees, never inspect their
own hats, and make compulsory guesses. We prove that a uniform bound
on decision-tree depth forces
\(\Prb(D_n\to+\infty)=0\), resolving the uniform-query question in the
preceding \repo\ research report.

More precisely, let \(G\) have the geometric distribution
\(\Prb(G=r)=2^{-r}\), \(r\ge1\). Every strategy with positive probability
of divergent excess satisfies
\[
 \sup_i\E\phi(Q_i)\ \ge\ \E\phi(G)
\]
for every nonnegative nondecreasing cost function \(\phi\), where \(Q_i\)
is player \(i\)'s number of inspected hats. One explicitly evaluated
version of Eldredge's existing block strategy attains equality
simultaneously for all these costs, while \(D_n\to+\infty\) almost surely.
Thus the exact uniform expected-query threshold is \(2\), the optimal
tail envelope is \(2^{-r}\), and the critical exponential-moment
parameter is \(\log 2\).

We also prove a sharp variance bound in terms of total expected queries,
derive precise growth and fluctuation laws for an explicit schedule,
and classify all finite parameter pairs attaining the first-moment
bound at the minimum possible inspection depth. Their failure sets
are affine codes with replicated simplex parity-check columns; for an
odd number of players at strict majority, these are affine Hamming
codes. Complete proofs,
finite verification code,
a source audit, and a concrete formalization roadmap are provided.

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Status (source 04).} This is an AI-assisted research manuscript with
independently reviewed mathematical arguments and executable finite
checks. The positive block mechanism is due to Eldredge, and the
analytic tools are classical. The cost classification and finite
refinements are proved here; global priority and proof-assistant
certification are not asserted.
\end{resultbox}

{\small\color{muted}Keywords (source 04): infinite hat games; adaptive queries;
Boolean Fourier analysis; hypercontractivity; geometric costs;
perfect codes; formal verification.\par}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]

Neither is an edition of the other, and neither cites the other. Like Parts~\ref{mbg:part:one}--\ref{mbg:ext:part} they are AI-assisted, unrefereed and not formalized; provenance, the merge decisions and a notation table are in Sections~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:provenance}, \ref{mbg:qry:sec:merge} and~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:notation}. \textbf{Source~06's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+44$ here (Sections~45--58) and source~04's Section~$k$ is Section~$k+58$ (Sections~59--69); in both, statement or equation $k.m$ is $(k+\text{offset}).m$}, and source~04's tagged displays $(k.m\,\mathrm a)$ are $(k+58).m\,\mathrm a$. Thus source~06's Theorem~9.3 (the threshold) is Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:threshold}, and source~04's Theorems~2.1, 4.2 and~7.4 are Theorems~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:main-frontier}, \ref{mbg:insp:cost-frontier} and~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:fin-affine}. Numbers such as ``Proposition~6.6'' or ``Remark~6.8'' that cite Eldredge~\cite{hat:Eldredge} are his and unchanged; ``Research question~13.2'' of the predecessor is Research question~29.2 of Part~\ref{mbg:hat:part}. Apart from this, the editorial changes listed in Section~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:provenance} and the dated \textbf{[write]} notes, no statement, proof, number or non-claim of either manuscript was changed.
