\section{The research intersection and the results}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:introduction}

\subsection{Why this is an intersection with Glazer's work}

The question is how coordinated predictors can succeed without observing
the coordinate they must predict. Glazer's work on choiceless box games
and his dissertation place this question at the meeting point of choice
principles, regularity, and probability \cite{GlazerBox,ext:GlazerThesis}.
There is also a direct connection to the particular statistic studied here:
Eldredge introduces excess correct guesses in response to a suggestion
by Elliot Glazer and Charles Wang \cite[p.~14]{hat:Eldredge}.
Thus the choice of topic rests on mathematical sources, independently of
the accessibility of the LinkedIn profile supplied in the request.

The relevant \repo\ source is \emph{No Universal Sublinear Bound for
Measurable Hat Guessing} \cite{qry:PriorHat}. Its further-research section
asks whether bounding the number of inspected hats uniformly still
permits \(D_n\to+\infty\) almost surely, and asks that query count be
distinguished from query distance. We settle that qualitative question,
extend it to arbitrary count budgets, and find an exact expected-cost
boundary. We also connect the finite certificates to the repository's
decision-tree and arithmetic-coding work \cite{qry:RepoBoxes,qry:ListCoding}.

\subsection{What is already known}

Eldredge proves that measurable strategies can have divergent positive
excess, using finite blocks of increasing size. His Remark~6.8 discusses
growth rates. The existing \repo\ report develops much faster prescribed
sublinear growth using Hamming-code constructions
\cite{hat:Eldredge,qry:PriorHat}. Coding methods for related partial prediction
problems have older antecedents in autoreducibility
\cite{hat:EMV}. None of these facts should be confused with the
bounded-query conclusion proved below.

Our positive construction uses Eldredge's rule, with a different emphasis:
we specify the order of queries, prove its exact cost distribution,
and combine it with a lower bound for every deterministic query algorithm.
The resulting threshold theorem is an additional conclusion, not a
claim to have invented its underlying block rule.

\subsection{A map of the conclusions}

Throughout, hats are independent fair bits and every player must guess.
\emph{Deterministic} allows arbitrary fixed algorithms and fixed parameters,
but no free random seed. A query reveals the hat of one other player.

\begin{longtable}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.29\textwidth}
 >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.58\textwidth}}
\toprule
Restriction & Proven consequence\\
\midrule
\endhead
At most \(d<\infty\) adaptive queries per player &
\(D_n\to+\infty\) and \(D_n\to-\infty\) each occur on a null, meager set.\\
\addlinespace
Deterministic algorithms with \(\sup_i\E Q_i<2\) &
The same impossibility, because expected cost forces a common depth bound.\\
\addlinespace
Deterministic algorithms with \(\sup_i\E Q_i=2\) &
Almost-sure positive divergence is possible, with geometric cost tails.\\
\addlinespace
Count budget \(q(i)\ge1\) eventually &
Almost-sure divergence is possible exactly when \(q\) is unbounded.\\
\addlinespace
Count and distance budget \(h(i)\to\infty\) &
Both constraints can be met simultaneously with almost-sure divergence.\\
\addlinespace
Merely unbounded distance budget &
Insufficient in general; an explicit counterexample has a three-colorable
visibility graph.\\
\addlinespace
Finite-chromatic visibility graph &
No positive-probability divergence for measurable strategies, even with
infinite neighborhoods; no nonmeager divergence for Baire-measurable rules.\\
\addlinespace
One query per player, finite team &
The greatest possible probability of strictly positive excess, allowing
team size to vary, is exactly \(3/4\).\\
\addlinespace
Free private random bits &
Every uniform expected budget \(C>1\) permits almost-sure divergence;
the deterministic threshold \(2\) does not apply.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Scope of the source comparison}

The repository snapshot inspected for this article is
\begin{center}
\small\texttt{\qrypin}.
\end{center}
The earlier hat manuscript is inside
\nolinkurl{docs/incoming/hat_guessing_research.zip}.
The companion box-games manuscript is at
\nolinkurl{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/ordinals-and-order-types/measurable-box-games/article.tex}.
We compared the directly relevant manuscripts and the cited literature.
We do not infer worldwide novelty from the absence of a matching search
result. In particular, the unrestricted choiceless question in
Glazer's paper is a different problem and is not resolved here.

\subsection{How the two sources are merged}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:merge}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
The two manuscripts prove the same central answer by the same construction: a common bound on query depth excludes divergence; a reachable path of length $L$ costs at least $2-2^{1-L}$ expected queries; Eldredge's block, evaluated partner first with early stopping, has a truncated geometric query law; the same schedules give the same rates; the exact uniform expected-query threshold is~$2$. Beyond that core they are complementary. Source~06 is the base because on the shared spine it has the weaker hypotheses and the stronger conclusion (any visibility graph with finitely many colours, Baire-measurable rules, divergence events null \emph{and} meagre) and because it answers all three clauses of Research question~29.2. Source~04's proofs use deterministic decision trees, the hypothesis of source~06's threshold theorem as well, so nothing printed from source~04 depends on a stronger hypothesis than the statement it is placed beside. To keep both numberings, source~06 is printed first and whole (Sections~45--58), then source~04 (Sections~59--69). The shared results:

\begin{center}\small

\section{The game, its algorithms, and three notions of cost}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:model}

Let
\[
 \Omega=\{-1,1\}^{\N}
\]
with the fair product probability measure and product topology.
Write \(X_i(x)=x_i\). A legal measurable prediction is
\(f_i:\Omega\to\{-1,1\}\) independent of coordinate \(i\):
changing only \(x_i\) does not change \(f_i(x)\).
Put
\begin{equation}
 Y_i=X_if_i,\qquad
 S_n=\sum_{i=1}^n\ind_{\{Y_i=1\}},\qquad
 F_n=\sum_{i=1}^nY_i=2S_n-n=2D_n.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:score}
\end{equation}
The independence of \(X_i\) from the information available to player \(i\)
gives
\begin{equation}
 \E Y_i=0,\qquad \E F_n=\E D_n=0.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:fairness}
\end{equation}
The variables \(Y_i\) need not be independent. Indeed, all the interesting
constructions exploit their dependence.

\begin{definition}[A deterministic query algorithm]
For each player \(i\), a binary decision tree specifies which other
coordinate to query next, as a function of the answers already received.
Leaves specify the prediction. No node queries \(i\). The number of
queries on input \(x\) is \(Q_i(x)\).
\end{definition}

A tree of depth \(d\) can query different coordinates on different
branches, so at most \(d\) queries is more general than dependence
on a predetermined set of \(d\) hats. Nevertheless it involves at most
\(2^d-1\) possible queried coordinates. Its prediction is continuous.
For cost lower bounds we may also allow algorithms that halt almost
surely; the lower bound itself will force everywhere bounded query depth
when the expected budget is below \(2\).

There are three separate resources:
\begin{enumerate}
\item A \emph{count budget} \(q(i)\) requires \(Q_i(x)\le q(i)\) on every
input.
\item A \emph{distance budget} \(h(i)\) permits player \(i\) to query
coordinate \(j\) only when \(|j-i|\le h(i)\).
\item An \emph{expected budget} controls \(\E Q_i\), with expectation over
the hat assignment, and also over independent coins if such coins are
explicitly allowed.
\end{enumerate}
Distance \(h(i)\) allows up to \(2h(i)\) possible hats; it is not itself a
query-count bound of \(h(i)\). Our simultaneous positive constructions
will satisfy both stronger inequalities separately.

We write
\[
 A_+=\{x:F_n(x)\to+\infty\},\qquad
 A_-=\{x:F_n(x)\to-\infty\}.
\]
These are measurable when the predictions are measurable. They have the
Baire property when the predictions are Baire measurable, and they are
Borel when all predictions are continuous.

\begin{remark}[Two tempting invalid shortcuts]
Equation~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:fairness} does not prevent \(F_n\to+\infty\) almost
surely. An unbounded negative contribution on rarer and rarer events can
maintain zero expectation. Also, changing one hat may affect infinitely
many predictions. We cannot simply declare \(A_+\) a tail event and
apply a zero-one law. The conditioning arguments below avoid that
assumption.
\end{remark}

\section{From conditional obstructions to probability zero}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:conditional}

We first isolate the step that upgrades a finite probability bound to an
almost-sure obstruction.

\begin{lemma}[Cylinder density]
\label{mbg:qry:lem:density}
Let \(A\subseteq\Omega\) be measurable. Suppose some \(c>0\) satisfies
\[
 \Prb(A\mid C)\le1-c
\]
for every nonempty cylinder fixing finitely many initial coordinates.
Then \(\Prb(A)=0\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Finite unions of initial-coordinate cylinders approximate every measurable
set in probability. One proof is to observe that the class of sets
approximable this way is a sigma-algebra: complements preserve
approximation; for a countable union, first truncate the union in measure,
then approximate its finitely many members. This class contains all
cylinders and hence the product sigma-algebra. Completion adds only null
modifications, which do not affect the argument.

The assumed inequality holds also for a finite union \(B\) of cylinders
of a common length, by adding over its disjoint atoms. Thus
\[
 \Prb(B\setminus A)\ge c\Prb(B).
\]
If \(\Prb(A)>0\), choose such a \(B\) with
\(\Prb(A\mathbin{\triangle}B)<\delta\), where
\(\delta<c\Prb(A)/(1+c)\). Then
\[
 \delta>\Prb(B\setminus A)\ge c\Prb(B)
 \ge c(\Prb(A)-\delta),
\]
a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Conditional sign criterion]
\label{mbg:qry:lem:conditional-sign}
Suppose \(Y_i\) are bounded by \(1\) in absolute value. For every
cylinder \(C\) fixing coordinates \(1,\ldots,m\), suppose a constant
\(c>0\), independent of \(m,C,n\), satisfies
\[
 \Prb\left(\sum_{i=m+1}^nY_i\le0\ \middle|\ C\right)\ge c
 \qquad(n>m).
\]
Then \(\Prb(A_+)=0\). The corresponding nonnegative inequality implies
\(\Prb(A_-)=0\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
On each of the displayed events \(F_n\le m\). Under the conditional
probability on \(C\), the probability that these events occur infinitely
often is at least \(c\): the probability of their union over \(n\ge N\)
is at least \(c\), and then one lets \(N\to\infty\).
Consequently \(\Prb(A_+\mid C)\le1-c\).
Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:density} finishes the proof. Apply the same argument to
\(-Y_i\) for the other direction.
\end{proof}

Conditioning works particularly well for blind prediction. After fixing
the first \(m\) hats, each \(Y_i\) with \(i>m\) still has conditional mean
zero: its owner hat is still an independent fair bit. The first \(m\)
scores may lose this property, but their total is bounded by \(m\).
This is why we delete owners, rather than trying to delete every
prediction affected by a fixed hat.

\section{Finite colors obstruct both measure and category success}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:colors}

\begin{definition}[Visibility graph]
An undirected graph \(G\) on \(\N\) is a visibility graph for a strategy
if \(f_i\) depends only on hats at neighbors of \(i\). Neighborhoods may
be infinite. An \(r\)-coloring is a map
\(c:\N\to\{1,\ldots,r\}\) with different colors at the two ends of every
edge.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Finite-chromatic obstruction]
\label{mbg:qry:thm:colors}
Suppose the strategy has a visibility graph with a finite \(r\)-coloring.
Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item For every finite set \(I\) of scored players,
\[
 \Prb\left(\sum_{i\in I}Y_i\le0\right)\ge2^{-r},
 \qquad
 \Prb\left(\sum_{i\in I}Y_i\ge0\right)\ge2^{-r},
\]
provided the predictions are measurable.
\item For measurable predictions, \(\Prb(A_+)=\Prb(A_-)=0\).
\item For predictions with the Baire property, both \(A_+\) and \(A_-\)
are meager.
\end{enumerate}
No degree bound, finite-information assumption, or bound on the number
of observers of a hat is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(a=(a_1,\ldots,a_r)\in\F_2^r\), define a homeomorphism preserving
product measure by
\[
 (T_ax)_j=(-1)^{a_{c(j)}}x_j.
\]
Flipping the bit \(a_{c(i)}\) changes \(X_i\) to its negative and leaves
every hat inspected by \(f_i\) unchanged. Therefore the average over
the group annihilates every score:
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{a\in\F_2^r}Y_i(T_ax)=0,
 \qquad
 \sum_{a\in\F_2^r}\sum_{i\in I}Y_i(T_ax)=0.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:color-average}
\end{equation}
At least one value in the latter sum is nonpositive and at least one is
nonnegative. Averaging the corresponding indicator inequality and using
measure preservation proves (a).

For (b), fix a cylinder \(C\) on the first \(m\) hats and modify \(T_a\)
so that it flips only coordinates greater than \(m\). These maps preserve
\(C\) and its conditional probability. Equation~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:color-average}
still holds for every owner \(i>m\). It follows that
\[
 \left|2^{-r}\sum_aF_n(T_ax)\right|\le m
 \qquad(n>m,\ x\in C).
\]
Thus the finitely many points \(T_ax\) cannot all belong to \(A_+\).
Their indicator average is at most \(1-2^{-r}\). Integration gives
\(\Prb(A_+\mid C)\le1-2^{-r}\), and
Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:density} proves nullity. The negative direction is identical.

For (c), if \(A_+\) were nonmeager and had the Baire property, it would
be comeager in some nonempty basic cylinder \(C\). Use the same
cylinder-preserving transformations. Each \(T_a^{-1}A_+\) would be
comeager in \(C\). Their finite intersection would be nonempty by the
Baire category theorem. A point in that intersection would make all
the sequences \(F_n(T_ax)\) tend to \(+\infty\), contradicting their
bounded average. Apply the same reasoning to \(A_-\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Bounded outgoing visibility admits finitely many colors]
\label{mbg:qry:lem:coloring}
Suppose each player can inspect a predetermined set of at most \(b\)
hats. The undirected graph obtained by joining two players whenever
either may inspect the other has a \((2b+1)\)-coloring.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In each finite induced subgraph on \(U\), the number of edges is at most
\(b|U|\), since every edge has an outgoing witness in \(U\).
Its average degree is at most \(2b\). The same holds in every induced
subgraph, so successive removal of a vertex of degree at most \(2b\),
followed by reverse greedy coloring, gives \(2b+1\) colors.
For the countable graph, consider the finitely branching tree of proper
colorings of the first \(n\) vertices. Every level is nonempty by the
finite argument. An infinite branch gives the claimed coloring.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The bounded-query question]
\label{mbg:qry:cor:bounded}
If each player makes at most \(d\) adaptive queries, for one common
finite \(d\), then \(A_+\) and \(A_-\) are null and meager.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The union of all coordinates queried by one depth-\(d\) decision tree
has size at most \(2^d-1\). Apply Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:coloring} with that
bound, and then Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:colors}. The rules are continuous.
For \(d=0\), the graph has no edges and one color suffices.
\end{proof}

This already settles the repository question without an analytic
inequality. The potentially large number of colors is irrelevant for
nullity and category. The next sections give substantially better
finite probability bounds.

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
This corollary answers the uniform-bound clause of Part~\ref{mbg:hat:part}'s Research question~29.2 negatively, and more strongly than asked: probability zero, not merely ``not almost surely''. Source~04 proves its probability part independently as Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-no-divergence}, by the analytic route that the paragraph after Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:finite-analytic} sketches (a sign bound for the conditioned score of bounded degree, then martingale convergence); its proof is printed there as a second route. Category is source~06's alone.
\end{writenote}

\section{Quantitative bounds from Boolean degree}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:fourier}

For a finite coordinate set \(V\), the Walsh characters are
\(\chi_A(x)=\prod_{j\in A}x_j\), \(A\subseteq V\).
Every real function on the finite cube has the unique expansion
\[
 P=\sum_{A\subseteq V}\widehat P(A)\chi_A,
 \qquad
 \E P^2=\sum_A\widehat P(A)^2.
\]
Its degree is the largest \(|A|\) with nonzero coefficient.

\begin{lemma}[Decision-tree degree]
\label{mbg:qry:lem:degree}
A Boolean rule computed by at most \(d\) adaptive queries has Walsh
degree at most \(d\). Consequently every finite sum of its correctness
scores has degree at most \(d+1\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
At the first query \(j\), with two remaining subtree functions
\(g_+,g_-\), the rule is
\[
 f=\frac{1+x_j}{2}g_++\frac{1-x_j}{2}g_-.
\]
Induction on depth proves the bound. Replacing \(x_j^2\) by \(1\) in a
multilinear representation cannot increase degree. Multiplication by
the owner coordinate increases degree by at most one, and summing does
not increase it.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The classical sign estimate, with a short elementary proof}

The following weaker form of the classical hypercontractive sign bound
has a convenient self-contained proof. The sharper constant is recorded
immediately afterward. These analytic inequalities are established
background, not new results of this article \cite{qry:Bonami,qry:ODonnell}.

\begin{lemma}[A sign bound]
\label{mbg:qry:lem:sign}
If \(P\) is nonzero, centered, and has degree at most \(k\), then
\[
 \Prb(P>0),\ \Prb(P<0)\ \ge\frac{1}{4\cdot9^k}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First prove
\begin{equation}
 \|P\|_4^2\le\sum_A3^{|A|}\widehat P(A)^2.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:bonami}
\end{equation}
Induct on the number of coordinates, writing \(P=A+x_jB\).
With \(a=\|A\|_4^2\) and \(b=\|B\|_4^2\), Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\[
 \E P^4=\E A^4+6\E A^2B^2+\E B^4
 \le a^2+6ab+b^2\le(a+3b)^2.
\]
The inductive estimates for \(A,B\) give~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:bonami}. Hence
\(\|P\|_4\le3^{k/2}\|P\|_2\).
Interpolation by H\"older's inequality yields
\[
 \|P\|_2\le\|P\|_1^{1/3}\|P\|_4^{2/3},
 \qquad
 \|P\|_1\ge3^{-k}\|P\|_2.
\]
Since \(P\) is centered, \(\E P_+=\E P_-=\|P\|_1/2\).
Another use of Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\[
 \tfrac12\|P\|_1
 =\E(P\ind_{\{P>0\}})
 \le\|P\|_2\Prb(P>0)^{1/2}.
\]
Squaring proves the positive estimate; replace \(P\) by \(-P\) for
the negative estimate.
\end{proof}

O'Donnell's Theorems~9.22 and~9.24 state the sharper classical bounds
\begin{equation}
 \|P\|_1\ge e^{-k}\|P\|_2,\qquad
 \Prb(P>0),\Prb(P<0)\ge\tfrac14e^{-2k}
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:sharpclassical}
\end{equation}
for centered nonzero \(P\) \cite{qry:ODonnell}. The same elementary
last step derives the second inequality from the first.

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
Source~04 states the same estimates as Lemmas~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-hypercontractivity} and~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-sign}, credits the sharper constant to Austrin and H\aa stad~\cite{insp:AustrinHastadSTOC} rather than to O'Donnell, and derives $\norm{P}_1\ge e^{-d}\norm{P}_2$ from the degree-$d$ hypercontractive inequality by letting $p\downarrow2$; that derivation is printed there.
\end{writenote}

\begin{corollary}[Uniform finite-horizon obstruction]
\label{mbg:qry:cor:finite-analytic}
For any finite set of players whose rules use at most \(d\) adaptive
queries,
\[
 \Prb(F\le0),\Prb(F\ge0)\ge\tfrac14e^{-2(d+1)},
 \qquad F=\sum_iY_i.
\]
The weaker constant \(1/(4\cdot9^{d+1})\) follows solely from the
proof above.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:degree}, centering, and~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:sharpclassical}.
If \(F\) vanishes identically, both nonstrict inequalities have
probability \(1\). Hats observed outside the scored team are simply
additional independent variables.
\end{proof}

After fixing finitely many hats and deleting their owners, the same
degree bound and conditional centering remain valid. Thus
Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:conditional-sign} gives a second proof of the probability
part of Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:bounded}.

\subsection{A necessary aggregate Fourier tail}

The degree argument has a stable version. This explains why a small
probability of a long inspection cannot simply be ignored in a
large team.

\begin{proposition}[Fourier-tail obstruction]
\label{mbg:qry:prop:spectral}
Let \(F\) be a nonzero centered function on a finite cube. Write
\(F=P+R\), where \(P\) is its Walsh projection to degrees at most \(k\),
and put
\[
 \delta=\frac{\|R\|_2^2}{\|F\|_2^2}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 \Prb(F\le0),\Prb(F\ge0)\ge
 \frac14\left(e^{-k}\sqrt{1-\delta}-\sqrt{\delta}\right)_+^2.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:spectral}
\end{equation}
In particular, if centered finite scores \(F_n\) satisfy
\(\Prb(F_n>0)\to1\), then for each fixed \(k\),
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{\|(F_n)_{>k}\|_2^2}{\|F_n\|_2^2}
 \ge\frac{1}{1+e^{2k}},
\]
with the ratios taken at the eventually nonzero scores.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Orthogonality gives
\(\|P\|_2=\sqrt{1-\delta}\|F\|_2\).
The triangle inequality,~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:sharpclassical}, and
\(\|R\|_1\le\|R\|_2\) imply
\[
 \|F\|_1\ge
 \left(e^{-k}\sqrt{1-\delta}-\sqrt{\delta}\right)_+\|F\|_2.
\]
Apply the centered positive-part argument from Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:sign}
directly to \(F\). For the final assertion, a subsequence with
\(\delta<1/(1+e^{2k})\) bounded away from that value would keep the
right side of~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:spectral} bounded away from zero.
\end{proof}

\section{Stronger finite bounds from sparse supports}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:supports}

The generic degree estimate disregards the special form \(Y_i=X_if_i\).
The owner coordinate in every monomial lets us produce finite
collections of sign flips that give better constants.

\begin{theorem}[Sparse-support cancellation]
\label{mbg:qry:thm:support}
Let \(I\) be a finite scored team with finite-coordinate prediction rules.
Write the nonzero Walsh support of
each legal rule as \(\mathcal A_i\), and suppose
\[
 b_i:=\sum_{A\in\mathcal A_i}(|A|+1)\le B,
 \qquad B\ge1.
\]
Let \(Q\) be the smallest power of \(2\) strictly greater than \(B\).
For arbitrary real weights \(w_i\), the score
\(F=\sum_{i\in I}w_iX_if_i\) satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \Prb(F\le0),\Prb(F\ge0)\ge Q^{-1}.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:support-bound}
\end{equation}
No nondegeneracy assumption is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose a finite \(V\) containing the owners and every coordinate on
which one of the prediction rules depends. Form a
hypergraph \(\mathcal H\) consisting of the distinct nonempty sets
\(\{i\}\cup A\), \(i\in I\), \(A\in\mathcal A_i\).
For every nonempty \(U\subseteq V\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{\substack{E\in\mathcal H\\E\subseteq U}}|E|
 \le \sum_{i\in I\cap U}b_i\le B|U|.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:incidence}
\end{equation}
Indeed, every edge contained in \(U\) has an owner in \(U\);
counting all its possible owners only overcounts.
The left side is the sum of vertex degrees in the induced hypergraph.
It therefore has a vertex incident to at most \(\lfloor B\rfloor\)
edges. Repeatedly remove such a vertex, retaining its removal order.

Write \(Q=2^r\). Assign labels \(v_j\in\F_2^r\) in reverse removal
order. Each edge whose other vertices have already been assigned
forbids exactly one label for the current vertex: the sum of those
other labels. There are at most \(\lfloor B\rfloor<Q\) such forbidden
labels. Choose any permitted one. Every edge then has
\[
 \sum_{j\in E}v_j\ne0.
\]
For \(a\in\F_2^r\), define
\[
 (T_ax)_j=(-1)^{a\cdot v_j}x_j.
\]
Character orthogonality gives
\[
 \sum_a\chi_E(T_ax)=
 \chi_E(x)\sum_a(-1)^{a\cdot\sum_{j\in E}v_j}=0
 \quad(E\in\mathcal H).
\]
Hence \(\sum_aF(T_ax)=0\) pointwise. At least one group value is
nonpositive and one is nonnegative. Averaging their indicators and
using the invariance of the fair cube proves~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:support-bound}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Predetermined sets of visible hats}

If a rule depends on at most \(d\) predetermined hats, then, including
even the subsets with zero coefficient,
\[
 b_i\le\sum_{A\subseteq[d]}(|A|+1)=(d+2)2^{d-1}.
\]
For \(d=0\) the value is \(1\). Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:support} yields
\begin{equation}
 \Prb(F\le0),\Prb(F\ge0)\ge
 c_d:=2^{-d-\lfloor\log_2(d+2)\rfloor}
 \ge\frac{1}{(d+2)2^d}.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:nonadaptive}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Adaptive trees}

For a depth-\(d\) tree, \(d\ge1\), expand the tree down to level \(d-1\).
There are at most \(2^{d-1}\) branches at that level. The remaining
function on each branch is a constant or a signed single coordinate.
Its product with the branch's path indicator has weighted support at most
\[
 \sum_{t=0}^{d-1}\binom{d-1}{t}(t+2)
 =(d+3)2^{d-2}.
\]
Here the weight of a monomial on \(t\) path variables and at most one
bottom variable is at most \(t+2\), after including the owner.
Short branches can be extended in this calculation by constant
subtrees; repeated variables and cancellation only reduce the bound.
Consequently
\[
 b_i\le B_d^{\rm ad}:=(d+3)2^{2d-3},
\]
and therefore
\begin{equation}
 \Prb(F\le0),\Prb(F\ge0)\ge
 c_d^{\rm ad}:=2^{-2d+2-\lfloor\log_2(d+3)\rfloor}
 \ge\frac{4}{(d+3)4^d}.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:adaptive}
\end{equation}
When \(d=0\), use \(1/2\). Fixing some hats and reexpanding the
remaining rules does not increase the weighted support, so these
constants also apply conditionally after deletion of fixed owners.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c c c c}
\toprule
\(d\)& Fixed visible set: \(c_d\)& Adaptive tree: \(c_d^{\rm ad}\)& Status\\
\midrule
0&\(1/2\)&\(1/2\)&Sharp\\
1&\(1/4\)&\(1/4\)&Sharp\\
2&\(1/16\)&\(1/16\)&Proven lower bounds\\
3&\(1/32\)&\(1/64\)&Proven lower bounds\\
4&\(1/64\)&\(1/256\)&Proven lower bounds\\
5&\(1/128\)&\(1/2048\)&Proven lower bounds\\
6&\(1/512\)&\(1/8192\)&Proven lower bounds\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{The exact one-query value}

\begin{theorem}[One query: sharp success probability]
\label{mbg:qry:thm:onequery}
Over all nonempty finite teams and legal deterministic strategies with
at most one query per player,
\[
 \sup\Prb(D>0)=\frac34.
\]
With zero queries the supremum is \(1/2\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The upper bound \(3/4\) is~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:adaptive} at \(d=1\).
For attainment, take three players in a directed cycle, each predicting
the opposite of the next player's hat. In sign notation,
\[
 F=-X_1X_2-X_2X_3-X_3X_1.
\]
If all hats agree, \(F=-3\); otherwise \(F=1\). The first event has
probability \(2/8=1/4\), and the second has probability \(3/4\).
The zero-query sum is a signed sum of independent hats, so global
complementation negates it; hence its strict-positive probability is
at most \(1/2\). One player attains equality.
\end{proof}

An alternative four-transformation proof for the one-query bound labels
the three colors of its visibility graph by the three nonzero vectors of
\(\F_2^2\). Singletons and edge endpoint sums then all have nonzero
labels. This also makes the small finite certificate particularly easy
to verify.

For a quantitative consequence of~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:nonadaptive}, if strict
positive excess has probability at least \(1-\eta\), then
\[
 d+\log_2(d+2)\ge\log_2(1/\eta).
\]
Writing \(L=\log_2(1/\eta)\) gives the explicit necessary condition
\[
 d\ge L-\log_2(L+2).
\]
If \(d>L\) this is automatic; otherwise use
\(\log_2(d+2)\le\log_2(L+2)\). We do not claim these finite bounds are
optimal beyond one query.

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
The three-player cycle is Part~\ref{mbg:part:one}'s three-box team of Section~\ref{mbg:sec:checks} (player $p$ guesses $1-x_{p+1\bmod3}$) and source~04's trace team for $r=2$, $m=1$ (Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:fin-trace}). Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:onequery} shows that no team with one query per player does better, so source~04's lower bound $p_1\ge3/4$ in Research question~\ref{mbg:insp:sec:questions}.2 is an equality.
\end{writenote}

\section{Expected query cost: the lower half of the exact threshold}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:expected}

The number \(2\) comes from the geometry of a binary decision tree under
fair independent answers. This observation turns an average resource
restriction into a worst-case restriction.

\begin{lemma}[A deep path costs almost two queries in expectation]
\label{mbg:qry:lem:pathcost}
Let a deterministic query algorithm have a feasible execution path with
at least \(L\ge1\) queries. Under independent fair hats,
\begin{equation}
 \E Q\ge\sum_{j=0}^{L-1}2^{-j}=2-2^{1-L}.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:pathcost}
\end{equation}
Consequently, if \(0\le C<2\) and \(\E Q\le C\), every execution has at
most
\begin{equation}
 d(C)=\left\lfloor1-\log_2(2-C)\right\rfloor
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:depthfrommean}
\end{equation}
queries.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For each \(j<L\), agreeing with the first \(j\) answers on that execution
forces the algorithm to make query \(j+1\). These answers specify at most
\(j\) independent hats, so their event has probability at least \(2^{-j}\).
Thus \(\Prb(Q\ge j+1)\ge2^{-j}\). Summing these tail probabilities gives
\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:pathcost}. Repeated queries do not invalidate the estimate:
they specify fewer fresh hats and can only increase the prefix
probability. Equivalently, they can first be eliminated.

Solving \(2-2^{1-L}\le C\) for the integer \(L\) gives
\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:depthfrommean}. An infinite execution would have arbitrarily
long feasible finite prefixes and would therefore force \(\E Q\ge2\).
The same argument handles algorithms initially assumed only to halt
almost surely; a nonterminating computation after a finite query
transcript would occur on a positive-probability cylinder and is already
excluded by that assumption.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Uniform expectation strictly below two is impossible]
\label{mbg:qry:cor:meanlower}
For deterministic legal query algorithms,
\[
 \sup_i\E Q_i<2
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \Prb(A_+)=\Prb(A_-)=0,
\]
and both divergence events are meager.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Choose a common \(C<2\) bounding the expectations. Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:pathcost}
gives a common finite depth bound \(d(C)\), so apply
Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:bounded}.
\end{proof}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c c c}
\toprule
Uniform expected cap \(C\)&Forced depth cap&Positive divergence\\
\midrule
\(1\)&\(1\)&Null and meager\\
\(3/2\)&\(2\)&Null and meager\\
\(7/4\)&\(3\)&Null and meager\\
\(15/8\)&\(4\)&Null and meager\\
\(31/16\)&\(5\)&Null and meager\\
\(2\)&No finite cap follows&Almost sure success is attainable\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{proposition}[The shape of trees below the threshold]
\label{mbg:qry:prop:comb}
In a reduced finite deterministic binary decision tree, where each queried
hat is fresh along its path, \(\E Q<2\) if and only if its internal
vertices form a single path. In this case, if its depth is \(L\), then
\[
 \E Q=2-2^{1-L},
\]
with the value \(0\) when \(L=0\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If the internal vertices form a path, exactly one answer continues at
each step before the final query, giving tail probabilities \(2^{-j}\)
and the stated sum.
Otherwise, consider the first vertex with two internal children.
If it is at depth \(k\), the path reaching it has probability \(2^{-k}\).
The expected cost before reaching or avoiding that vertex is
\(2-2^{1-k}\); the conditional expected remaining cost at that vertex
is at least \(2\), since both answers force another query.
The total is therefore at least
\[
 2-2^{1-k}+2^{-k}\cdot2=2.
\]
\end{proof}

The critical construction below uses precisely these path-shaped
decision trees, with depths tending to infinity. Every individual mean
is below \(2\), but the means have no common bound strictly below \(2\).

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:pathcost} and Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:meanlower} are source~04's Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-path} and Corollary~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-mean}, proved there by the same path argument; source~04 states the lemma as the stochastic domination $Q\succeq\min(G,L)$ and extends the corollary to every nonnegative nondecreasing cost (Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-frontier}). Proposition~\ref{mbg:qry:prop:comb} has no counterpart there.
\end{writenote}

\section{The efficient implementation of a known block strategy}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:block}

For this section use bits \(x_i\in\{0,1\}\), with \(0\) black and \(1\)
white. Form a block of \(2m\) players in the logical order
\[
 S_1,T_1,S_2,T_2,\ldots,S_m,T_m.
\]
The letter \(T\) here names a partner role, not a sign transformation.
The baseline pairs strategy has \(S_j\) copy \(T_j\)'s hat and \(T_j\)
guess the opposite of \(S_j\)'s hat. It gives exactly one correct guess
per pair.

Eldredge's block rule modifies this baseline when a player sees only
black hats \cite[Proposition~6.6]{hat:Eldredge}. The following operational
description computes that same prediction rule while often stopping
after its first query.

\begin{definition}[Early-stopping block algorithm]
\label{mbg:qry:def:block}
Player \(T_j\) queries \(S_j\)'s hat and predicts its opposite.
Player \(S_j\) follows this decision tree:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Query the partner \(T_j\). If its hat is \(1\), predict \(1\) and stop.
\item If its hat is \(0\), inspect the other \(2m-2\) visible hats in a
fixed order, stopping at the first \(1\). If one is found, predict \(0\).
\item If none is found, predict \(1\).
\end{enumerate}
No query refers to the player's own hat.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Exact block law and prefixes]
\label{mbg:qry:prop:blocklaw}
Let \(\Delta_m\) be the surplus contributed by a complete block.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_m=
 \begin{cases}
 -m,&\text{all hats are black},\\
 1,&\text{there is exactly one white hat, on an \(S\)-player},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:blocklaw}
\end{equation}
Thus
\begin{equation}
 \Prb(\Delta_m=-m)=4^{-m},\quad
 \Prb(\Delta_m=1)=m4^{-m},\quad
 \Prb(\Delta_m=0)=1-(m+1)4^{-m}.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:blockprob}
\end{equation}
On every block except the all-black block, the surplus at completed-pair
boundaries is nondecreasing. On such a nonbad block, relative to the
complete-block endpoint, the surplus at every logical prefix differs
by at most \(1\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
An \(S\)-player departs from copying its partner only when every visible
hat is black. If the entire block is black, all players predict white;
every pair then contributes \(-1\).
If the only white hat is on \(S_j\), that player's modified prediction
is correct and its partner's opposite prediction is also correct.
Their pair contributes \(+1\); all other pairs remain neutral.

If the only white hat belongs to a \(T\)-player, its opposite-partner
prediction was already white. Its \(S\)-partner copies white and is wrong,
so the pair is neutral. If there are at least two white hats, each
\(S\)-player sees a white hat and uses the baseline copying rule.
All pairs are neutral. This proves the pointwise law; counting the
all-black configuration and the \(m\) favorable singleton configurations
gives~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:blockprob}.

On a nonbad block there is at most one positive pair and no negative pair.
A partially completed pair contributes either \(1/2\) or \(-1/2\).
In a positive block every \(S\)-player is correct. Before the exceptional
pair the surplus is \(0\)
at pair boundaries, after it the surplus is \(1\), and its first member
is correct. The difference from the final value is therefore in
\([-1,1/2]\). In a neutral block it is in \([-1/2,1/2]\).
\end{proof}

In particular \(\E\Delta_m=-m4^{-m}+m4^{-m}=0\).
For example, when \(m=2\), the three surplus values are
\(-2,1,0\) with probabilities \(1/16,2/16,13/16\).
The mechanism gains one on some rare configurations and loses much
more on even rarer configurations.

\begin{proposition}[Exact query distribution]
\label{mbg:qry:prop:cost}
For an \(S\)-player in a block of \(m\) pairs,
\[
 Q_S\ \stackrel{\mathrm d}{=}\ \min(G,2m-1),
\]
where \(G\) has geometric law
\(\Prb(G=t)=2^{-t}\), \(t=1,2,\ldots\).
Consequently
\begin{align}
 \E Q_S&=2-2^{-(2m-2)}, & Q_T&=1, \label{mbg:qry:eq:costmean}\\
 \Prb(Q_i>t)&\le2^{-t} &&
 (t=0,1,2,\ldots). \label{mbg:qry:eq:costtail}
\end{align}
For \(S\)-players the tail inequality is equality when \(t<2m-1\)
and is zero afterward. Every player has expected cost strictly below
\(2\), and the expected cost averaged over a pair is
\[
 \frac32-2^{-(2m-1)}<\frac32.
\]
For \(0<\lambda<\log2\), uniformly in \(m\) and the player,
\begin{equation}
 \E e^{\lambda Q_i}\le\frac{e^\lambda}{2-e^\lambda}.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:mgf}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
To continue after its first query, an \(S\)-player must see a black
partner. To continue after \(t\) queries, all \(t\) inspected hats must
have been black. They are distinct independent fair bits. This has
probability \(2^{-t}\), until the \(2m-1\) visible hats are exhausted.
The tail-sum formula gives~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:costmean}.
Stochastic domination by \(G\) gives~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:mgf} from
\[
 \E e^{\lambda G}
 =\sum_{t\ge1}(e^\lambda/2)^t
 =\frac{e^\lambda}{2-e^\lambda}.
\]
The partner role uses exactly one query.
\end{proof}

The cost statement counts individual hats, including the partner.
It assumes neither free parity queries nor a public signal.
This distinction matters: an implementation that first inspects every
visible hat computes the same guesses but misses the expected-cost
conclusion completely.

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
Definition~\ref{mbg:qry:def:block} and Propositions~\ref{mbg:qry:prop:blocklaw}--\ref{mbg:qry:prop:cost} are source~04's implementation of Section~\ref{mbg:insp:con-section} with its Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:con-block-score} and Proposition~\ref{mbg:insp:con-query-distribution}, found independently (source~04 writes $k$ for the number of pairs, here~$m$). Source~04's proposition adds that, for blocks of unbounded size, $\sup_i\E\phi(Q_i)=\E\phi(G)$ for every nonnegative nondecreasing~$\phi$, so the bound~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:mgf} is the exact supremum. The block rule is Eldredge's~\cite[Proposition~6.6]{hat:Eldredge}, as both sources say. Source~06's verifier calls the $T$ role~D, as source~04 did.
\end{writenote}

\section{Infinite schedules, exact rates, and the sharp threshold}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:schedule}

Choose a real parameter \(0<\varepsilon\le1\). For every integer \(m\ge2\),
stage \(m\) consists of
\begin{equation}
 L_m=\left\lceil\frac{4^m}{m^{1+\varepsilon}}\right\rceil
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:schedule}
\end{equation}
consecutive disjoint blocks of \(m\) pairs, each using
Definition~\ref{mbg:qry:def:block}. Stages occur in increasing order.
For rational \(\varepsilon\), these schedules can be made computable
using integer power comparisons; the especially simple
\(\varepsilon=1\) schedule uses only exact integer arithmetic.

\begin{theorem}[Almost-sure growth with uniformly geometric query tails]
\label{mbg:qry:thm:construction}
The stage strategy satisfies
\[
 D_n\to+\infty\quad\as,\qquad
 \sup_i\E Q_i=2,\qquad
 \E Q_i<2\ \text{for each }i,
\]
and the uniform bounds~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:costtail} and~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:mgf}.
More precisely, at every physical prefix,
\begin{align}
 D_n&\sim\frac{(\log_4 n)^{1-\varepsilon}}{1-\varepsilon}
 &&(0<\varepsilon<1), \label{mbg:qry:eq:ratepower}\\
 \frac{D_n}{\log\log n}&\longrightarrow1
 &&(\varepsilon=1), \label{mbg:qry:eq:ratelog}
\end{align}
almost surely, with natural logarithms in~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:ratelog}.
The worst-case number of queried hats and their maximum distance from
their owner are both \(O(\log n)\) for player \(n\).
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Only finitely many losses, infinitely many gains}

\begin{proof}[Proof of divergence in Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:construction}]
Call an all-black block bad and a block with the favorable singleton
white hat positive. The sum of bad-block probabilities is
\[
 \sum_{m\ge2}L_m4^{-m}
 \le \sum_{m\ge2}m^{-1-\varepsilon}+\sum_{m\ge2}4^{-m}<\infty.
\]
The first Borel--Cantelli lemma therefore gives only finitely many bad
blocks almost surely.
On the other hand, the sum of positive-block probabilities is
\[
 \sum_{m\ge2}L_m m4^{-m}
 =\sum_{m\ge2}m^{-\varepsilon}+O(1)=\infty.
\]
Distinct blocks use disjoint hats, so their positive events are
independent. There are infinitely many positive blocks almost surely.
For completeness, independence gives probability at most
\(\exp(-\sum p_b)\) of no positive block on a finite interval of blocks;
letting the endpoint tend to infinity and then intersecting over
starting points proves the assertion.

After the last bad block, completed-pair surpluses never decrease and
increase by \(1\) at each positive block. Odd physical prefixes differ
from completed-pair values by \(1/2\).
This proves divergence at all prefixes. Proposition~\ref{mbg:qry:prop:cost}
gives the cost bounds, and its means approach \(2\) as \(m\to\infty\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{A self-contained strong law for the gains}

\begin{lemma}[Independent indicator counting]
\label{mbg:qry:lem:counting}
Let \(B_1,B_2,\ldots\) be independent indicators, let
\(M_j=\sum_{\ell\le j}\E B_\ell\), and suppose \(M_j\to\infty\).
Then
\[
 \frac{\sum_{\ell\le j}B_\ell}{M_j}\longrightarrow1
 \quad\as.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(j_k\) be the first index for which \(M_{j_k}\ge k^2\).
Because each increment of \(M_j\) is at most \(1\),
\(k^2\le M_{j_k}<k^2+1\). Independence gives variance at most
\(M_{j_k}\), so for any fixed \(\delta>0\),
\[
 \Prb\left(\left|\sum_{\ell\le j_k}B_\ell-M_{j_k}\right|
             >\delta M_{j_k}\right)
 \le\frac{1}{\delta^2M_{j_k}}
 \le\frac{1}{\delta^2k^2}.
\]
Borel--Cantelli, followed by a countable sequence of positive
\(\delta\)'s tending to zero, proves the limit along \(j_k\).
For \(j_k\le j\le j_{k+1}\), both the partial indicator sum and its
expectation are nondecreasing, and
\(M_{j_{k+1}}/M_{j_k}\to1\). Squeezing gives the full limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of the rates in Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:construction}]
Let \(G_M\) be the number of positive blocks through stage \(M\).
Its mean is
\begin{equation}
 \mu_M=\sum_{m=2}^M L_m m4^{-m}
 =\sum_{m=2}^Mm^{-\varepsilon}+O(1).
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:gainmean}
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:counting} implies \(G_M/\mu_M\to1\) almost surely.
At completed block endpoints after the last bad block, the total
surplus is the number of positive blocks minus
\[
 B=\sum_{\text{bad blocks }b}m(b),
\]
where \(m(b)\) is its number of pairs. We have \(B<\infty\) almost
surely. After the final bad block, the same identity holds at
completed-pair endpoints with the gain count recording the exceptional
pair of each positive block.

The total number of physical players through stage \(M\) is
\begin{equation}
 N_M=\sum_{m=2}^M2mL_m
 \sim\frac83\frac{4^M}{M^\varepsilon}.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:playercount}
\end{equation}
To check the constant, divide
\(\sum_{m=2}^M4^m/m^\varepsilon\) by \(4^M/M^\varepsilon\).
The term at \(m=M-j\) tends to \(4^{-j}\), whose sum is \(4/3\).
The part \(m<M/2\) is exponentially negligible; in the remaining
part it is dominated by \(2^\varepsilon4^{-j}\).
The rounding contribution to \(N_M\) is \(O(M^2)\) and is negligible.
Multiplication by \(2\) gives \(8/3\).

Thus, uniformly for a physical index in stage \(M\),
\[
 \log_4 n=M+O(\log M).
\]
The means in adjacent stages have ratio tending to one, because
\(\mu_M-\mu_{M-1}=M^{-\varepsilon}+O(M4^{-M})\).
After the last bad block, all completed-pair scores within a stage
lie between its two endpoint scores; the unfinished-pair error is
at most \(1/2\). The endpoint asymptotics therefore hold at every
prefix. Finally,
\[
 \mu_M\sim\frac{M^{1-\varepsilon}}{1-\varepsilon}
 \quad(0<\varepsilon<1),\qquad
 \mu_M=\log M+O(1)\quad(\varepsilon=1).
\]
These formulas and~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:playercount} prove
\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:ratepower}--\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:ratelog}.
Each query remains in a block of \(2M\) players and there are at most
\(2M-1\) of them. This proves both \(O(\log n)\) resource statements.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The exact expected-cost threshold}

\begin{theorem}[Critical deterministic expected budget]
\label{mbg:qry:thm:threshold}
Let \(\mathcal S_+\) be the class of deterministic legal query strategies
with \(\Prb(D_n\to+\infty)>0\), and let \(\mathcal S_1\) require that
probability to equal \(1\). Then
\begin{equation}
 \inf_{\mathcal S\in\mathcal S_+}\sup_i\E Q_i
 =
 \inf_{\mathcal S\in\mathcal S_1}\sup_i\E Q_i
 =2.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:threshold}
\end{equation}
The value \(2\) is attained by an everywhere finite, computable strategy
whose query tails satisfy~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:costtail}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:meanlower} excludes every value below \(2\), even
for positive-probability success. The \(\varepsilon=1\) strategy of
Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:construction} attains \(2\) with almost-sure success.
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
Source~04 proves the same threshold as the mean case of Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:main-frontier}, by the same two halves (Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-path} with Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-no-divergence} below, the early-stopping block with its schedule above), and strengthens the lower half to every nonnegative nondecreasing cost: tails $2^{-r}$, second moment~$6$, exponential moments $z/(2-z)$ for $1\le z<2$ and none for $z\ge2$ (Corollary~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-moments}). Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:construction} and source~04's Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:con-rate-theorem} are the same theorem, with $p=1+\varepsilon$ and $\lfloor4^k/k^p\rfloor$ blocks of $k$ pairs in stage~$k$ for $\lceil4^m/m^{1+\varepsilon}\rceil$; source~04's proof, a second route through Kronecker's lemma, is printed there because its later results use its notation.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{Why fairness and almost-sure growth coexist}

Although \(B\) is almost surely finite,
\begin{equation}
 \E B=\sum_{m\ge2}L_m m4^{-m}=\infty.
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:debtmean}
\end{equation}
Every finite endpoint has equal expected positive gain and negative
debt. Along almost every sample, only finitely many losses actually
occur and infinitely many gains remain. Passing the limit through
expectation would require an integrability condition that fails.

The same distinction explains why the geometric query tail does not
contradict the degree obstruction. The queries are usually short,
but their deterministic depths are unbounded. Rare long branches
support an essential part of the aggregate score's variance.
Proposition~\ref{mbg:qry:prop:spectral} makes the latter assertion quantitative:
success with probability tending to one requires a definite fraction
of Fourier mass above every fixed degree.

\begin{proposition}[Category behavior at the critical cost]
\label{mbg:qry:prop:categorypositive}
For the contiguous stage strategy of Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:construction},
the set \(A_+\) is conull and meager. There is a comeager set on which
\[
 \limsup_nD_n=+\infty,\qquad \liminf_nD_n=-\infty.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Conullity is already proved. For positive integers \(K,N\), the sets
\[
 U^+_{K,N}=\bigcup_{n\ge N}\{D_n>K\},\qquad
 U^-_{K,N}=\bigcup_{n\ge N}\{D_n<-K\}
\]
are open, since every score uses finitely many hats. They are dense.
Given any cylinder, first complete the finitely many blocks meeting its
fixed coordinates. Then assign sufficiently many subsequent blocks
positive configurations to reach a score above \(K\), or all-black
configurations to reach a score below \(-K\). The endpoint can be
chosen beyond \(N\), and each instruction fixes only finitely many
additional hats.
The intersection of all these open dense sets is comeager and has the
two asserted limit behaviors. It is disjoint from \(A_+\).
\end{proof}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
This is the phenomenon of Part~\ref{mbg:hat:part}'s Theorems~\ref{mbg:hat:thm:category}--\ref{mbg:hat:thm:success} (a conull meagre success set) for the critical-cost construction. It does not answer Part~\ref{mbg:hat:part}'s Research question~29.10, which asks whether strong growth can hold on a set that is conull \emph{and} comeagre: the construction here gives a meagre success set, and by Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:bounded} every strategy of bounded depth has meagre divergence sets.
\end{writenote}

\section{Prescribed budgets: count and distance are different}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:budgets}

\subsection{Arbitrarily sparse high-budget players}

\begin{lemma}[Two ordered matchings]
\label{mbg:qry:lem:matchings}
Take two disjoint infinite subsequences of \(\N\), and pair consecutive
members within each subsequence. At any physical prefix,
at most one pair from each subsequence has begun but not finished.
The total surplus from all such unfinished pairs has absolute value
at most \(1\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In the increasing enumeration of one subsequence, a prefix contains an
initial segment, which has at most one unpaired final member.
That member contributes \(1/2\) or \(-1/2\). There are two subsequences.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact classification of count budgets]
\label{mbg:qry:thm:budgets}
Let \(q:\N\to\N\cup\{0\}\) be finite valued and satisfy \(q(i)\ge1\)
for all sufficiently large \(i\). The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \(q\) is unbounded.
\item There is a deterministic legal strategy with \(Q_i(x)\le q(i)\)
for every player and every input, such that \(D_n\to+\infty\) almost surely.
\item There is such a strategy with positive-probability divergence.
\end{enumerate}
The strategy in (ii) can additionally satisfy
\(\Prb(Q_i>t)\le2^{-t}\) for all \(i,t\).
If \(q\) is computable and unbounded, an everywhere terminating
computable strategy can be constructed from it.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If \(q\) is bounded, Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:bounded} excludes (iii).
The implication (ii) to (iii) is immediate.

Assume \(q\) is unbounded. Let every player with \(q(i)=0\) use a
constant guess. There are finitely many such players, so their total
surplus is a bounded random offset; their locations need not be known
in advance.
Use the \(\varepsilon=1\) list of blocks from~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:schedule}.
For each block of \(m\) pairs, select \(2m\) previously unused indices,
all beyond the indices of the preceding block, each satisfying
\[
 q(i)\ge2m-1.
\]
This is possible: a finite-valued unbounded sequence has infinitely many
indices above every fixed threshold. Leave at least two unselected
guard indices between successive blocks, ensuring that the unselected
positive-budget subsequence is infinite.

Pair consecutive selected indices within each block and run
Definition~\ref{mbg:qry:def:block} on them. They may be far apart physically;
only the number of hats queried is constrained. Pair all unselected
indices with positive budget consecutively in their own increasing
enumeration and give them the baseline neutral pairs strategy.
Their cost is \(1\).
Selected players use at most \(2m-1\le q(i)\) queries.

Selected blocks depend on disjoint hats. The same two summability
arguments as in Section~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:schedule} give finitely many bad blocks
and infinitely many positive blocks almost surely. Completed neutral
pairs contribute zero. Completed selected pairs outside bad blocks
contribute \(0\) or \(1\), with infinitely many \(1\)'s.
The total negative contribution of bad blocks is finite almost surely.
The argument of Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:matchings} bounds the unfinished-pair
error by \(1\) at every physical prefix, besides the bounded contribution
of the zero-budget players. Therefore \(D_n\to+\infty\).
The query-tail distribution of each selected rule is unchanged by
relocating its coordinates; neutral and zero-budget players also satisfy
the stated tail bound.

For computable \(q\), choose eligible indices by increasing search,
using the first possible choices and designated guard indices.
Each search terminates by unboundedness. To determine the role of a
given input index \(i\), construct blocks until their endpoint passes
\(i\). The positive-budget complement is decidable by this procedure;
its next member can also be found by search because the guards and
eventual positivity ensure infinitude. Thus no bound on the locations
of the finitely many zero budgets is needed.
The resulting finite prediction algorithm terminates on every input.
\end{proof}

The eventual lower bound \(q(i)\ge1\) is material. It lets the unused
players be paired without creating an uncontrolled random score.
The theorem does not classify arbitrary budgets having infinitely many
zero entries.

\subsection{A budget tending to infinity permits local construction}

\begin{theorem}[Simultaneous count and distance constraints]
\label{mbg:qry:thm:local}
Let \(h:\N\to\N\cup\{0\}\) satisfy \(h(i)\to\infty\).
There is a deterministic strategy such that every query by player \(i\)
satisfies
\[
 Q_i(x)\le h(i),\qquad |j-i|\le h(i),
\]
and \(D_n\to+\infty\) almost surely. The uniform geometric query tail
and the expectation bound \(\E Q_i<2\) are retained.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Again take the \(\varepsilon=1\) list of blocks. Before placing a block
of length \(2m\), move to a sufficiently late odd starting position at
which every position in the next \(2m\) places has
\(h(i)\ge2m-1\). Such a position exists because \(h(i)\to\infty\).
Fill each intervening even-length gap with adjacent neutral pairs.
If either member of a filler pair has budget zero, let both members
guess constantly instead. Only finitely many filler pairs are
exceptional, and their total surplus is bounded.

Every active query is within its contiguous block, so both inequalities
hold. Neutral queries have count and distance \(1\), and exceptional
players make no queries. Inserting the filler pairs changes
completed-pair surplus by only a bounded total. The same block argument
proves almost-sure divergence and preserves the query-cost bounds.
If \(h\) is computable, a finite search for the next admissible interval
suffices; a computable modulus of convergence is not needed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The same unbounded numerical budget can permit one task
and forbid the other}

\begin{theorem}[Count--distance separation]
\label{mbg:qry:thm:separation}
Define
\begin{equation}
 h(i)=
 \begin{cases}
 k+1,&i=4^k,\quad k\ge1,\\
 1,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{mbg:qry:eq:sparsebudget}
\end{equation}
As a query-count budget, \(h\) permits \(D_n\to+\infty\) almost surely.
As a distance budget, every measurable legal strategy has
\(\Prb(A_+)=\Prb(A_-)=0\), and every Baire-measurable such strategy has
meager divergence events.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The sequence is unbounded and everywhere at least \(1\), so the
count conclusion follows from Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:budgets}.
For the distance conclusion, form the undirected allowed graph:
join \(i,j\) if \(|i-j|\le h(i)\) or \(|i-j|\le h(j)\).
Give every special index \(4^k\) color \(3\), and every other index
color \(1\) or \(2\) according to its parity.

An edge between two ordinary indices has length \(1\), so it joins
different parity colors. No two special indices can be adjacent:
for consecutive special indices the gap is \(3\cdot4^k\), larger
than both \(k+1\) and \(k+2\); all other gaps are larger.
An edge incident to exactly one special index also joins different
colors. This is a proper three-coloring.
Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:colors} applies even if each player reads its entire
allowed neighborhood.
\end{proof}

The distinction is structural. In the count model, selected high-budget
players can inspect one another across arbitrary gaps. In the distance
model, the high-radius centers remain isolated from one another.
Merely observing that \(h\) is unbounded does not identify which model
is intended.

\subsection{One-way visibility produces independent correctness}

\begin{proposition}[Acyclic directed visibility]
\label{mbg:qry:prop:acyclic}
Orient a visibility edge \(i\to j\) when player \(i\) may inspect hat \(j\).
If the directed graph has no directed cycle, then for measurable legal
rules the signs \(Y_i\) are independent fair signs. This remains true
with infinite sets of visible hats.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For any nonempty finite set \(I\) of owners, its induced directed graph
has a vertex \(i\) with no incoming edge. No \(Y_j\), \(j\in I\setminus
\{i\}\), depends on \(X_i\), and \(f_i\) does not depend on \(X_i\).
Conditioning on all other hats therefore gives
\[
 \E\prod_{j\in I}Y_j=0.
\]
All nonempty joint moments vanish. For any finite assignment of signs
\(y_i\), expand
\[
 \Prb(Y_i=y_i\text{ for all }i\in I)
 =\E\prod_{i\in I}\frac{1+y_iY_i}{2}=2^{-|I|}.
\]
This is precisely independence and fairness.
\end{proof}

In particular, past-only and future-only inspection give ordinary
symmetric random-walk scores, even if the number of available hats is
unbounded. Such a walk reaches every positive and negative integer
almost surely. An elementary proof uses the finite gambler's-ruin
calculation: starting at \(0\), the probability of hitting \(-a\)
before \(b\) is \(b/(a+b)\), obtained by stopping the bounded fair walk
at its first exit from \((-a,b)\). The exit is almost surely finite,
since a sufficiently long run of equal steps forces it and such
runs have a fixed positive probability on disjoint trials. Let
\(b\to\infty\), and then intersect over integer \(a\); reverse signs
for positive levels. Hence neither divergence event has positive
probability. This elementary observation emphasizes the role of
directed cycles in coordinated surplus.

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
With Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:bounded}, this section answers the three clauses of Part~\ref{mbg:hat:part}'s Research question~29.2 qualitatively: a uniform bound on the number of queries excludes $D_n\to+\infty$ almost surely, indeed with positive probability; for a count budget $q(i)$ that is eventually at least one, almost-sure divergence is possible exactly when $q$ is unbounded (Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:budgets}); and a count budget differs from a distance budget (Theorems~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:local}--\ref{mbg:qry:thm:separation}). The question's first clause, which excess \emph{profiles} remain attainable under a given $q(i)$, is answered only as ``divergent or not''; the attainable growth rates under a budget, budgets with infinitely many zeros and general distance profiles stay open (Research questions~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:questions}.3, \ref{mbg:qry:sec:questions}.4 and~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:questions}.6).
\end{writenote}

\section{Free private randomness changes the expected-cost problem}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:random}

The word deterministic in Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:threshold} cannot be omitted.
Randomization is harmless for a fixed worst-case depth bound, but
it changes the expected query budget.

\begin{proposition}[Bounded depth with independent random seeds]
\label{mbg:qry:prop:randomdepth}
If random seeds are independent of all hats and every realized prediction
algorithm uses at most the same finite \(d\) queries, then
\(\Prb(A_+)=\Prb(A_-)=0\), where probability includes both hats and seeds.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Condition on all seeds. The resulting deterministic strategy satisfies
Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:bounded}. Integrate the resulting zero probabilities.
For this statement we assume the randomized strategy is jointly
measurable, as ordinary query algorithms are.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Expected budgets arbitrarily close to one]
\label{mbg:qry:thm:randomcost}
For every \(C>1\), there is a legal strategy using independent private
random bits at the players, with
\[
 \sup_i\E Q_i<C,\qquad D_n\to+\infty\quad\as.
\]
No communication or shared random seed is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose a dyadic \(0<\alpha<\min\{1,C-1\}\), so activation can be
sampled using finitely many fair private bits, and use the
\(\varepsilon=1\) stage schedule. Each \(S\)-player independently
activates its early-stopping rule with probability \(\alpha\);
otherwise it simply copies its partner. Every \(T\)-player always
predicts the opposite of its partner.

On a block that is not all black, no pair has negative surplus.
A favorable singleton white hat on an \(S\)-player yields \(+1\)
exactly when that player activates, an event of probability
\(\alpha m4^{-m}\). All-black blocks are still the only possible
negative blocks; their probabilities remain \(4^{-m}\), regardless
of the activation decisions.
Thus there are only finitely many negative blocks and infinitely
many positive blocks almost surely. Block events are independent
because both their hats and their private coins are disjoint.
The same prefix proof gives positive divergence.

A \(T\)-player uses one query. For an \(S\)-player,
\[
 \E Q_S=(1-\alpha)\cdot1+
 \alpha\bigl(2-2^{-(2m-2)}\bigr)<1+\alpha<C.
\]
This proves the claim.
\end{proof}

The lower bound from a fixed deterministic branch no longer applies:
the algorithm may decide to enter a long branch with a small probability
supplied by a free private coin. If a coin were instead generated by
observing an additional hat, that observation would have to be charged
as a query. The theorem explicitly concerns the free-randomness model.
It proves sufficiency for every \(C>1\); it does not determine the
optimal randomized threshold.

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
Source~04 treats the other convention, a free \emph{public} coin with costs averaged over it, in Section~\ref{mbg:insp:sec:model}: mixing the optimal strategy with probability~$\epsilon$ and a zero-query strategy gives uniform expected cost at most $2\epsilon$ with divergence of probability at least~$\epsilon$, so its positive-probability lower bound fails in that model. The two observations are complementary; the sharp randomized thresholds are Research questions~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:questions}.2 and~\ref{mbg:insp:sec:questions}.6.
\end{writenote}

\section{Finite certificates, reproducibility, and formalization}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:verification}

\subsection{Executed checks}

The package contains two independent Python programs using only the
standard library. Their outputs use exact integers and rational
numbers for the mathematical checks. Assertions in these programs
are explicit runtime checks, rather than checks disabled by optimized
Python execution.

\paragraph{The block and query verifier.}
\nolinkurl{verify_hat_queries.py} executes the actual query algorithms
through a guarded oracle. It exhaustively checks every physical hat
assignment for \(m=1,\ldots,8\).

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l r}
\toprule
Quantity&Executed count\\
\midrule
Physical assignments&87,380\\
Primary player executions&1,339,848\\
Reexecutions after flipping the owner's hat&1,339,848\\
Queries in primary executions&2,009,628\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The verifier checks that no player queries its own hat or repeats a
query, and that both the guess and the complete query transcript are
unchanged when the owner's hat is flipped.
It compares the executable rule with an independently expressed
declarative rule, checks individual fairness and the complete
three-case block law, verifies every claimed prefix bound, and computes
the exact query histogram and geometric tail at each player position.
It also checks the integer stage accounting for \(\varepsilon=1\)
through stage \(80\). The result is
\nolinkurl{hat_query_verification.json}.

\paragraph{The orbit verifier.}
\nolinkurl{verify_orbit_bounds.py} exhausts all one-query profiles on
teams of size \(1,\ldots,5\). There are \(2n\) distinct rules for each
player: the two constants, and a signed copy of any of the other
\(n-1\) hats. Hence there are \((2n)^n\) profiles.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r r r c}
\toprule
\(n\)&Profiles&Profile/assignment pairs&Minimum \(\Prb(F\le0)\)\\
\midrule
1&2&4&\(1/2\)\\
2&16&64&\(1/2\)\\
3&216&1,728&\(1/4\)\\
4&4,096&65,536&\(1/2\)\\
5&100,000&3,200,000&\(1/4\)\\
\midrule
Total&104,330&3,267,332&\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

It also constructs the labels of Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:support} for nonlinear
fixed-visibility examples of bounds \(2\) and \(3\), and for a genuinely
adaptive depth-\(2\) multiplexer example. It checks exact Walsh
reconstruction, the support-incidence bound on every induced vertex
set, each nonzero support-label sum, and every actual score orbit.
These examples account for another \(5,120\) assignment/group pairs.
The result is \nolinkurl{orbit_verification.json}.

\subsection{What the computations establish}

Both programs passed. The finite enumeration is useful because an
own-coordinate dependency, a factor-of-two error in surplus, or a
prefix error can invalidate an otherwise plausible infinite proof.
The tests exercise exactly those risks. They do not prove the
all-parameter theorem, the probabilistic limit, the category statement,
or historical priority. Those mathematical claims stand on their
proofs in the article.

To reproduce the certificates and compile the article, run:
\begin{lstlisting}
python3 verify_hat_queries.py
python3 verify_orbit_bounds.py
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error hat_query_thresholds.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error hat_query_thresholds.tex
\end{lstlisting}
The main TeX source includes its bibliography and requires no downloaded
image assets or external bibliography database.

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
These commands and file names are the delivery's. In the collection the programs are shipped as \texttt{code/\allowbreak 05-hat-queries-\allowbreak verify\_\allowbreak hat\_\allowbreak queries.py} and \texttt{code/\allowbreak 05-hat-queries-\allowbreak verify\_\allowbreak orbit\_\allowbreak bounds.py}, their outputs as \texttt{data/\allowbreak 05-hat-queries-\allowbreak hat\_\allowbreak query\_\allowbreak verification.json} and \texttt{data/\allowbreak 05-hat-queries-\allowbreak orbit\_\allowbreak verification.json}, and the source notes as \texttt{05-hat-queries-\allowbreak SOURCE\_\allowbreak NOTES.txt}, all byte-identical to the delivery; the manuscript's source and PDF, the delivered \texttt{README.txt} and the checksum file are not shipped and survive in the archive of the arrival commit \texttt{afd7ffabb}. By default each program writes its report beside itself under the delivery name, so run in place it would add an unprefixed file to the report's \texttt{code/}; the report's \texttt{README.md} reruns them on a scratch copy, or with \texttt{--output} into a scratch directory. The counts above are those of the shipped JSON files, reproduced at the write (Section~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:provenance}).
\end{writenote}

\subsection{A practical route to proof-assistant verification}

The existing \repo\ list-coding project supplies finite-list validity,
decoding, and round-trip interfaces \cite{qry:ListCoding}. Those interfaces
can encode finite trees and certificates, but do not by themselves
validate a probabilistic or Fourier assertion. A suitable development
would separate the following obligations:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Query semantics and blindness.}
Represent a finite binary decision tree with a forbidden owner
coordinate; prove that evaluation and its transcript are invariant
under changing the owner hat. Prove the depth and path-probability
lemmas by induction.
\item \textbf{Finite score and block law.}
Use the integer score \(F=2D\) to avoid fractions in the executable
semantics. Formalize the three configurations of
Proposition~\ref{mbg:qry:prop:blocklaw}, then prove the exact query-tail
formula and the pair-prefix bound for arbitrary \(m\).
\item \textbf{Finite group certificates.}
A certificate gives a binary vector for each coordinate. A checker
verifies that every displayed score-support sum is nonzero. Its
soundness theorem should derive orbit cancellation and the finite
sign probability bound. The certificate is meaningful only after
the displayed Walsh representation is also verified.
\item \textbf{Infinite measure and category.}
Prove the finite-color theorem using the product-space group action,
then prove the cylinder-density step and the Baire intersection step.
Alternatively, formalize the conditional sign criterion.
\item \textbf{Scheduling and limits.}
Formalize disjoint blocks, independence, the two summability
calculations, the indicator-counting lemma, and the two-matchings
prefix transfer. Finally connect the deterministic schedules to
their asymptotic equivalents.
\end{enumerate}

The shortest complete mathematical milestone is the bounded-query
nullity theorem via finite colorings and cylinder density. The shortest
useful finite milestone is the exact one-query \(3/4\) theorem.
The expected-cost threshold then requires only the path-cost lemma
and the already isolated positive block construction. No claim is made
that these additional formalization steps have been completed.

\section{Further research questions}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:questions}

The following questions are proposed continuations. Except for the
explicitly cited starting point, they are questions formulated by this
article, not a claim that each is a recognized longstanding open problem.

\begin{question}[Optimal finite constants after one query]
Determine
\[
 \alpha_d=\inf_{\text{finite legal teams with depth at most }d}
             \Prb(F\le0)
\]
for \(d\ge2\), and its asymptotic behavior as \(d\to\infty\).
Is the factor \(d\) in the sparse-support bound necessary?
Does adaptive choice of queries improve the optimum over a
predetermined visible set at the same count?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[The exact randomized expected-cost threshold]
Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:randomcost} gives almost-sure divergence under every
uniform expected cap \(C>1\). Is \(1\) optimal when random bits are
free and private? Is the boundary value attained? Compare positive
probability with almost-sure divergence, and compare private coins,
shared coins, and randomness generated by charged hat queries.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Growth at the deterministic critical budget]
Under the sharp uniform cap \(\sup_i\E Q_i\le2\), how quickly can
\(D_n\) tend to infinity almost surely? Our explicit schedules give
sublogarithmic rates with sharp equivalents. Can one obtain
\(D_n\asymp\log n\), or faster, while retaining the geometric tail
\(\Prb(Q_i>t)\le2^{-t}\)?
The earlier unrestricted-growth theorem does not impose this
expected-cost constraint.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Count budgets with infinitely many zeros]
Classify arbitrary finite-valued \(q(i)\ge0\), dropping the eventual
lower bound \(1\) in Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:budgets}. How do the frequency and
placement of zero-query players interact with the capacity of the
high-budget players to compensate for their fluctuations?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[A structural visibility criterion]
Characterize directed visibility graphs that admit almost-sure
divergent positive surplus. Finite colorability of the undirected
visibility graph is an obstruction, and acyclicity of the directed
graph forces independent correctness. Which genuinely cyclic
structures suffice? Can the problem be reduced to disjoint finite
subgames with suitable gain and loss probabilities?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Distance budgets without convergence]
Theorems~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:local} and~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:separation} leave a gap between
\(h(i)\to\infty\) and mere unboundedness. Find a useful necessary and
sufficient condition on an arbitrary distance profile.
In particular, can one express it through the arrangement of
intervals \([i-h(i),i+h(i)]\), rather than through a difficult
optimization over strategies?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Sharp support-label certificates]
Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:support} uses a greedy upper bound on the dimension of
binary labels. Determine the smallest label space for the score
hypergraph of a given strategy. Which improvements survive
conditioning on arbitrary finite cylinders? An efficient algorithm
with a short certificate could improve finite bounds without
formalizing general hypercontractivity.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Spectral structure of critical-cost success]
How sharp is Proposition~\ref{mbg:qry:prop:spectral} for legal hat scores?
Compute the degree distribution of the efficient block construction
and identify exactly where its negative-debt variance resides.
Can aggregate Fourier information distinguish rates attainable with
the same query-tail bound?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[More colors and biased hats]
For \(q\) equally likely colors, a deterministic branch of length \(L\)
has probability \(q^{-L}\), so the corresponding path-cost scale is
\(q/(q-1)\). Does that scale govern any sharp expected-query
threshold for excess over \(n/q\), or do compulsory neutral
constructions require additional information?
For biased hats, first specify the correct benchmark; blindly
subtracting \(n/2\) no longer expresses individual fairness.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Average cost across the team]
The threshold \(2\) concerns the supremum over individual expected
costs. It is different from
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\E Q_i.
\]
Our active pairs already have mean cost below \(3/2\), and sparse
neutral padding changes the team average further. Determine the
sharp average-cost constraints compatible with almost-sure growth
and prescribed rates.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Fluctuations and inverse waiting times]
For the critical schedule, positive blocks are independent rare
events, whereas negative debt is finite almost surely but has
infinite expectation. Obtain fluctuation theorems around
\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:ratepower}--\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:ratelog}, and asymptotic laws for the
first index after which the excess permanently exceeds a given
level. Such laws should distinguish random gain fluctuations from
the heavy distribution of the last bad block.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Foundational and effective strength]
Locate the precise logical strength of the countable coloring,
cylinder-density, and category arguments. Does the bounded-depth
nullity theorem admit a particularly weak formalization when
strategies are given by computable trees? Can one obtain effective
null or meager covers uniformly from a bound on the trees?
A proof should distinguish a classical null set from an effectively
null set, and a finite certificate from its soundness theorem.
\end{question}

\begin{writenote}[Status of the questions, 4 October 2026, batch~92]
All twelve questions stay open as printed; the write searched no literature for them. Several coincide with source~04's questions of Section~\ref{mbg:insp:sec:questions}, as the note there lists. Two of source~04's results bear on them without answering them: its Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:fin-trace} gives finite teams of depth $k=2^{r-1}-1$ with $\Prb(F\le0)=2^{-r}$, so $\alpha_k\le1/(2(k+1))$ for those~$k$ in question~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:questions}.1, against the lower bounds of~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:adaptive}; and its Section~\ref{mbg:insp:con-section} computes the endpoint central limit theorem, the variance and the realized and maximal costs of the critical schedule, the first steps of questions~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:questions}.10--\ref{mbg:qry:sec:questions}.11.
\end{writenote}

\section{Conclusions and contribution ledger}
\label{mbg:qry:sec:conclusions}

The repository's bounded-inspection question has a negative answer
in a stronger form: under any fixed adaptive query bound, divergent
excess has probability zero and occurs on a meager set.
The exact qualitative classification for nonzero count budgets is
unboundedness, even when the high-budget players are arbitrarily sparse.
Distance budgets obey a different criterion, as witnessed by the
explicit three-colorable counterexample.

The central quantitative boundary is~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:threshold}. A uniform
deterministic expected cost strictly below two forces bounded depth.
At two, an efficient implementation of a known construction gives
almost-sure divergence with exponentially decaying query tails.
Free private randomization changes that optimization problem.
