\section{The question and the research intersection}
\label{mbg:insp:sec:intro}

The central conclusion is that \emph{two expected inspections per
player are exactly sufficient}, with the expectation interpreted
uniformly across players and the strategies required to be
deterministic functions of the hats. This is an exact threshold, not
an asymptotic order estimate. A stronger theorem identifies the entire
optimal family of increasing query costs with a geometric law.

The game is simple to state. Infinitely many players receive independent
fair black or white hats. Each player may inspect other hats, possibly
choosing the next inspection based on earlier observations. No player
may inspect their own hat or communicate after the hats are assigned.
Every player must guess. If \(S_n\) counts the correct guesses among the
first \(n\) players, individual fairness gives
\(\E S_n=n/2\). Nevertheless, a suitable strategy can make
\(S_n-n/2\) tend to \(+\infty\) on almost every hat assignment.
Eldredge proved this last assertion in Proposition~6.6 of
\cite{hat:Eldredge}.

This topic has a direct, documented connection to Elliot Glazer:
the paragraph preceding that proposition credits Glazer and Charles
Wang with suggesting the study of excess correct guesses.
Glazer's institutional biography also identifies set theory, formal
systems, choice paradoxes, and finite and infinite puzzles among his
interests \cite{insp:GlazerBio}. Lietz and Winkel's work gives another
explicit connection between a Glazer puzzle and finite-inspection
infinite hat games \cite{insp:LietzWinkel}. We rely on these accessible
sources; the LinkedIn page supplied with the request did not return
readable content during the source review.

The \repo\ connection is equally specific. Its earlier manuscript
\emph{No Universal Sublinear Bound for Measurable Hat Guessing}
asks in Research question~13.2 whether a uniform bound on the number
of inspected hats permits \(D_n\to+\infty\) almost surely
\cite{qry:PriorHat}. The present article answers that question negatively,
and strengthens the conclusion to probability zero. The repository's
older measurable-box-games report separately asks about finite teamwork
and the costs of adaptive observations \cite{qry:RepoBoxes}; our finite
optimality results address a precise family within that program.

\subsection{What the preceding work already established}

The predecessor already supplies strategies with logarithmic excess and
strategies exceeding any prescribed positive sublinear scale, using
increasingly large syndrome blocks. Those growth conclusions are not
claimed as new here. Eldredge's rare all-black blocks already establish
the underlying possibility of almost-sure divergent excess.
The improvement here is to identify the optimal \emph{cost of reading
the hats}, including the distinction between a maximum depth and
an expectation. The lower bound requires a uniform sign estimate for
Boolean polynomials, followed by a conditional argument that excludes
positive-probability divergence.

Classical hypercontractivity supplies the analytic estimate
\cite{insp:AustrinHastad,insp:AustrinHastadSTOC}. We include a direct proof of a
sufficient version, so the main argument can be read without a
separate reference on Boolean analysis. The finite-field construction
uses standard binary simplex geometry. Once the adaptive-tree argument
has forced affinity, the classification of its constant-weight dual
is a classical coding theorem of Bonisoli \cite{insp:Bonisoli}, whose
binary case is also proved directly below.

\section{The model and the main statements}
\label{mbg:insp:sec:model}

\subsection{Hats, guesses, and decision trees}

Work on the product probability space
\(\Omega=\{0,1\}^{\N_{>0}}\) with independent fair coordinates
\(x_i\). A deterministic adaptive strategy for player \(i\) is a binary
query tree. Each internal node names a coordinate \(j\ne i\), the
observed value selects the next branch, and a leaf returns the guessed
bit. The tree may refer to arbitrarily distant coordinates.
All trees are fixed before the hats are assigned.

Let \(Q_i(x)\) be the number of queries on input \(x\). We permit
almost-surely terminating trees when stating lower bounds. The
construction proving sharpness uses finite trees and terminates on
every input. Repeated inspections of a previously read coordinate
can always be removed by retaining its answer; a \emph{reduced query
implementation} below makes no such repeated inspections. Unnecessary
queries can only increase the costs being minimized.

Write \(\widehat x_i\) for player \(i\)'s output and set
\[
 S_n=\sum_{i=1}^n\indic{\widehat x_i=x_i},
 \qquad
 D_n=S_n-\frac n2,
 \qquad
 T_n=2D_n.
\]
For Boolean Fourier arguments it is convenient to use the signs
\(\varepsilon_i=(-1)^{x_i}\) and
\(g_i=(-1)^{\widehat x_i}\). Then
\[
 Y_i=\varepsilon_i g_i
 =2\indic{\widehat x_i=x_i}-1,
 \qquad
 T_n=\sum_{i=1}^nY_i.
\]
The guess \(g_i\) is independent of the coordinate \(\varepsilon_i\).
Thus \(\E Y_i=0\), \(\E T_n=0\), and \(\E S_n=n/2\).
No independence between different \(Y_i\)'s is asserted.

The \emph{reachable depth} \(d_i\) is the supremum of the lengths of
consistent query paths. A uniform depth bound means that one finite
integer \(k\) satisfies \(d_i\le k\) for every player. A uniform expected
cost bound is a bound on \(\sup_i\E Q_i\). These are different
requirements.

\subsection{A complete frontier, stated before its proof}

Let \(G\) be geometric with success probability \(1/2\), on the positive
integers:
\[
 \Prb(G=r)=2^{-r}\quad(r\ge1),
 \qquad \Prb(G>r)=2^{-r}\quad(r\ge0).
\]
For a nonnegative nondecreasing function
\(\phi:\N\to[0,\infty]\), define the uniform cost
\[
 C_\phi(\mathcal A)=\sup_{i\ge1}\E\phi(Q_i).
\]
Here \(\N\) includes \(0\) when it is the domain of a cost function.

\begin{theorem}[Exact geometric frontier]\label{mbg:insp:thm:main-frontier}
In the deterministic adaptive binary hat model:
\begin{enumerate}
\item If the reachable query depths are uniformly bounded, then
\(\Prb(D_n\to+\infty)=0\).
\item Every strategy \(\mathcal A\) with
\(\Prb(D_n\to+\infty)>0\) satisfies
\[
 C_\phi(\mathcal A)\ge\E\phi(G)
 \quad\hbox{for every nonnegative nondecreasing }\phi.
\]
\item There exists one strategy \(\mathcal A_*\), using only finite
trees, for which \(D_n\to+\infty\) almost surely and
\[
 C_\phi(\mathcal A_*)=\E\phi(G)
 \quad\hbox{for all these }\phi\hbox{ simultaneously}.
\]
The same strategy satisfies \(S_n/n\to1/2\) almost surely.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

The lower bound is proved in the sections on the conditional
obstruction and decision-tree costs. The upper bound is the explicit
early-stopping implementation in the construction section. Allowing
infinite values of \(\E\phi(G)\) makes the theorem include genuinely
impossible uniform costs.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Uniform cost & Exact optimum for divergent excess & Attainment\\
\midrule
\(\sup_i\E Q_i\) & \(2\) & \(\mathcal A_*\)\\
\(\sup_i\E Q_i^2\) & \(6\) & \(\mathcal A_*\)\\
\(\sup_i\E Q_i^3\) & \(26\) & \(\mathcal A_*\)\\
\(\sup_i\Prb(Q_i>r)\), \(r\ge0\) & \(2^{-r}\) & \(\mathcal A_*\)\\
\(\sup_i\E z^{Q_i}\), \(1\le z<2\) &
\(z/(2-z)\) & \(\mathcal A_*\)\\
\(\sup_i\E 2^{Q_i}\) & \(+\infty\) & No finite bound possible\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The critical mean assertion is
\(\sup_i\E Q_i=2\). In the realizing strategy, every individual
expectation can be strictly below \(2\). What is impossible is a
\emph{common gap}: \(\sup_i\E Q_i\le2-\epsilon\) for some
\(\epsilon>0\).

\begin{theorem}[Two further quantitative conclusions]\label{mbg:insp:thm:main-other}
For any almost-surely terminating strategy with finite expected query
costs,
\[
 \Var(T_n)\le n+\sum_{i=1}^n\E Q_i.
\]
The constant is sharp. For a finite team of \(N\) players attaining
\[
 \Prb(S_N\ge s)=\frac{N}{2s},
 \qquad s>N/2,
\]
every uniform worst-case query bound is at least \(s-1\).
Such a finite optimum can be attained at depth \(s-1\) if and only if
\[
 N=m(2^r-1),\qquad s=m2^{r-1}
 \quad\text{for some integers }m,r\ge1.
\]
Every minimum-depth extremizer has an affine failure set whose
parity-check matrix contains each nonzero binary column exactly \(m\)
times. An explicit nonadaptive trace strategy realizes every allowed
parameter pair.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Two distinctions that the hypotheses must preserve}

First, divergence is stronger than eventual positivity. A bounded-depth
strategy can have positive probability that \(D_n>0\) eventually while
the excess stays bounded; Remark~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-eventual-majority} gives an
explicit example. The theorem concerns unbounded persistent growth.

Second, the source of randomness matters for query cost. The theorem
counts randomness obtained by inspecting hats. If a free public coin
is allowed and its cost is ignored, run \(\mathcal A_*\) with public
probability \(\epsilon\), and otherwise use a zero-query strategy.
The uniform expected cost is at most \(2\epsilon\), while divergent
excess has probability at least \(\epsilon\). Thus the positive-
probability lower bound is false for this different cost convention.
A theorem with external randomization requires a budget conditional
on the random seed or a separate analysis.

The order in which inspections are performed is local to each player;
it does not give players access to other players' guesses or transcripts.
None of the proofs uses post-assignment communication.

Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:main-frontier}(1) is the probability part of source~06's Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:bounded} (proved here as Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-no-divergence}); its part~(2), for every nondecreasing cost, is this source's alone (Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-frontier}); its part~(3) is Propositions~\ref{mbg:insp:con-query-distribution} and Theorems~\ref{mbg:insp:con-rate-theorem}--\ref{mbg:insp:var-strong-law}; its mean case is source~06's Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:threshold}. Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:main-other} is this source's alone (Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:var-weighted} and Section~\ref{mbg:insp:sec:fin}). The public-coin remark above and source~06's Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:randomcost} on private coins are complementary (the note after it).
\end{writenote}


\section{Bounded inspection cannot produce a divergent surplus}
\label{mbg:insp:obs-section}

Throughout this section the hats $(\varepsilon_i)_{i\in\N}$ are independent
and uniform on $\{-1,1\}$.  A legal guess $g_i\in\{-1,1\}$ is a measurable
function of the hats other than $\varepsilon_i$.  Write
\[
 Y_i=\varepsilon_i g_i,
 \qquad T_n=\sum_{i=1}^n Y_i=2S_n-n,
\]
where $S_n$ counts the correct guesses.  Thus $\E Y_i=0$, although the
variables $Y_i$ need not be independent or form martingale differences.
An inspection algorithm is a deterministic binary decision tree: the
next inspected coordinate and the eventual guess depend on the answers
already received.  It never inspects its own hat.  We count individual
hat inspections, and denote this count by $Q_i$.  Unreachable branches
can be deleted and repeated inspections can be removed without changing
the guess or increasing its cost.  These conventions also apply to
trees which terminate almost surely rather than on every input.

\subsection{A dimension-free polynomial lemma}

For a finite set of coordinates, write
$\chi_A(\varepsilon)=\prod_{j\in A}\varepsilon_j$ for the Walsh characters.
The Walsh degree of $f=\sum_A\widehat f(A)\chi_A$ is the largest $|A|$
for which $\widehat f(A)\ne0$.  A depth-$k$ decision tree computes a
function of Walsh degree at most $k$: each leaf is specified by at most
$k$ answers, and its indicator is a product of at most $k$ factors
$(1\pm\varepsilon_j)/2$.  Consequently, if every player uses depth at
most $k$, then $T_n$ has degree at most $k+1$.  It depends on finitely
many coordinates even when inspected indices are chosen adaptively:
each of the first $n$ trees has finitely many nodes.

We give the elementary hypercontractive estimate needed below.  This
also isolates precisely which standard analytic ingredient enters the
argument; see the hypercontractivity discussion in
\cite{insp:AustrinHastad,insp:AustrinHastadSTOC}.

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
The degree bound above is source~06's Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:degree}, and Lemmas~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-hypercontractivity}--\ref{mbg:insp:obs-sign} state the estimates proved inside source~06's Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:sign} (inequality~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:bonami} and its last step). They are printed here because this source's later proofs cite them. The proofs are source~04's own: the induction runs for the noise operator $\mathcal T_{1/\sqrt3}$ rather than for the weighted coefficients of~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:bonami}, an equivalent form of the same Bonami induction; the second paragraph of the proof of Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-sign} derives the sharper constant, which source~06 cites.
\end{writenote}

\begin{lemma}[A fourth-moment bound]
\label{mbg:insp:obs-hypercontractivity}
If $f$ is a real Walsh polynomial of degree at most $d$, then
\[
 \norm{f}_4\le 3^{d/2}\norm{f}_2,
 \qquad
 \norm{f}_1\ge 3^{-d}\norm{f}_2.
\]
Both estimates are independent of the number of coordinates.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal T_\rho$ multiply the Walsh coefficient of $\chi_A$ by
$\rho^{|A|}$.  We first prove
\[
 \norm{\mathcal T_{1/\sqrt3}h}_4\le\norm{h}_2
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:obs-hypercontractivity}a}
\]
by induction on the number of coordinates.  Decompose
$h(x',x_N)=a(x')+x_Nb(x')$ and put
$A=\mathcal T_{1/\sqrt3}a$, $B=\mathcal T_{1/\sqrt3}b$.
Averaging first over $x_N$ gives
\[
 \E(\mathcal T_{1/\sqrt3}h)^4
 =\E\left(A^4+2A^2B^2+\frac19B^4\right).
\]
Cauchy--Schwarz and the induction hypothesis bound the right side by
\[
 \norm{a}_2^4+2\norm{a}_2^2\norm{b}_2^2
       +\frac19\norm{b}_2^4
 \le (\norm{a}_2^2+\norm{b}_2^2)^2
 =\norm{h}_2^4.
\]
The zero-coordinate case is immediate, proving the induction.
Now apply this result to
$h=\sum_A3^{|A|/2}\widehat f(A)\chi_A$.
Then $\mathcal T_{1/\sqrt3}h=f$, and Parseval gives
$\norm{h}_2\le3^{d/2}\norm{f}_2$.
Finally, H\"older interpolation gives
$\norm{f}_2\le\norm{f}_1^{1/3}\norm{f}_4^{2/3}$,
which yields the asserted first-moment lower bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Both signs have positive probability]
\label{mbg:insp:obs-sign}
Let $P\not\equiv0$ be a real Walsh polynomial of degree at most $d$
with $\E P=0$.  Then
\[
 \min\{\Prb(P<0),\Prb(P>0)\}
 \ge \frac{1}{4\cdot9^d}.
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:obs-sign}a}
\]
The standard general hypercontractive inequality improves the right
side to $e^{-2d}/4$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Set $P_+=\max(P,0)$ and $P_-=\max(-P,0)$.
The zero mean gives
$\E P_+=\E P_-=\norm{P}_1/2$.
Cauchy--Schwarz therefore gives, for either sign,
\[
 \frac14\norm{P}_1^2
 \le \norm{P}_2^2\,\Prb(P>0),
 \qquad
 \frac14\norm{P}_1^2
 \le \norm{P}_2^2\,\Prb(P<0).
\]
Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-hypercontractivity} proves the displayed bound.

For completeness, the sharper constant follows from the usual
degree-$d$ hypercontractive estimate
$\norm{P}_p\le(p-1)^{d/2}\norm{P}_2$ for $p>2$.
Interpolating between $L^1$ and $L^p$ gives
\[
 \norm{P}_1
 \ge (p-1)^{-dp/(2(p-2))}\norm{P}_2.
\]
Letting $p\downarrow2$ yields
$\norm{P}_1\ge e^{-d}\norm{P}_2$, and the preceding
Cauchy--Schwarz argument applies.  This sign-balance argument is
standard; see Austrin--H\aa stad
\cite[Theorem~2.6 and Corollary~4.2]{insp:AustrinHastadSTOC}
for this Boolean estimate and its applications.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The conditional obstruction}

The preceding lemma immediately gives a uniform upper bound below one
on $\Prb(T_n>0)$.  That observation alone rules out almost-sure
divergence, but does not rule out divergence on an event of intermediate
probability.  Conditioning on finitely many hats closes this gap.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform bounded depth]
\label{mbg:insp:obs-no-divergence}
Suppose that every player uses a deterministic legal decision tree
of depth at most the same finite integer $k$.  Then
\[
 \Prb(T_n\longrightarrow+\infty)
 =\Prb(T_n\longrightarrow-\infty)=0.
\]
No bound on the number of players who inspect a given hat is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
This is the probability part of source~06's Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:bounded}, there proved by finite colourings (with category as well). The proof below is source~04's, a second route: the analytic one that source~06 sketches after Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:finite-analytic}, with source~06's Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:density} replaced by the martingale convergence theorem.
\end{writenote}

\begin{proof}[Second proof (source~04)]
Let $c_k=1/(4\cdot9^{k+1})$ and fix a cylinder event $C$ specifying
the values of a finite set $F$ of hats.  Put $m=|F|$.  On this
conditional probability space, split
\[
 T_n=U_n+V_n,
 \quad U_n=\sum_{\substack{i\le n\\i\notin F}}\varepsilon_i g_i,
 \quad V_n=\sum_{\substack{i\le n\\i\in F}}\varepsilon_i g_i.
\]
For $i\notin F$, its own hat is still an independent fair bit and the
guess still does not depend on it.  Hence $\E(U_n\mid C)=0$.
Fixing coordinates does not increase Walsh degree, so $U_n$ has
conditional degree at most $k+1$.  Also $|V_n|\le m$.
If $U_n$ vanishes identically, its probability of being nonpositive is
one; otherwise Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-sign} applies.  In either case,
\[
 \Prb(T_n\le m\mid C)
 \ge\Prb(U_n\le0\mid C)\ge c_k.
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:obs-no-divergence}a}
\]
It follows that
$\Prb(T_n\le m\ \text{infinitely often}\mid C)\ge c_k$:
the probability of a limsup of events is at least the limsup of their
probabilities.  For $A=\{T_n\to+\infty\}$ we obtain
\[
 \Prb(A\mid C)\le1-c_k
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:obs-no-divergence}b}
\]
for every finite cylinder $C$.

Let $\mathcal F_m=\sigma(\varepsilon_1,\ldots,\varepsilon_m)$.
The last bound holds on every atom of $\mathcal F_m$, so
$\E(\indic{A}\mid\mathcal F_m)\le1-c_k$ almost surely for every $m$.
The upward martingale convergence theorem gives
$\E(\indic{A}\mid\mathcal F_m)\to\indic{A}$ almost surely.
Thus $\indic{A}\le1-c_k$ almost surely and $\Prb(A)=0$.
Applying the same argument to the legal guesses $-g_i$ proves the
claim about negative divergence.  The sharper constant in
Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-sign} can replace $c_k$ throughout.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Divergence is stronger than an eventual strict majority]
\label{mbg:insp:obs-eventual-majority}
It would be false to replace divergence by eventual positivity in
Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-no-divergence}.  Let the first two players guess $+1$.
Partition the remaining players into consecutive pairs $(a,b)$, with
$g_a=\varepsilon_b$ and $g_b=-\varepsilon_a$.  Each pair has total
score zero.  On the probability-$1/4$ event
$\varepsilon_1=\varepsilon_2=+1$, every subsequent prefix has score
$1$, $2$, or $3$.  This depth-one strategy has an eventual strict
majority with positive probability, but its surplus stays bounded.
The conditional proof above avoids any unsupported assertion that
eventual positivity is a tail event.
\end{remark}

\section{The geometric lower frontier for inspection costs}
\label{mbg:insp:cost-section}

The obstruction has an exact consequence for inspection complexity.
Let $G$ be a geometric random variable with success probability $1/2$,
supported on the positive integers:
\[
 \Prb(G=s)=2^{-s}\quad(s\ge1),
 \qquad \Prb(G>r)=2^{-r}\quad(r\ge0).
\]
We first record the cost of merely having a long reachable path.

\begin{lemma}[A path forces geometric cost]
\label{mbg:insp:cost-path}
If a deterministic decision tree has a reachable path of length $d$,
then its inspection count $Q$ stochastically dominates $\min(G,d)$.
In particular,
\[
 \Prb(Q>r)\ge2^{-r}\quad(0\le r<d),
 \qquad \E Q\ge2-2^{1-d}.
\]
For $d=0$ the second inequality reads $\E Q\ge0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
This is source~06's Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:pathcost} (with $d$ for $L$), stated as a stochastic domination. Source~04's proof is source~06's argument: following the first $r<d$ answers of the path has probability at least $2^{-r}$ and forces $Q>r$, which is the domination; summing the tails gives the mean bound; repeated queries only raise the probability of a consistent prefix; and every finite prefix of a reachable infinite branch has positive probability, so a tree of unbounded depth has $\E Q\ge2$. It is not printed a second time; the proof of Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:pathcost} serves.
\end{writenote}

\begin{theorem}[Universal monotone-cost lower bound]
\label{mbg:insp:cost-frontier}
Consider a deterministic legal strategy for which
$\Prb(T_n\to+\infty)>0$.  For every nonnegative nondecreasing function
$\varphi:\{0,1,2,\ldots\}\to[0,\infty]$,
\[
 \sup_i\E\varphi(Q_i)\ge\E\varphi(G).
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:cost-frontier}a}
\]
In particular, for each integer $r\ge0$,
\[
 \sup_i\Prb(Q_i>r)\ge2^{-r}.
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:cost-frontier}b}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-no-divergence} implies that the reachable depths of
the players' trees cannot have a common finite bound.  Consequently,
for every $d$ there is a player with a reachable path of length at least
$d$.  Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-path} gives, for that player,
$\E\varphi(Q_i)\ge\E\varphi(\min(G,d))$.
Taking the supremum over players and then letting $d\to\infty$ proves
the first assertion by monotone convergence.  Alternatively, applying
the same lemma to a path longer than $r$ proves the tail assertion
directly.  It matters that one sufficiently deep player satisfies all
the first $d$ tail bounds simultaneously; no interchange of a supremum
and an infinite sum is being assumed.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A uniform gap below two is impossible]
\label{mbg:insp:cost-mean}
If $\sup_i\E Q_i\le C<2$, then
$\Prb(T_n\to+\infty)=0$.  More precisely, every player's reachable
depth is at most
\[
 \left\lfloor1-\log_2(2-C)\right\rfloor.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-path} bounds any reachable depth $d$ by
$2-2^{1-d}\le C$, which rearranges to the displayed inequality.
Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-no-divergence} then applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Pointwise strictness does not give a uniform gap]
\label{mbg:insp:cost-strictness}
The hypothesis is a common bound $C<2$.  The collection of individual
inequalities $\E Q_i<2$ does not imply it: the expectations may
approach two as the players' finite worst-case depths grow.  The
positive construction in this article exploits exactly this
distinction.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Moments, generating functions, and tail envelopes]
\label{mbg:insp:cost-moments}
Every strategy satisfying the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-frontier} obeys
\[
 \sup_i\E Q_i\ge2,
 \qquad \sup_i\E Q_i^2\ge6,
 \qquad
 \sup_i\E Q_i^s\ge\sum_{j\ge1}j^s2^{-j}\quad(s>0).
\]
For $1\le z<2$ it obeys
\[
 \sup_i\E z^{Q_i}\ge\frac{z}{2-z},
\]
whereas $\sup_i\E z^{Q_i}=\infty$ for every $z\ge2$.
Moreover, a common tail bound
$\Prb(Q_i>r)\le b_r$ rules out positive-probability divergence if
$b_r<2^{-r}$ at even one integer $r\ge0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-frontier} with $\varphi(q)=q^s$ and
$\varphi(q)=z^q$.  The geometric series gives
$\E z^G=\sum_{j\ge1}(z/2)^j=z/(2-z)$ for $z<2$; its divergence
for $z\ge2$ proves the corresponding assertion.  Differentiation at
$z=1$, or direct summation, gives $\E G=2$ and $\E G^2=6$.
Finally, if $b_r<2^{-r}$, no player can have a reachable path longer
than $r$, by Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-path}.  Uniform bounded depth applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[How the positive construction completes the frontier]
\label{mbg:insp:cost-attainment}
A successful construction for which
$\Prb(Q_i>r)\le2^{-r}$ for every $i,r$ has $Q_i$ stochastically
dominated by $G$.  Combined with Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:cost-frontier}, this
proves equality in every worst-player cost bound above, simultaneously
for every nonnegative nondecreasing $\varphi$.  Thus the constructive
upper bound establishes an exact stochastic frontier, including the
mean and all increasing moment costs.  The lower bound concerns
deterministic inspection algorithms.  Free external random bits can
select a large decision tree with a small probability without paying
for the binary answers in its selection; the path-probability proof
does not apply to that different cost model.
\end{remark}


\section{An optimal inspection implementation of Eldredge's blocks}
\label{mbg:insp:con-section}

The upper construction uses the finite block strategy in
\cite[Proposition~6.6]{hat:Eldredge}.  Its probabilistic mechanism, including the
existence of almost surely divergent excess, is due to Eldredge.  The point
here is to implement that strategy by short adaptive decision trees, compute
their full cost distribution, and give a schedule with exact asymptotics.
All decisions below are deterministic functions of observed hats.  No
external random bits, communication, or shared inspection transcript are
available.  Different players who inspect the same hat each pay for their
own inspection.

\subsection{The finite block and its early stopping algorithm}

Use $0$ for black and $1$ for white.  A block of $2k$ consecutive players is
divided into adjacent ordered pairs.  The first member of each pair is an
S player and the second a T player.  Ordinarily S copies the partner's color
and T guesses its opposite.  This gives exactly one correct guess in every
pair, for every hat assignment.

Eldredge's exception instructs any player who sees only black hats elsewhere
in the block to guess white.  The following implementation computes exactly
the resulting guesses.

\begin{enumerate}
\item A T player inspects the partner and guesses the opposite color.  No
further inspection is needed: if the exception applies, the partner is
black and the prescribed guess is already white.
\item An S player first inspects the partner.  If the partner is white,
the player guesses white immediately.  If the partner is black, the player
inspects the remaining $2k-2$ hats in a fixed order.  At the first white hat,
the player stops and guesses black.  If every inspected hat is black, the
player guesses white.
\end{enumerate}

In particular, every player halts on every assignment, and no player
inspects their own hat.  The ordering of the remaining hats can be increasing
player index; it has no probabilistic significance.

\begin{lemma}[Exact block score]\label{mbg:insp:con-block-score}
Let $Z$ be the number of correct guesses in this block minus $k$.  Then
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{configuration}&Z&\text{probability}\\ \hline
\text{all hats black}&-k&4^{-k}\\
\text{exactly one white hat, belonging to S}&1&k4^{-k}\\
\text{all other configurations}&0&1-(k+1)4^{-k}.
\end{array}
\]
In a block of the second or third kind, the centered score is nondecreasing
at pair boundaries.  In a block of the first kind, its values at successive
pair boundaries are $-1,-2,\ldots,-k$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
This is source~06's Proposition~\ref{mbg:qry:prop:blocklaw} for $m=k$ pairs, and source~04's proof is that proposition's case analysis: all black, every pair wrong twice; a single white hat on an S player, its pair right twice; a single white hat on a T player, or two or more white hats, every pair right once; one all-black and $k$ favourable configurations among the $4^k$. The case analysis also gives the assertions about pair boundaries, including the values $-1,\ldots,-k$ in an all-black block, where every pair contributes~$-1$. It is not printed a second time.
\end{writenote}

The case distinction is useful because it controls every prefix of the
block, not only its total.  Passing from an even prefix to the following odd
prefix changes the centered score by exactly $1/2$ or $-1/2$.

\begin{proposition}[The full inspection distribution]
\label{mbg:insp:con-query-distribution}
For a T player, $Q=1$.  For an S player in a block of size $2k$,
\[
 Q\ \stackrel{\mathrm{law}}{=}\ \min\{G,2k-1\},
 \qquad \Prb(G=j)=2^{-j}\quad(j=1,2,\ldots).
\]
Consequently, for integer $r\geq0$,
\[
 \Prb(Q>r)=
 \begin{cases}
 2^{-r},&0\leq r<2k-1,\\
 0,&r\geq2k-1,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad
 \E Q=2-4^{1-k}.
\]
For a family containing arbitrarily large blocks and every nonnegative
nondecreasing function $\phi:\{0,1,\ldots\}\to[0,\infty)$,
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-cost-envelope}
 \sup_i\E\phi(Q_i)=\E\phi(G),
\end{equation}
where either side may be infinite.  In particular,
\begin{align}
 \sup_i\E Q_i&=2, &
 \sup_i\Prb(Q_i>r)&=2^{-r},\label{mbg:insp:con-mean-tail}\\
 \sup_i\E Q_i^a&=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}j^a2^{-j}
       &&(a>0),\label{mbg:insp:con-moments}\\
 \sup_i\E e^{\theta Q_i}&=
 \begin{cases}
 \dfrac{e^\theta}{2-e^\theta},&0\leq\theta<\log2,\\[4pt]
 +\infty,&\theta\geq\log2.
 \end{cases}\label{mbg:insp:con-mgf}
\end{align}
Every individual mean is strictly less than $2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For S, the inspected hats are distinct independent fair bits, listed with
the partner first.  The procedure stops at the first white hat, or after
all $2k-1$ available hats have been inspected.  This gives the stated law.
The tail-sum formula gives
\[
 \E Q=\sum_{r=0}^{2k-2}2^{-r}=2-2^{-(2k-2)}.
\]
The variables $\min\{G,2k-1\}$ increase to $G$.  Apply monotone convergence
to $\phi$; T's constant cost $1$ cannot increase the supremum.  The remaining
identities follow by choosing the indicated cost functions.  For the
exponential moment, the series is geometric with ratio $e^\theta/2$.
\end{proof}

Thus the same implementation realizes the entire geometric cost envelope,
including second moment $6$ and every finite higher moment.  Combined with
the lower cost bound proved earlier, equation~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-cost-envelope}
attains all of those lower bounds simultaneously.  This is stronger than
merely giving an implementation of mean cost at most two.

\subsection{A deterministic schedule and exact excess rates}

Fix $1<p\leq2$.  For each $k=2,3,\ldots$, stage $k$ consists of
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-schedule}
 R_k=\left\lfloor\frac{4^k}{k^p}\right\rfloor
\end{equation}
independent consecutive blocks, each of size $2k$.  Let
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-endpoints}
 N_K=2\sum_{k=2}^{K}kR_k,
 \qquad
 \mu_K=\sum_{k=2}^{K}kR_k4^{-k}.
\end{equation}
Here independence refers to the independent hat coordinates of disjoint
blocks, not to any extra randomization of the strategy.

\begin{theorem}[Exact rates at every prefix]\label{mbg:insp:con-rate-theorem}
For the schedule~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-schedule}, almost surely
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-endpoint-rate}
 D_{N_K}\sim\mu_K.
\end{equation}
More precisely, the following limits hold over all integer prefixes $n$:
\begin{align}
 \frac{D_n}{(\log_4 n)^{2-p}/(2-p)}&\longrightarrow1,
       &&1<p<2,\label{mbg:insp:con-all-prefix-power}\\
 \frac{D_n}{\log\log n}&\longrightarrow1,
       &&p=2.\label{mbg:insp:con-all-prefix-loglog}
\end{align}
Unsubscripted logarithms are natural.  In both cases,
$D_n\to+\infty$ and $S_n/n\to1/2$ almost surely.
\end{theorem}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
This is source~06's Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:construction} with $p=1+\varepsilon$, for the schedule with $\lfloor4^k/k^p\rfloor$ instead of $\lceil4^m/m^{1+\varepsilon}\rceil$ blocks in stage~$k$; rounding does not affect the rates. Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:con-bernoulli-slln} is source~06's Lemma~\ref{mbg:qry:lem:counting}, proved there by Chebyshev's inequality along a subsequence, here by Kronecker's lemma. The proof below is source~04's second route; its notation ($H_K$, $L_K$, $\mu_K$, $N_K$) and equations are used by the rest of this section.
\end{writenote}

We give the details, including the passage from endpoints to arbitrary
prefixes.  The distinction matters because eventual growth at a selected
subsequence alone would not prove eventual excess at every time.

\begin{lemma}[A strong law for success counts]\label{mbg:insp:con-bernoulli-slln}
Let $I_1,I_2,\ldots$ be independent Bernoulli variables with means
$a_b$, and put $A_B=\sum_{b\leq B}a_b$.  If $A_B\to\infty$, then
\[
 \frac{\sum_{b\leq B} I_b}{A_B}\longrightarrow1
 \quad\text{almost surely}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Discard a finite initial segment so that $A_{b-1}>0$.  Since
$\Var(I_b)\leq a_b=A_b-A_{b-1}$,
\[
 \sum_b\frac{\Var(I_b)}{A_b^2}
 \leq\sum_b\int_{A_{b-1}}^{A_b}\frac{du}{u^2}<\infty.
\]
The convergence theorem for independent centered series shows that
$\sum_b(I_b-a_b)/A_b$ converges almost surely.  Kronecker's lemma then gives
$\sum_{b\leq B}(I_b-a_b)/A_B\to0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:con-rate-theorem}]
Call a block bad, good, or neutral according to
Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:con-block-score}.  The expected total number of bad blocks is
\[
 \sum_{k=2}^{\infty}R_k4^{-k}
 \leq\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}k^{-p}<\infty.
\]
By the first Borel--Cantelli lemma, only finitely many bad blocks occur
almost surely.  If $B_{k,r}$ is the indicator that block $r$ of stage $k$ is
bad, their total weighted loss therefore satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-finite-loss}
 L_\infty:=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{R_k}kB_{k,r}<\infty
 \quad\text{almost surely}.
\end{equation}

Let $H_K$ count good blocks through stage $K$, and let $L_K$ be the same
partial sum as in~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-finite-loss}.  Good-block indicators are
independent across blocks and have mean $k4^{-k}$ in stage $k$.  Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-mu-asymptotics}
 \mu_K=\sum_{k=2}^{K}k^{1-p}+O(1)
 \sim
 \begin{cases}
 K^{2-p}/(2-p),&1<p<2,\\
 \log K,&p=2.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
The bounded error follows from the summability of $k4^{-k}$ and the error
of at most one introduced by each floor.  In particular $\mu_K\to\infty$.
Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:con-bernoulli-slln} yields $H_K/\mu_K\to1$ almost surely.
At stage endpoints the score identity is exact:
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-score-decomposition}
 D_{N_K}=H_K-L_K.
\end{equation}
Since $L_K\to L_\infty<\infty$ almost surely, this proves
\eqref{mbg:insp:con-endpoint-rate}.

To relate stages to player indices, the geometrically weighted sum gives
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-size-asymptotics}
 N_K\sim\frac{8}{3}\,4^K K^{1-p}.
\end{equation}
For completeness, the floor errors in $N_K$ are $O(K^2)$.  In the main sum,
write $k=K-j$ and divide by $4^K K^{1-p}$.  The terms with fixed $j$ converge
to $4^{-j}$, and their sum tends to $\sum_{j\geq0}4^{-j}=4/3$;
the geometrically decaying remainder is negligible.  The factor two in
$N_K$ yields~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-size-asymptotics}.  Hence
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-stage-log}
 K\sim\log_4 n
 \quad\text{uniformly for }N_{K-1}\leq n\leq N_K.
\end{equation}

On the probability-one event already obtained, there is a last bad block.
After it, $D_n$ is nondecreasing at even indices by
Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:con-block-score}.  Thus, for all sufficiently large $K$ and
$N_{K-1}\leq n\leq N_K$,
\[
 D_{N_{K-1}}-\frac12\leq D_n\leq D_{N_K}+\frac12.
\]
Also $\mu_{K-1}/\mu_K\to1$.  Squeezing between these endpoints and using
\eqref{mbg:insp:con-mu-asymptotics}--\eqref{mbg:insp:con-stage-log} proves
\eqref{mbg:insp:con-all-prefix-power}.  When $p=2$, one uses
$\log K=\log\log n-\log\log4+o(1)$, which gives
\eqref{mbg:insp:con-all-prefix-loglog}.  Finally these normalizing functions are
$o(n)$, so $S_n/n=1/2+D_n/n\to1/2$.
\end{proof}

The rates in this theorem are exact equivalents, with specified leading
constants.  The earlier repository report discussed in the introduction
already addresses the unrestricted growth questions raised in
\cite[Remark~6.8]{hat:Eldredge}.  The refinements here concern inspection costs
and exact asymptotics of this particular block family.

\subsection{Normal fluctuations and rare losses}

\begin{proposition}[Central limit theorem at stage endpoints]
\label{mbg:insp:con-clt}
For $1<p\leq2$,
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-clt-formula}
 \frac{D_{N_K}-\mu_K}{\sqrt{\mu_K}}
 \ \xrightarrow{\mathrm{law}}\ \mathcal N(0,1).
\end{equation}
Nevertheless,
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-actual-variance}
 \E D_{N_K}=0,
 \qquad
 \Var(D_{N_K})
 =\sum_{k=2}^{K}k(k+1)R_k4^{-k}
 \sim\frac{K^{3-p}}{3-p}.
\end{equation}
Thus the normal limit describes typical fluctuations without asserting
convergence of moments.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The variance of $H_K$ is
\[
 v_K=\sum_{k=2}^{K}R_k(k4^{-k})(1-k4^{-k})
     =\mu_K-O(1),
\]
because
\[
 \sum_{k=2}^{\infty}R_k k^2 16^{-k}
 \leq\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}k^{2-p}4^{-k}<\infty.
\]
The centered Bernoulli summands have absolute value at most one, while
$v_K\to\infty$.  The Lindeberg condition is therefore eventually empty for
each fixed positive truncation parameter.  The Lindeberg--Feller theorem
gives $(H_K-\mu_K)/\sqrt{\mu_K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{law}}\mathcal N(0,1)$.
Equation~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-finite-loss} implies $L_K/\sqrt{\mu_K}\to0$ almost
surely, so~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-score-decomposition} and Slutsky's theorem prove
\eqref{mbg:insp:con-clt-formula}.  Independence between $H_K$ and $L_K$ is neither
claimed nor needed.

For one block, Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:con-block-score} gives
$\E Z=0$ and $\E Z^2=k(k+1)4^{-k}$.  Summing variances of independent
blocks proves the exact identity in~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-actual-variance}.  The floor
errors are summable after weighting by $k(k+1)4^{-k}$, and
$\sum_{k\leq K}k^{2-p}\sim K^{3-p}/(3-p)$, giving its equivalent.
\end{proof}

This distinction explains why almost sure positive divergence is compatible
with zero expected excess at every deterministic endpoint.  The total
weighted loss $L_\infty$ is finite almost surely but has infinite mean:
\[
 \E L_\infty
 =\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}kR_k4^{-k}=\infty.
\]
Every finite stage still balances its rare negative contribution against
its positive one.  One cannot pass an expectation through the almost sure
limit or infer concentration of the whole score from concentration of the
good-block count.

\subsection{The inspection cost actually paid along the line}

The uniform bound controls the most expensive individual distribution.
The cost averaged over actual players is smaller, because half the players
always make exactly one inspection.

\begin{theorem}[Almost sure average inspection cost]
\label{mbg:insp:con-realized-cost}
Let $A_n=\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_i$ be the number of inspection operations made by
the first $n$ players under~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-schedule}.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-cost-limit}
 \frac{A_n}{n}\longrightarrow\frac32
 \quad\text{almost surely}.
\end{equation}
This statement concerns all prefixes $n$, and the convergence holds on the
same probability-one event as the score asymptotics after intersecting
finitely many probability-one events.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Enumerate all blocks by $b=1,2,\ldots$; write $k_b$ for their half-sizes,
$C_b$ for their total inspection costs, and
$T_B=2\sum_{b\leq B}k_b$ for their endpoint indices.  The variables $C_b$
are independent across blocks.  Proposition~\ref{mbg:insp:con-query-distribution}
gives
\begin{equation}\label{mbg:insp:con-block-cost-mean}
 \E C_b=k_b(3-4^{1-k_b}),
 \qquad
 \frac{\E C_b}{2k_b}=\frac32-\frac12\,4^{1-k_b}.
\end{equation}
\begin{writenote}[Corrected 4 October 2026, batch~92]
The manuscript printed the second identity as $\frac{\E C_b}{2k_b}=\frac32-\frac12,4^{1-k_b}$, a comma where a product is meant; dividing the first identity by $2k_b$ gives the product printed here.
\end{writenote}

All individual second moments are at most $\E G^2=6$; for T the second
moment is one.  Cauchy--Schwarz, which does not require independence within
a block, gives
\[
 \E C_b^2
 \leq (2k_b)\sum_{i\text{ in block }b}\E Q_i^2
 \leq14k_b^2.
\]
The schedule implies $k_b=O(\log(b+1))$: before stage $k$, the preceding
stage alone has order $4^{k-1}/(k-1)^p$ blocks.  Since $T_B\geq4B$,
\[
 \sum_{b=1}^{\infty}\frac{\Var(C_b)}{T_b^2}
 \leq C\sum_{b=1}^{\infty}\frac{\log^2(b+1)}{b^2}<\infty
\]
for a finite constant $C$.  The independent-series convergence theorem
followed by Kronecker's lemma now yields
\[
 \frac{\sum_{b\leq B}(C_b-\E C_b)}{T_B}\longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{almost surely}.
\]
Because $k_b\to\infty$, the weighted averages of
\eqref{mbg:insp:con-block-cost-mean} tend to $3/2$.  This proves
$A_{T_B}/T_B\to3/2$.

Finally, the deterministic worst total cost of a block of half-size $k$ is
\[
 k(2k-1)+k=2k^2.
\]
For $T_B\leq n<T_{B+1}$ the uncompleted block therefore contributes at most
$2k_{B+1}^2=O(\log^2(n+1))=o(n)$ additional inspections, and contains only
$2k_{B+1}=o(n)$ players.  The endpoint limit consequently extends to every
$n$, proving~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-cost-limit}.
\end{proof}

The number $3/2$ in~\eqref{mbg:insp:con-cost-limit} is an attained average for this
implementation.  It is not asserted to be the least possible average among
all successful strategies.  The sharp statement established by the cost
envelope concerns the supremum of individual expected costs and, more
generally, the supremum of each nondecreasing inspection cost functional.

\begin{proposition}[The longest realized inspection]\label{mbg:insp:con-max-cost}
For the same schedule, let $M_n=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}Q_i$.  Then
\[
 \frac{M_n}{\log_2 n}\longrightarrow1
 \quad\text{almost surely}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $N_{K-1}\leq n\leq N_K$, every player among the first $n$ belongs to
a stage of index at most $K$, so $M_n\leq2K-1$.  Equation
\eqref{mbg:insp:con-size-asymptotics} gives $\log_2 n=2K+O(\log K)$ uniformly in
this interval.  Thus $\limsup M_n/\log_2 n\leq1$ deterministically.

Fix $0<\epsilon<1$ and put $d_K=\lfloor2(1-\epsilon)K\rfloor$.
For all sufficiently large $K$, $1\leq d_K\leq2K-1$.  Choose one S player
from each block of stage $K$.  Their costs are independent across blocks,
and each has probability $2^{-(d_K-1)}\geq4^{-(1-\epsilon)K}$ of being at
least $d_K$.  The probability that none reaches $d_K$ is consequently at
most
\[
 \bigl(1-4^{-(1-\epsilon)K}\bigr)^{R_K}
 \leq\exp\!\left(-c\frac{4^{\epsilon K}}{K^p}\right)
\]
for some $c>0$ and all large $K$.  These probabilities are summable.
Borel--Cantelli implies $M_{N_K}\geq d_K$ eventually, almost surely.
For $N_{K-1}\leq n\leq N_K$, monotonicity gives
$M_n\geq M_{N_{K-1}}\geq d_{K-1}$, so
$\liminf M_n/\log_2 n\geq1-\epsilon$.  Intersect the probability-one
events for a countable sequence of positive $\epsilon$ tending to zero.
\end{proof}

Thus the realized average tends to $3/2$ while the largest inspection grows
like $\log_2 n$.  The cost distribution has a uniformly light tail, but its
support must continue expanding along the line.


\section{Inspection, dependence, and the density of correct guesses}
\label{mbg:insp:var-section}

Divergence of the surplus does not imply a limiting success frequency
above $1/2$.  Inspection complexity also gives a quantitative bound on
the possible dependence among the scores, and makes this distinction
precise.

\subsection{An exact covariance identity}

For a Boolean function $g$, let
\[
 \partial_jg
 =\frac{g(\varepsilon^{j,+})-g(\varepsilon^{j,-})}{2},
 \qquad
 \Inf_j(g)=\E(\partial_jg)^2,
 \qquad
 \Inf(g)=\sum_j\Inf_j(g).
\]
Here $\varepsilon^{j,+}$ and $\varepsilon^{j,-}$ denote the two
configurations obtained by setting coordinate $j$ to $+1$ and $-1$.
Thus $\Inf_j(g)$ is the probability that flipping that coordinate
changes $g$.  The definitions apply to measurable functions of
countably many hats, with a possibly infinite total influence.

\begin{lemma}[Mutual inspection controls covariance]
\label{mbg:insp:var-covariance}
For distinct players $i,j$,
\[
 \E(Y_iY_j)=\E\bigl[(\partial_jg_i)(\partial_ig_j)\bigr],
 \qquad
 |\E(Y_iY_j)|
 \le\sqrt{\Inf_j(g_i)\Inf_i(g_j)}.
\]
For any deterministic almost-surely terminating inspection algorithm
computing $g_i$,
\[
 \Inf_j(g_i)\le\Prb(\text{player $i$ inspects hat $j$}),
 \qquad \Inf(g_i)\le\E Q_i.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Condition on all hats except $\varepsilon_i,\varepsilon_j$.
Because each guess ignores its own hat, it has the form
\[
 g_i=a+\varepsilon_jb,
 \qquad g_j=c+\varepsilon_id,
\]
where $a,b,c,d$ depend on neither of these two hats, and
$b=\partial_jg_i$, $d=\partial_ig_j$.
Averaging $\varepsilon_i\varepsilon_jg_ig_j$ over the two independent
fair bits leaves exactly $bd$.  A further expectation and
Cauchy--Schwarz prove the first two assertions.

If flipping hat $j$ changes the output of a deterministic algorithm,
the algorithm must inspect $j$.  Otherwise the original input and the
flipped input follow the same path and have the same output.  For an
almost-surely terminating tree, the exceptional null set and its image
under a coordinate flip are still null, so the argument remains valid
almost surely.  Taking probabilities proves the individual influence
bound.  Summing over $j$ bounds total influence by the expected number
of distinct inspected hats, which is at most $\E Q_i$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A sharp variance bound]
\label{mbg:insp:var-weighted}
For any finite set of players $I$ and real coefficients $(a_i)_{i\in I}$,
\[
 \Var\!\left(\sum_{i\in I}a_iY_i\right)
 \le\sum_{i\in I}a_i^2
       \left(1+\sum_{j\in I\setminus\{i\}}\Inf_j(g_i)\right)
 \le\sum_{i\in I}a_i^2(1+\E Q_i).
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:var-weighted}a}
\]
In particular, if $B_n=\sum_{i=1}^n\E Q_i$, then
\[
 \Var(T_n)\le n+B_n.
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:var-weighted}b}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $\E Y_i=0$ and $Y_i^2=1$, the variance is
\[
 \sum_{i\in I}a_i^2+
 2\sum_{\substack{i,j\in I\\i<j}}a_ia_j\E(Y_iY_j).
\]
By Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:var-covariance}, the summand for a pair is at most
$2|a_ia_j|\sqrt{\Inf_j(g_i)\Inf_i(g_j)}$.
The inequality $2uv\le u^2+v^2$ bounds this by
\[
 a_i^2\Inf_j(g_i)+a_j^2\Inf_i(g_j).
\]
Summing over unordered pairs gives the first inequality; the
influence--inspection bound gives the second.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Sharpness and cancellation]
\label{mbg:insp:var-sharpness}
The factor $1+k$ cannot be reduced when the allowed depth is $k$.
Partition the players into blocks of size $m=k+1$, and let each player
guess the product of the other $k$ hats in the same block.  All $m$
scores in a block equal its parity, and different block parities are
independent fair signs.  At complete block endpoints,
\[
 \Var(T_n)=mn=(k+1)n,
\]
attaining Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:var-weighted}.  There is no comparable positive
lower bound: opposite-copy pairs have total score identically zero.
More generally, covariance is possible only through mutual
dependence.  If at least one of $\Inf_j(g_i)$ and $\Inf_i(g_j)$
vanishes for each distinct pair, the scores are pairwise uncorrelated
and $\Var(T_n)=n$, even though higher-order dependence may remain.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Finite-sample and almost-sure consequences}

\begin{corollary}[Concentration and convergence in mean square]
\label{mbg:insp:var-concentration}
For $t>0$,
\[
 \Prb(|T_n|\ge t)\le\frac{n+B_n}{t^2},
 \qquad
 \Prb(T_n\ge t)\le\frac{n+B_n}{n+B_n+t^2}.
\]
Equivalently, for $\epsilon>0$,
\[
 \Prb\left(\left|\frac{S_n}{n}-\frac12\right|\ge\epsilon\right)
 \le\frac{n+B_n}{4\epsilon^2n^2}.
\]
If $B_n=o(n^2)$, then $S_n/n\to1/2$ in mean square.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The two-sided estimate is Chebyshev's inequality.  For the one-sided
estimate, let $\sigma^2=\Var(T_n)$ and apply Markov's inequality to
$(T_n+u)^2$, with $u\ge0$:
\[
 \Prb(T_n\ge t)\le\frac{\sigma^2+u^2}{(t+u)^2}.
\]
Taking $u=\sigma^2/t$ gives Cantelli's bound
$\sigma^2/(\sigma^2+t^2)$; its monotonicity in $\sigma^2$ allows
Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:var-weighted} to be substituted.  Finally,
\[
 \E\left(\frac{S_n}{n}-\frac12\right)^2
 =\frac{\Var(T_n)}{4n^2}\le\frac{n+B_n}{4n^2},
\]
which proves the remaining assertions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Uniformly bounded mean inspection gives fair density]
\label{mbg:insp:var-strong-law}
If $\sup_i\E Q_i\le K<\infty$, then
\[
 \frac{T_n}{n}\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \frac{S_n}{n}\longrightarrow\frac12
 \quad\text{almost surely}.
\]
The same conclusion holds under the more general sufficient condition
\[
 \sum_{i\ge1}\frac{1+\E Q_i}{i^2}<\infty.
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:var-strong-law}a}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under the uniform bound, Chebyshev's inequality gives
\[
 \sum_{r\ge1}
 \Prb\bigl(|T_{r^2}|>\epsilon r^2\bigr)
 \le\frac{K+1}{\epsilon^2}\sum_{r\ge1}\frac1{r^2}<\infty.
\]
Borel--Cantelli, applied to a countable sequence of positive
$\epsilon$ tending to zero, implies $T_{r^2}/r^2\to0$ almost surely.
For $r^2\le n<(r+1)^2$, bounded score increments give
\[
 |T_n-T_{r^2}|\le2r+1.
\]
Division by $n$ completes the proof in the uniform case.

For the general condition, put $c_i=1+\E Q_i$,
$C_n=\sum_{i\le n}c_i$, and fix $a>1$.
For the eventually increasing sequence $n_r=\lfloor a^r\rfloor$,
geometric summation gives a finite constant $A_a$ such that
\[
 \sum_r\frac{C_{n_r}}{n_r^2}
 =\sum_i c_i\sum_{r:n_r\ge i}\frac1{n_r^2}
 \le A_a\sum_i\frac{c_i}{i^2}<\infty.
\]
Chebyshev and Borel--Cantelli therefore give $T_{n_r}/n_r\to0$
almost surely.  Between consecutive $n_r$, bounded increments imply
$\limsup_n|T_n|/n\le a-1$ almost surely.
Taking the intersection of these full-measure events for
$a=1+1/\ell$, $\ell\ge1$, proves the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the critical construction achieves]
\label{mbg:insp:var-density-interpretation}
For the construction with $\sup_i\E Q_i=2$,
Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:var-weighted} gives $\Var(T_n)\le3n$, and
Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:var-strong-law} gives limiting success density exactly
$1/2$.  Its almost-sure divergent surplus is therefore a sublinear
phenomenon.  It is fully consistent with $\E T_n=0$ for every finite
$n$: the variables $T_n$ are not uniformly integrable, and rare losses
can balance the positive outcomes at each finite stage.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A robust form of the degree obstruction}

The preceding obstruction is not restricted to exact degree bounds.
It also forces the normalized variance of a highly successful score
to leave every fixed collection of low Walsh degrees.

\begin{proposition}[Spectral escape]
\label{mbg:insp:var-spectral-escape}
Let $X\not\equiv0$ be a mean-zero square-integrable function of
independent fair bits, and let $L$ be its orthogonal projection onto
Walsh degrees at most $d$.  Set $\delta=\Prb(X<0)$.  Then
\[
 \frac{\norm{L}_2^2}{\norm{X}_2^2}
 \le 2\cdot3^d\sqrt\delta.
 \tag{\ref{mbg:insp:var-spectral-escape}a}
\]
Thus, if $\Prb(T_n>0)\to1$, then for every fixed $d$,
\[
 \frac{\sum_{|A|\le d}\widehat T_n(A)^2}{\Var(T_n)}
 \longrightarrow0
\]
along the nonzero scores.  In particular, this conclusion is necessary
for almost-sure positive divergence.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The assertion is trivial if $L=0$.  Otherwise orthogonal projection,
H\"older's inequality, and Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:obs-hypercontractivity} give
\[
 \norm{L}_2^2
 =\E(XL)
 \le\norm{X}_{4/3}\norm{L}_4
 \le3^{d/2}\norm{X}_1^{1/2}\norm{X}_2^{1/2}\norm{L}_2.
\]
After division and squaring,
\[
 \frac{\norm{L}_2^2}{\norm{X}_2^2}
 \le3^d\frac{\norm{X}_1}{\norm{X}_2}.
\]
Since $\E X=0$, Cauchy--Schwarz yields
\[
 \norm{X}_1=2\E X_-
 \le2\norm{X}_2\sqrt{\Prb(X<0)},
\]
which proves the estimate.  In countably many coordinates the same
fourth-moment bound for a fixed-degree projection follows by
finite-coordinate approximation and Fatou's lemma.  Finally,
$\Prb(T_n>0)\to1$ implies $\Prb(T_n<0)\to0$, so the estimate applies
for each fixed $d$.  Almost-sure positive divergence implies the
stated probability convergence by bounded convergence.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[An approximate-degree consequence]
\label{mbg:insp:var-approximate-degree}
If a mean-zero score has a proportion at least $\alpha>0$ of its
variance in degrees at most $d$, then
Proposition~\ref{mbg:insp:var-spectral-escape} gives the explicit lower bound
$\Prb(X<0)\ge\alpha^2/(4\cdot9^d)$.
For example, an $L^2$ approximation by degree at most $d$ with relative
error at most $\eta<1$ guarantees
$\alpha\ge1-\eta^2$ and hence
\[
 \Prb(X<0)\ge\frac{(1-\eta^2)^2}{4\cdot9^d}.
\]
This concerns the approximation of the aggregate score, not separate
approximations to individual guesses.  Uniformly small inspection
tails for each player do not by themselves provide such an aggregate
relative-error estimate.
\end{remark}


\section{Sharp inspection cost for finite first-moment extremizers}
\label{mbg:insp:sec:fin}

This section studies finite teams, with no restriction to linear prediction
rules.  The lower bounds apply to adaptive inspections.  Besides a sharp
cost bound, we obtain a rigidity statement: attaining that bound forces the
all-wrong configurations to form an affine binary code of a specific type.
The coding construction is classical in its ingredients; the statements
below distinguish those ingredients from the inspection-cost conclusions.

\subsection{Model and equality in the first-moment bound}

There are $n\geq1$ players, and the hat vector
$x=(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$ is uniform on $\{0,1\}^n$.  Player $j$ predicts
$g_j(x_{-j})\in\{0,1\}$ using a deterministic decision tree that may inspect
coordinates other than $j$.  Let $Q_j(x)$ be the number of inspected
coordinates.  Repeated inspections can be deleted, so we assume that a
coordinate is queried at most once on any execution.  Define
\[
 H_j(x)=\indic{g_j(x_{-j})=x_j},\qquad
 S(x)=\sum_{j=1}^nH_j(x),\qquad
 Q(x)=\sum_{j=1}^n Q_j(x).
\]
Fix an integer $s$ with $n/2<s\leq n$.  Blindness to one's own hat gives
$\E H_j=1/2$, and therefore
\begin{equation}
 \Prb(S\geq s)\leq \frac{\E S}{s}=\frac{n}{2s}.
 \label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-markov}
\end{equation}
We call equality in \eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-markov} \emph{first-moment saturation}.
This is an equality condition, not an assertion that the bound is
attainable for arbitrary $n,s$.

\begin{lemma}[The two possible scores]\label{mbg:insp:lem:fin-two-scores}
First-moment saturation holds if and only if
$S(x)\in\{0,s\}$ for every $x$.  In this case, with
\[
 C=\{x:S(x)=0\},\qquad d=2s-n,\qquad
 \delta=\Prb(C)=\frac{d}{2s},
\]
the set $C$ is nonempty and contains no two adjacent vertices of the
binary cube.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Subtracting $s\Prb(S\geq s)$ from $\E S$ yields
\[
 \E\bigl[S\indic{S<s}\bigr]+
 \E\bigl[(S-s)\indic{S\geq s}\bigr].
\]
Both terms are nonnegative.  They vanish exactly when the two-score
condition holds; every cube point has positive probability.  The formula
for $\delta$ follows from $\E S=n/2$.  If $c\in C$ and $c^{(i)}$ is
obtained by flipping coordinate $i$, player $i$ makes the same prediction
at both inputs and hence is correct at $c^{(i)}$.  Thus $c^{(i)}\notin C$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A local lower bound that permits adaptive queries}

\begin{theorem}[Inspection cost at every all-wrong input]
\label{mbg:insp:thm:fin-local}
Suppose first-moment saturation holds.  At every $c\in C$ and for every
coordinate $i$, at least $s-1$ other players inspect hat $i$ on their
executions at $c$.  Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 Q(c)\geq n(s-1)\quad(c\in C).
 \label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-local-cost}
\end{equation}
In particular, a uniform per-player decision-tree depth bound $k$ must
satisfy $k\geq s-1$.  Without any uniform depth assumption,
\begin{align}
 \E[Q\mid C]&\geq n(s-1),\label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-conditional-cost}\\
 \E Q&\geq 2\delta n(s-1)
       =\frac{nd(s-1)}{s}.
       \label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-average-cost}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix $c\in C$ and $i$.  By Lemma~\ref{mbg:insp:lem:fin-two-scores},
$S(c^{(i)})=s$.  Player $i$ is one of those $s$ correct players.  Each of
the other $s-1$ correct players, say $j$, has the same own hat at $c$ and
$c^{(i)}$, but changes from an incorrect prediction to a correct one.
Therefore $g_j(c_{-j})\ne g_j(c^{(i)}_{-j})$.

A deterministic decision tree that does not inspect coordinate $i$ on its
execution at $c$ follows precisely the same path at $c^{(i)}$: every
queried answer on that path is unchanged.  It would produce the same
output, a contradiction.  Thus each of these $s-1$ players inspects $i$.
Summing over $i$ counts every inspection at $c$ once, proving
\eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-local-cost}, the depth bound, and
\eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-conditional-cost}.

For the unconditional bound, count changes of prediction along cube
edges.  Each of the $n|C|$ undirected edges with one endpoint in $C$ has
exactly $s-1$ prediction changes by players other than the coordinate
being flipped.  There is no double counting of these edges because $C$
is independent.  A prediction change across an edge forces that
coordinate to be inspected at \emph{both} endpoints, by the same
decision-tree argument.  Equivalently, if
\[
 I_j(x)=\#\{i\ne j:g_j(x_{-j})\ne g_j(x^{(i)}_{-j})\},
\]
then $I_j(x)\leq Q_j(x)$.  Summing the contributions of just the edges
incident to $C$ gives
\[
 \sum_x Q(x)\geq\sum_x\sum_j I_j(x)
 \geq 2n|C|(s-1).
\]
Division by $2^n$ proves \eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-average-cost}.
\end{proof}

At equality in the uniform depth bound $k=s-1$, the query graph at any
$c\in C$ has both its in-degrees and its out-degrees equal to $s-1$.
Moreover, every queried coordinate at that input changes the querying
player's prediction when flipped.  These conclusions follow because all
inequalities in the local counting proof are then equalities.

Independent randomization does not weaken the theorem.  Conditional on
the random seed, \eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-markov} holds for the resulting deterministic
strategy.  Saturation after averaging forces saturation for almost every
seed.  Applying the theorem seed by seed gives the same bounds, with
expectations over hats and randomness.  A stated uniform depth bound must
hold for those deterministic realizations.

\subsection{Fourier rigidity and the failure-set geometry}

For $A\subseteq[n]$, write
$\chi_A(x)=(-1)^{\sum_{i\in A}x_i}$ and
$\widehat f(A)=\E[f\chi_A]$.  These characters are an orthonormal basis
for functions on the uniform binary cube.  The following argument
includes the needed Fourier identities.

\begin{theorem}[Geometry at the minimum depth]
\label{mbg:insp:thm:fin-fourier}
Suppose first-moment saturation holds and every player has decision-tree
depth at most $s-1$.  For $f=\ind C$, every nonconstant nonzero Fourier
coefficient has degree exactly $s$.  Consequently:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item $C$ has uniform projections onto every set of at most $s-1$
 coordinates;
 \item every input outside $C$ has exactly $d=2s-n$ neighbors in $C$;
 \item every player inspects exactly $s-1$ hats at every input.
\end{enumerate}
For odd $n$ and $s=(n+1)/2$, the closed radius-one balls centered at $C$
partition the cube: $C$ is a binary one-error-correcting perfect code.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $F=2S-n$.  First-moment saturation gives
\begin{equation}
 F=d-2sf.
 \label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-F-f}
\end{equation}
Each signed correctness function
$2H_j-1=(-1)^{x_j+g_j(x_{-j})}$ has degree at most $k+1$ when the
prediction tree has depth at most $k$.  Indeed, the indicator of a leaf
is a product of at most $k$ factors $(1\pm\chi_{\{i\}})/2$; the signed
prediction is a signed sum of these indicators.  Multiplication by
$\chi_{\{j\}}$ increases the degree by at most one.  Thus
$\deg f\leq s$ under the present depth assumption.

Independence of $C$ gives
$\E[(f(x)-f(x^{(i)}))^2]=2\delta$ for every $i$.
Since flipping $i$ negates exactly the characters containing $i$,
Parseval's identity yields
\begin{equation}
 \sum_A |A|\widehat f(A)^2=\frac{n\delta}{2},
 \qquad
 \sum_{A\ne\varnothing}\widehat f(A)^2
 =\delta(1-\delta)=\frac{n\delta}{2s}.
 \label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-energy}
\end{equation}
The average nonconstant Fourier degree is therefore exactly $s$.
All those degrees are at most $s$, so every nonzero one equals $s$.
This also supplies a second proof of the depth lower bound: without
assuming $k=s-1$, the same average degree implies $k+1\geq s$.

If $J\subseteq[n]$ has $|J|\leq s-1$, expansion of the indicator of any
specified $J$-pattern into characters gives
\[
 \Prb(C\text{ and }x_J=a)=\delta\,2^{-|J|}.
\]
This proves the uniform-projection assertion, often described as an
orthogonal-array property.

Let $\mathcal A$ be cube adjacency:
$(\mathcal Af)(x)=\sum_i f(x^{(i)})$.
Its action on a character is
$\mathcal A\chi_A=(n-2|A|)\chi_A$.  The constant term of $f$ is $\delta$,
and all its other terms have degree $s$.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal Af=n\delta+(n-2s)(f-\delta)
            =d(1-f).
 \label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-equitable}
\end{equation}
This says that $C$ has no internal edges and that each outside vertex
has exactly $d$ neighbors in $C$.

Finally, put $k=s-1$.  If a player's tree had a leaf of depth
$\ell<k$, that leaf would fix $\ell$ other hats and the prediction.
On $C$ the player's own hat would then be fixed to the opposite bit.
But the $\ell$ queried hats together with the player's own hat are at
most $k$ coordinates, whose projection from $C$ is uniform.  The
purportedly excluded pattern must therefore occur, a contradiction.
All leaves have depth $k$.  When $d=1$, equation
\eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-equitable} says that every vertex outside $C$ has one
neighbor in $C$, which proves the perfect-code assertion.
\end{proof}

The depth hypothesis matters.  Saturation by itself only makes $C$
independent; it does not make $C$ a perfect code.  In particular, the
perfect-code conclusion is a property of inspection-efficient
extremizers, rather than a consequence of the first moment alone.

\begin{theorem}[Affine rigidity and the exact parameter criterion]
\label{mbg:insp:thm:fin-affine}
A first-moment-saturating strategy with per-player depth at most $s-1$
exists if and only if there are integers $r\geq1$ and $m\geq1$ such that
\begin{equation}
 n=m(2^r-1),\qquad s=m2^{r-1}.
 \label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-parameters}
\end{equation}
For every such strategy, its all-wrong set $C$ is affine.  After
permuting coordinates, it is a coset of the kernel of a binary
$r\times n$ matrix containing each nonzero column of $\{0,1\}^r$
exactly $m$ times.  In particular, for odd $n$ at the strict-majority
threshold, an extremizer of minimum depth has an affine Hamming failure
code.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Necessity]
Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:fin-fourier} supplies the uniform projections through
size $k=s-1$, and every leaf has depth exactly $k$.
Consider one leaf for player $j$, querying the set $J$ of $k$ other
coordinates and reached by the pattern $x_J=a$.  The conditional
density of $C$ on the $s$ coordinates $A=J\cup\{j\}$ has the form
\begin{equation}
 \E[f\mid x_A]=\delta+\widehat f(A)\chi_A(x).
 \label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-leaf-projection}
\end{equation}
All other nonconstant characters supported on $A$ vanish by the
Fourier conclusion.  At this leaf, $C$ excludes the value of $x_j$
equal to the leaf's prediction.  Substitution of that excluded pattern
into \eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-leaf-projection} gives
$\widehat f(A)=\varepsilon_A\delta$ for some
$\varepsilon_A\in\{-1,1\}$.  Consequently
$\E[\chi_A\mid C]=\varepsilon_A$, and therefore every point of $C$
satisfies the affine parity equation $\chi_A=\varepsilon_A$.

Take these equations for every leaf of every player, and let $D$ be
their common solution set.  We have $C\subseteq D$.  Conversely, if
$x\in D$, follow each player's tree to its actual leaf.  The parity
equation associated with that leaf, together with its queried pattern,
forces that player's own bit to be the opposite of the prediction.
Every player is wrong, so $x\in C$.  Thus $C=D$, a nonempty affine
solution space.

Write $C=a+V$, with $V$ a linear subspace of codimension $r$.
Its density is $\delta=2^{-r}$, and $r\geq1$.  Let
$L=V^\perp$.  Direct summation over $a+V$ gives
\[
 \widehat f(A)=
 \begin{cases}
  2^{-r}\chi_A(a),&\mathbf 1_A\in L,\\
  0,&\mathbf 1_A\notin L,
 \end{cases}
\]
where $\mathbf 1_A\in\{0,1\}^n$ denotes the binary incidence vector of $A$.
Every nonzero word of $L$ therefore has weight $s$.
The identity $\delta=d/(2s)=2^{-r}$ gives
$s=d2^{r-1}$ and $n=d(2^r-1)$, proving
\eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-parameters} with $m=d$.

The remaining constant-weight classification is the binary case of
Bonisoli's theorem~\cite{insp:Bonisoli}; we include its short Fourier proof
here.  Choose a full-rank generator matrix for $L$ and let
$M_b$ count its columns equal to $b\in\{0,1\}^r$.  For every nonzero
$u$, the word obtained by multiplying this matrix by $u$ has weight
$s$, so
\[
 \sum_b M_b(-1)^{u\cdot b}=n-2s=-m,
 \qquad \sum_b M_b=n.
\]
Fourier inversion on $\{0,1\}^r$ now gives $M_0=0$ and
$M_b=m$ for every $b\ne0$.  This proves the claimed description of $C$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An explicit trace construction attaining every allowed pair}

The next construction completes the sufficiency proof and gives actual
inspection sets and prediction formulas.  When $m=1$, its syndrome-zero
set is the usual binary Hamming code; the proof below establishes every
property used here without relying on a coding-theoretic black box.

\begin{theorem}[Replicated trace strategy]\label{mbg:insp:thm:fin-trace}
For every $r,m\geq1$, the parameters in
\eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-parameters} admit a nonadaptive strategy using exactly
$s-1$ inspections per player and satisfying
\[
 \Prb(S=s)=1-2^{-r}=\frac{n}{2s},\qquad
 \Prb(S=0)=2^{-r}.
\]
For $m=1$, this gives $n=2^r-1$, $s=2^{r-1}$, and the sharp depth
$k=2^{r-1}-1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $K$ be the field with $q=2^r$ elements, and let
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}(z)=z+z^2+\cdots+z^{2^{r-1}}\in\{0,1\}
\]
be its absolute trace.  The trace is linear over the two-element field.
It is nonzero because its defining polynomial is nonzero and has
degree $2^{r-1}<q$, so it cannot vanish at all $q$ field elements.
Consequently exactly $q/2$ elements have trace one.  Fix
$c\in K$ with $\operatorname{Tr}(c)=1$.

Index the players by $(v,a)\in K^*\times\{1,\ldots,m\}$, and put
\[
 Z(x)=\sum_{w\in K^*}\sum_{b=1}^m x_{w,b}\frac{c}{w}\in K.
\]
Player $(v,a)$ predicts the unique value of its own bit that would make
$\operatorname{Tr}(vZ)=1$.  Explicitly, all arithmetic in the prediction
formula is modulo two:
\begin{equation}
 g_{v,a}=1+
 \sum_{(w,b)\ne(v,a)}
    \operatorname{Tr}\!\left(\frac{vc}{w}\right)x_{w,b}.
 \label{mbg:insp:eq:fin-trace-rule}
\end{equation}
The coefficient of its own hat in $\operatorname{Tr}(vZ)$ is
$\operatorname{Tr}(c)=1$, so this is a blind and well-defined prediction.
It needs precisely the other hats whose displayed coefficients are one.
As $w$ runs over $K^*$, the element $vc/w$ runs over $K^*$; precisely
$q/2$ of those values have trace one.  Counting $m$ copies and removing
the player's own hat leaves $mq/2-1=s-1$ inspected hats.

Player $(v,a)$ is correct exactly when $\operatorname{Tr}(vZ)=1$.
If $Z=0$, no player is correct.  If $Z\ne0$, multiplication by $Z$
permutes $K^*$, so exactly $q/2$ choices of $v$, each with $m$ copies,
are correct.  Thus $S=mq/2=s$.

Finally, $Z$ is a surjective linear map from the binary hat space to
the $r$-dimensional binary vector space $K$: its coefficients include
every nonzero element of $K$.  All its fibers have cardinality
$2^{n-r}$, and a uniform hat vector makes $Z$ uniform on $K$.
Therefore $\Prb(Z=0)=1/q$.  The displayed score distribution follows,
and Theorem~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:fin-local} proves that no strategy saturating the
same first-moment bound can have smaller uniform depth.
\end{proof}

For $m=1$, the coefficients $c/w$ are distinct and nonzero.  A nonzero
syndrome $Z$ can be eliminated by flipping exactly the unique hat whose
coefficient equals $Z$.  This directly verifies the perfect-code
property of the failure set.  For general $m$, each nonzero syndrome
has exactly $m=d$ such single-hat corrections, in agreement with
\eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-equitable}.

The small odd strict-majority cases are
\[
\begin{array}{c|rrrr}
 r&1&2&3&4\\\hline
 n&1&3&7&15\\
 s&1&2&4&8\\
 k=s-1&0&1&3&7\\
 \Prb(C)&1/2&1/4&1/8&1/16.
\end{array}
\]
The minimum-depth classification is narrower than the unrestricted
problem of optimizing the score distribution: it imposes a sharp
inspection budget and derives both arithmetic restrictions and affine
structure from equality in that budget.

\emph{What this section settles elsewhere in the report.} (i)~Part~\ref{mbg:part:one}'s Q9, with $m$ players, $n$ boxes and $q=2$: by Theorems~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:fin-affine} and~\ref{mbg:insp:thm:fin-trace}, for $(m,q,n)=(N,2,N)$ with $N=m(2^r-1)$ and the threshold $t=s=m2^{r-1}$, the best probability of at least $t$ successes over legal decision trees is $1-2^{-r}$, attained by the trace strategy (player~$i$ targets box~$i$ and reads $s-1$ other boxes, a legal nonadaptive tree); the first-moment bound~\eqref{mbg:insp:eq:fin-markov} holds for every team of blind binary output maps with free targets, since each succeeds on exactly half of the configurations (Proposition~\ref{mbg:prop:matching}), so the blind optimum is the same. This holds for every $m\ge1$. The even case $m=2a$ is the family of Part~\ref{mbg:hat:part}'s Corollary~\ref{mbg:hat:cor:finite-optimal}, attained there by the syndrome block, which reads all $N-1$ other hats of its block rather than $s-1$; the case $m=1$, $r=2$ is Part~\ref{mbg:part:one}'s three-box team of Section~\ref{mbg:sec:checks}, and $r=1$ is $(m,2,m)$ with $t=m$ and optimum~$1/2$. As in the batch-90 note, the same optimum holds for every $n\ge N$, the further boxes being left unread (the write's observation). (iii)~Research question~29.5: for the parameters of Corollary~\ref{mbg:hat:cor:finite-optimal} the extremizers of minimum depth $s-1$ exist ($m=2a$) and all have an affine failure set with each nonzero parity-check column repeated $m$ times; extremizers of greater depth, such as the syndrome block when $r\ge2$, are not classified. The notes after the questions of Sections~\ref{mbg:sec:questions} and~\ref{mbg:hat:sec:questions} record this.
