\section{A concrete route to formalization in ProveIt}
\label{mbg:insp:sec:formalization}

The results in this article are mathematical proofs, not additions to a
proof-assistant library.  Their connection with \repo is nevertheless
specific: the repository already distinguishes finite executable certificates,
their mathematical meanings, and the arithmetic formulas that represent those
meanings \cite{qry:ProveIt,qry:ListCoding}.  The earlier hat-guessing manuscript asks for
an information-cost theorem and a complete certificate chain
\cite{qry:PriorHat}; the box-game report separately identifies the missing
decision-tree compiler and reflection theorem \cite{qry:RepoBoxes}.  The following
development would address those interfaces without treating an executable
experiment as a proof of an infinite probability statement.

\subsection{Operational semantics before query bounds}

The first module should use a finite binary tree whose leaves carry guesses
and whose internal nodes carry natural-number labels of queried hats.  The
two children correspond to the two observed colors.  A tree is valid for
player \(i\) when every reachable query avoids \(i\); in a normalized tree,
no coordinate is queried twice along one branch.  Labels may repeat on
different branches.  There should be no restriction on the distance of a
query, on the number of players that can inspect one hat, or on a common
finite set containing every player's observations.

Evaluation on a configuration returns both a guess and its number of queries.
The distinction is necessary: two trees can compute the same Boolean function
with different costs.  The basic structural induction should prove the
following facts.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Evaluation terminates, and changing player \(i\)'s own hat changes
neither its guess nor its query transcript.
\item Only the finite collection of labels appearing in the tree can affect
evaluation.
\item If the tree has depth at most \(q\), its sign-valued output has a
multilinear representation of degree at most \(q\).
\item A reachable branch of length \(h\) gives
\(\mathbb P(Q\ge r)\ge 2^{1-r}\) for \(1\le r\le h\).
For a tree without repeated queries along a branch, the probability of
following any prescribed \(r-1\) answers is exactly \(2^{1-r}\).
\end{enumerate}

The degree proof is the recursion that combines the two child polynomials
using the indicators of the queried sign.  Multilinear reduction uses
\(x_j^2=1\), and cannot increase degree.  Multiplying the guess sign by the
player's own sign gives the correctness sign, with degree at most \(q+1\).
This is an algebraic invariant of evaluation, not an assumption about
independence of different players' successes.

The fourth assertion supplies the finite foundation of the cost frontier.
For a nonnegative nondecreasing cost function \(\Phi\), prove first the exact
finite identity
\[
 \mathbb E\Phi(Q)
 =\Phi(0)+\sum_{r=1}^{h}
       \bigl(\Phi(r)-\Phi(r-1)\bigr)\mathbb P(Q\ge r).
\]
It then gives a lower bound by the corresponding truncated geometric sum.
The infinite geometric value is introduced only afterward, by monotone
convergence.  Keeping these two steps separate handles finite and infinite
cost frontiers with the same finite theorem.

\subsection{Finite cube algebra and the obstruction}

For the first \(n\) players, the union of their tree labels and their own
coordinates is finite.  Thus their score can be analyzed on an ordinary
finite Boolean cube, even when they query hats with arbitrarily large
indices.  The second module should establish the orthogonality of Walsh
characters, the relevant fourth-moment inequality for bounded-degree
polynomials, and the resulting two-sided sign bound for nonzero centered
polynomials.  All expectations at this stage are finite averages.

The proof must also cover a polynomial that is identically zero.  A statement
about a strictly negative value cannot include that case, whereas the
one-sided event needed by the obstruction can.  Recording this distinction in
the lemma statements avoids silently dividing by a zero second moment.

The crucial interface is stability under fixing finitely many hat colors.
Substituting constants cannot increase polynomial degree.  Moreover, for a
player whose own coordinate has not been fixed, its correctness sign still
has mean zero: its own fair bit remains independent of its guess.  If a
finite set \(F\) has been fixed, only players in \(F\) can contribute to the
conditional mean of the score.  Their total absolute contribution to the
excess is at most \(|F|/2\), uniformly in the prefix length.

A suitable finite theorem therefore has three explicit parameters: the
depth bound, the fixed-coordinate cylinder, and the prefix length.  It
supplies a positive lower bound, depending only on depth, for a score that
does not exceed the cylinder's fixed additive allowance.  It requires no
bound on the number of times an observed hat is reused across the team.
The conditional formulation is essential to the final probability-zero
conclusion; an unconditional sign bound alone would only rule out
almost-sure divergence.

\subsection{The countable probability layer}

The third module works on the fair product space of countably many hats.
Finite-tree evaluations are cylinder-measurable, so the excess variables
and the event \(D_n\to+\infty\) are measurable.  The finite-cube theorem
transfers to every finite-coordinate cylinder.  A cylinder approximation
lemma, or equivalently convergence of conditional expectations with respect
to the first \(m\) coordinates, then rules out a positive-measure divergence
event.

This step should not invoke a zero-one law for tail events.  In the permitted
query model, changing one early hat can alter infinitely many later guesses,
so the divergence event has not been assumed invariant under finite
coordinate changes.  Approximation of an arbitrary measurable event by
finite-coordinate information is the appropriate statement.

The positive construction needs a different probability interface.
Its bad blocks have summable probabilities, whereas independent good blocks
have a divergent sum of probabilities.  The two Borel--Cantelli conclusions
must be proved or imported with those respective hypotheses.  A separate
deterministic prefix lemma extends block-end divergence to every physical
prefix.  Endpoint calculations alone do not establish that conclusion.

Finally, the geometric query law is verified on each finite block.  Its
uniform tail bound supplies the upper cost estimate, and increasing block
sizes approach the geometric value.  This yields the sharp mean and
exponential-moment boundaries through the same cost identity; a separate
probabilistic construction for each cost is unnecessary.

\subsection{What the existing coding library supplies}

The inspected \nolinkurl{PAListCoding.Basic} module contains \nolinkurl{encode},
\nolinkurl{decode}, and proofs named \nolinkurl{encode_valid},
\nolinkurl{encode_length}, \nolinkurl{encode_nth},
\nolinkurl{encode_injective},
\nolinkurl{valid_iff_existsUnique_encode},
\nolinkurl{encode_decode}, and \nolinkurl{decode_encode}.
It also exposes an actual arithmetic validity formula and its standard-model
specification.  The inspected \nolinkurl{PAListCoding.Predicates} module supplies
guarded relations including \nolinkurl{HasLength}, \nolinkurl{Entry},
\nolinkurl{Concat}, \nolinkurl{NoDuplicates}, \nolinkurl{Occurrences},
\nolinkurl{SumElements}, and \nolinkurl{ProductElements}, together with arithmetic
formula interfaces \cite{qry:ListCoding}.

These interfaces can represent a finite tagged serialization of a query tree
and a finite transcript.  A new validity predicate must check the node tags,
child references, queried labels, and the exclusion of the player's own
coordinate.  A compiler correctness theorem would then relate the decoded
object to the operational semantics above.  Existing list round-trip
theorems justify recovering the serialized list; they do not already prove
the hat-specific semantics.

Two limitations deserve explicit treatment.  First, the inspected Lean
encoding and decoder are declared noncomputable.  Their mathematical
contracts can support a proof, but an efficient external certificate format
requires its own executable implementation and a proved bridge.  Second, the
exported formula specifications concern truth in the standard natural-number
model.  They should not be described as internal PA derivations of every
existence or correctness assertion without an additional syntactic proof.

\subsection{What finite verification can certify}

A companion checker can exhaust small cubes, compare tree evaluation with the
stated block rule, reject an own-hat query, and compute exact distributions
of scores, prefixes, and costs.  Its rational arithmetic can check the finite
geometric sums and the boundary cases of the cost identity.  These tests are
useful for detecting an incorrect branch, a query-count convention, or an
off-by-one error in a prefix.

They do not establish hypercontractivity for arbitrary degree, measurable
event approximation, either Borel--Cantelli theorem, or the infinite cost
frontier.  Nor does a successful run prove that the checker's acceptance
predicate entails the mathematical statement it is meant to certify.
The existing box-game Python checker has the same distinction between
execution and a proved reflection theorem \cite{qry:RepoBoxes}.

The practical order is therefore: verify tree semantics and finite geometric
costs; prove the finite cube obstruction and its cylinder version; connect
the audited serialization interfaces; and only then assemble the countable
probability theorems.  Each stage has a theorem-level contract that can be
reviewed independently, and none requires presenting the current manuscript
as already kernel-checked.



\section{Finite verification and reproducibility}
\label{mbg:insp:sec:verification}

The supplied program \nolinkurl{code/verify_finite.py} uses only the
Python standard library and exact integer arithmetic. Its final run
completed all \(19\) cases listed below. It exhausts every hat assignment
for each listed case; it does not sample assignments or search over
all possible strategies.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Family & Parameter cases & Hat assignments checked\\
\midrule
Exceptional blocks, \(t=2,4,\ldots,12\) & 6 & 5,460\\
Trace strategies, \(r=1,2,3,4\), \(m=1\) & 4 & 32,906\\
Replicated trace strategies & 9 & 21,180\\
\midrule
Total & 19 & 59,546\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The replicated cases are \(r=1\), \(m=2,\ldots,6\);
\(r=2\), \(m=2,3,4\); and \(r=3,m=2\).
Across the cases, the program performed \(838{,}498\) comparisons under
flipping a player's own hat, verifying that both the computed output
and, where adaptive, the transcript remain unchanged.
For the exceptional blocks it checked \(30{,}927\) individual
nonnegative pair increments and the same number of pair prefixes.
For the trace strategies it checked \(73{,}367\) failure-boundary
edge sensitivities.

The independent descriptions compared by the checker are the operational
query rules, the direct classification of block configurations, and the
linear syndrome characterization. This catches an implementation that
produces a correct final count while illegally reading its own hat, as
well as a wrong branch order or an incorrect query count. The trace
checks also verify uniform syndrome fibers, the exact number of
all-wrong neighbors, and the minimum-depth query counts.

The complete exact distributions and individual case counts are stored
in \nolinkurl{data/verification.json}. That file is written only after
every assertion succeeds. To reproduce the checks from the extracted
package directory, run
\begin{lstlisting}
python3 code/verify_finite.py
\end{lstlisting}
To rebuild this standalone article, run
\begin{lstlisting}
pdflatex hat_inspection_frontier.tex
pdflatex hat_inspection_frontier.tex
\end{lstlisting}
A third run may be needed if a local TeX installation changes the
table-of-contents pagination; the supplied build script runs three.
No bibliography database, generated figure, or network connection is
required.

The finite enumeration verifies the supplied implementations and their
finite identities. The probability-zero obstruction, the general
affine-rigidity theorem, and the infinite cost frontier depend on the
proofs in the article. No enumeration of these finite cases is offered
as a substitute for those arguments.

\section{Further research questions}
\label{mbg:insp:sec:questions}

The exact geometric frontier settles the existence question for
uniform deterministic query costs. It leaves the following quantitative
and structural problems. These are proposed research targets; except
where an earlier source is explicitly cited, they are not advertised
as a list of already recognized open problems.

\begin{question}[Growth at the optimal cost frontier]
Characterize the increasing functions \(h(n)\to\infty\) for which a
finite-tree strategy can satisfy
\[
 D_n\asymp h(n)\quad\text{almost surely},
 \qquad
 \sup_i\Prb(Q_i>r)\le2^{-r}\quad(r\ge0).
\]
In particular, can the optimal geometric tail coexist with
\(D_n\asymp\log n\), or with \(D_n\) growing like a positive power of
\(n\)? The present schedule gives explicit powers of \(\log n\) smaller
than the first power. The predecessor's much faster construction
\cite{qry:PriorHat} does not supply this optimal cost envelope.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[The general depth--confidence curve]
Let \(p_k\) be the supremum of
\(\Prb(S_N>N/2)\) over all finite teams and deterministic adaptive
strategies of depth at most \(k\).
The polynomial argument gives an explicit exponential gap from \(1\).
The trace family gives
\[
 p_{\,2^{r-1}-1}\ge1-2^{-r}
 =1-\frac{1}{2(k+1)}.
\]
Determine the correct asymptotic behavior of \(1-p_k\). In particular,
is the gap necessarily of order \(1/k\), or can it decay much faster?
The exponential example for arbitrary polynomials does not automatically
respect the own-hat restriction.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Stability of finite optimality]
Suppose a finite strategy comes within \(\epsilon\) of the first-moment
bound. Which quantitative approximation to the failure-set and
query-graph structure follows? An effective stability theorem should
measure both the probability mass outside the ideal two-point score
law and the distance from the relevant equitable partition.
Exact equality is rigid enough for the arguments in this article;
the useful scale of the error terms is unresolved here.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[The inspection penalty for nonlinear perfect codes]
For odd team sizes at strict majority, the affine-rigidity theorem excludes every
nonaffine perfect code from the minimum-depth class. For a prescribed
nonaffine perfect code, determine the least depth of a probability-optimal
strategy having that failure set. Is one extra query ever sufficient,
and can the extra depth grow with the block length? The ordinary
perfect-code geometry alone does not determine the answer.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Beyond the classified minimum-depth pairs]
The exact parameter criterion settles existence at depth \(s-1\).
For parameters outside that family, determine whether the first-moment
bound can still be attained with greater depth, and how much greater.
When the probability bound is unattainable even with full visibility,
determine the optimum and its inspection cost. This retains the
unresolved part of the finite-team questions in
\cite{qry:RepoBoxes,qry:PriorHat}.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Charged and uncharged external randomness]
Determine the sharp cost frontier when protocols may use a public
random seed. Distinguish expectations conditional on the seed from
unconditional expectations, and charge random bits explicitly in a
third variant. The simple mixture in the model section already
separates the positive-probability criterion from almost-sure success
under an unconditional cost convention.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Visibility distance and computational time]
The cost theorem counts inspected hats, with no bound on their indices.
For a target growth law \(h\), simultaneously minimize the number of
queries, the farthest inspected index, and the time needed to compute
the next queried coordinate. These are different resources.
The explicit block schedule has logarithmic block lengths relative to
its physical position, but faster excess profiles may require a
different compromise.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[More colors and biased hats]
Extend the theorem to \(q\) equiprobable colors, with baseline \(n/q\),
or to a nonuniform product measure with an explicitly chosen fairness
criterion. A depth-\(d\) decision path then has probabilities governed
by the individual branch masses. Determine whether a single extremal
stopping-time distribution still governs all increasing costs.
The binary paired neutral block has a special exact-balance property
that cannot simply be assumed for another alphabet.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Typical fluctuations and rare losses]
For cost-optimal schedules, classify the possible central-limit and
large-deviation behavior of the positive gain count and the total
random loss. The normal limit derived here coexists with an exactly
zero mean for the unnormalized surplus. Obtain quantitative estimates
that separate typical fluctuations from the negative tail carrying
the expectation.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Average cost across players]
The displayed construction has realized population average cost
converging to \(3/2\), while its maximum individual expected cost has
supremum \(2\). Determine optimal population-average costs under
specified excess growth and visibility constraints. Such averages are
not governed by the same optimization as \(\sup_i\E Q_i\); inserting
balanced pairs can alter the former without changing the latter.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Quantitative spectral escape]
High confidence of positive excess forces its normalized Fourier
mass out of every fixed degree. Determine how fast that transfer must
occur for prescribed confidence and surplus, taking account of the
own-coordinate restrictions on the individual score functions.
The variance bound suggests that query influences, Fourier degree,
and the tail of the query depth should be studied jointly.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[A kernel-checked cost classification]
Implement the finite-tree semantics, the degree bound, a suitable
hypercontractivity estimate, the finite-cylinder obstruction, and the
geometric stopping-time comparison in a proof assistant. The finite
trace construction offers a separate test of a certificate interface.
The objective is to certify the quantified infinite statements,
including the positive-probability conclusion and the exact supremum
over individual costs, rather than only their finite examples.
\end{question}

\begin{writenote}[Status of the questions, 4 October 2026, batch~92]
All twelve questions stay open; source~06 settles one value asked for in 68.2 (below). The write searched no literature for them. \emph{Overlaps with source~06's questions of Section~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:questions}:} 68.1 is 57.3; 68.2 asks about the same quantity as 57.1, since $1-p_k$ is source~06's $\alpha_k$; 68.6 and 57.2 overlap (randomized thresholds, public and private coins); 68.8 is 57.9; 68.9 is close to 57.11; 68.10 is 57.10; 68.11 is close to 57.8; 68.7 and 57.6 both separate query count from query distance. \emph{Partial answers in this report:} for 68.2, source~06's Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:onequery} gives $p_1=3/4$, so the trace bound $p_{2^{r-1}-1}\ge1-2^{-r}$ is an equality at $k=1$ ($r=2$), and its sparse-support bound~\eqref{mbg:qry:eq:adaptive} gives the lower bound $1-p_k\ge c^{\rm ad}_k$; for 68.6, source~06's Theorem~\ref{mbg:qry:thm:randomcost} shows that with free \emph{private} coins every uniform expected cap $C>1$ admits almost-sure divergence, while the sharp randomized thresholds stay open. 68.5 retains the open part of Part~\ref{mbg:part:one}'s Q9 and of Part~\ref{mbg:hat:part}'s Research questions~29.4--29.5 (see the notes after the questions of Sections~\ref{mbg:sec:questions} and~\ref{mbg:hat:sec:questions}).
\end{writenote}

\section{What has been resolved}
\label{mbg:insp:sec:resolved}

\begin{writenote}[Added 4 October 2026, batch~92]
The ``definitive negative answer'' below is shared with source~06, which reached it independently against the same revision (Corollary~\ref{mbg:qry:cor:bounded}, with category, and Section~\ref{mbg:qry:sec:budgets} for the other clauses of the question); what this section lists as this source's own beyond that answer is the frontier for every nondecreasing cost, the fluctuation, variance and density results and the finite classification.
\end{writenote}

The uniform-query question in the preceding \repo\ manuscript has
a definitive negative answer in the stated model. The proof also
shows why a mere expectation calculation is insufficient: a centered
score may be positive on almost every input, with rare losses carrying
its expectation. Bounded decision depth prevents that concentration
uniformly, and conditioning on finite hat assignments upgrades this
obstruction to probability zero for divergent excess.

The positive direction has a different role. Eldredge's construction
already supplies divergent excess, and evaluating its exception by
early stopping yields truncated geometric query costs. Combining that
implementation with the lower bound determines all increasing
worst-player costs simultaneously. This is the basis of the exact
mean threshold \(2\), the tail envelope \(2^{-r}\), and the
exponential-moment boundary.

For finite teams, first-moment optimality imposes a concrete amount
of inspection at every all-wrong assignment. At the sharp depth,
the failure set must be affine with replicated simplex parity-check
columns. This gives a full parameter criterion, and the replicated
trace strategy realizes every allowed case. In particular, strict
majority for an odd team size at minimum depth forces an affine
Hamming failure code.

These conclusions are conventional mathematical proofs with
independent finite diagnostics. They do not replace the remaining
formalization work or establish bibliographic priority.

\clearpage
